# Reading pack: every kept source, condensed


# STREAM: Theravada, kasina and East Asian Buddhism (theravada-east-asian)

## [bud-ajaan-lee-keeping-the-breath-in-mind] Keeping the Breath in Mind & Lessons in Samadhi
Ajaan Lee Dhammadharo; Thai originals 1950s; English 1979, 3rd ed. 1990; teacher_manual; rights in_copyright; access fulltext_saved; screen pass; keep; relevance 4, practicality 4, confidence medium (Read in digest only; the seven basic steps and the Method 2 text were not seen, so whether they add induction advice is unconfirmed.)
- mode: ambiguous. Signs are said to be 'seen' (a pale, murky vapor, your own skull, a bright white nimitta) and mastery gives 'the ability to see without opening your eyes', but the glossary calls nimitta a 'mental sign or image' and Lee also says they can be made small, large, near, far 'through the power of thought', which leans imaginal. The digest does not settle whether the white light is a visual-field percept.
- eyes: closed; stimulus independence: not_applicable
- ONSET both: Method 1 is an induction of sorts: move the mind through bases in the head (forehead, top of head, middle of brain), spread the refined breath, and 'you may find' signs arise. For someone who sees nothing the foreword says signs are individual, 'not really essential', and should not be forced; if none come, 'learn how to use what you do experience'. No time scale in the digest.
- techniques: breath_control, concentration_nonvisual, attention_to_spontaneous_phenomena, posture_or_body_lock; phenomena: smoke_mist_cloud, white, sphere_pearl_bindu, all_pervading_light, figures_or_forms
- conditions: prerequisites: Mind and breath put in order and made stable before receiving 'guests'; mastery of the seven basic steps.; other: Study with a skilled teacher recommended for the knowledge-use stage.
- stages: signs (nimitta): Arise once breath spreads through the head: tingling, pale murky vapor, own skull. -> bright white nimitta: Chosen, enlarged to head size, lowered to centre of chest, spread to every pore; body parts may appear as pictures. -> mastery: Brilliantly white ball or lump of light can be produced at will and used as a field for knowledge or mental pictures.
- with the light: Take only one sign at a time, enlarge it to head size, bring it to the chest, spread it with the breath through the body; later evoke at will. Unwanted signs: long deep breaths into the heart dismiss them. Do not be pleased or upset by whatever appears.
- obstacles/fixes: Unwanted nimitta: breathe deep and long down into the heart. | Unwanted body pictures: take two or three long breaths. | Signs you cannot control: have nothing to do with them.
- cautions: Uncontrolled signs 'might lead you astray'. | Getting hooked on visions breeds pride and strays from the Middle Path. | Treat visions as arising, fading and disappearing; stay neutral.
- interpretation: The white nimitta is a pure breath that can cleanse the blood and ease pain; signs are 'guests' of the breath, and the glossary calls them mental sign or image.
- practice: Settle on the breath and shift awareness through bases in the head, letting each spot fill with refined breath and spread downward. Keep awareness broad. When signs appear (tingling, a pale murky vapor, your skull) do not be moved by them; if you do not want them, breathe long and deep into the heart. If one appears that you want, take it one at a time, expand it to the size of your head, bring it down to the centre of the chest, make the breath and light as white and bright as possible, and let both spread to every pore. Keep the mind on the refined breath alone. With practice you can raise the white ball of light at will and use it as a field for knowledge. If no signs come, they are not essential; do not force.
  - passage (onset; Translator's foreword, source lines 116-190): Signs and visions are individual and not essential; if they do not come, use what does.
  - passage (method; Method 1, source lines 814-918): After moving through the head bases, breath gives rise to signs such as a pale murky vapor or the skull.
  - passage (development; Method 1, source lines 814-918): Enlarge the white light to head size, then lower it to the chest and spread it through the body.
  - passage (mode; Lessons in Samadhi, 'guests', source lines 1160-1248): Mastered signs give heightened senses, including seeing with eyes closed.
- new leads: Thanissaro Bhikkhu on nimitta and jhana light

## [bud-bodhicitta-sastra-t1665] Jingangding yuqie zhong fa anouduoluo sanmiao sanputi xin lun (Bodaishinron; Treatise on Bodhicitta), T1665
attributed to Nagarjuna; translated/compiled by Amoghavajra (705-774); 8th c.; Taisho edition vol. 32 (1924-1934); classical_text; rights public_domain; access fulltext_saved; screen pass; keep_marginal; relevance 3, practicality 2, confidence high (Text read in full for the relevant sections; mode judgment limited because 見 may be noetic.)
- mode: imaginal_crossing. The disc is first 'contemplated' (觀白月輪) within the heart; the text then says that by this contemplation one 'illuminates and sees' (照見) the original mind, and 'I see my own mind shaped like a moon-disc' (我見自心形如月輪). The A-syllable gloss grades 'barely seeing' (纔見) versus 'constantly seeing' (常見). Whether this 見 is visual or noetic is not specified, so the crossing is claimed only by vocabulary.
- eyes: unspecified; stimulus independence: not_applicable
- ONSET development_only: Starts from deliberate visualisation; no advice for one who cannot see it; prerequisite is the three secrets (body mudra, speech mantra, mind samadhi) of esoteric practice, i.e. initiation. Thresholds: a first glimpse counts as seeing ultimate truth; constant seeing marks the first bhumi.
- techniques: visualization_crossing, letter_or_symbol_contemplation, devotion_or_transmission; phenomena: moon_disc, white, all_pervading_light, letter
- conditions: prerequisites: Three secrets (三密) of esoteric yoga; vajra-bound mudra (金剛縛) in the A verse; other: Eight-petalled white lotus one cubit across; white-light A syllable
- stages: 纔見 (barely seen): First glimpse of the round bright pure consciousness: 'seeing ultimate truth' -> 常見 (constantly seen): Stable seeing: entry into the first bodhisattva ground -> 廓周法界: Gradual expansion to fill the dharmadhatu and space, contracting and extending at will
- with the light: Stabilise, then expand to fill space and contract at will (卷舒自在).
- safety flags: transmission_required_by_tradition
- interpretation: The moon-disc is the form of one's own mind as bodhicitta, pure and luminous like the full moon.
- practice: Within the heart contemplate a white moon-disc; by this, see the original mind as clear and pure, like the light of the full moon filling space without distinctions. Recognise: 'I see my own mind shaped like a moon-disc.' In the A-syllable form, with the vajra-bound mudra, contemplate an eight-petalled white lotus a cubit across with a shining white A upon it, and settle on the round bright pure consciousness. A first glimpse is counted as seeing ultimate truth; constant seeing marks the first ground; then enlarge it gradually until it fills the dharmadhatu, extending and contracting it freely.
  - passage (mode; T32.573c13-c16): The practitioner contemplates a white moon-disc in the heart and thereby sees the original mind as pure, like full-moon light filling space.
  - passage (mode; T32.573c19-c20): 'I see my own mind shaped like a moon-disc.'
  - passage (method; T32.574b06): Eight-petalled white lotus a cubit across, the A syllable shining white upon it.
  - passage (stages; T32.574b08-b11): Glimpsing = seeing ultimate truth; constant seeing = first ground; then expand to fill the dharmadhatu.
- new leads: Kakuban, Ajikan texts (Kogyo Daishi zenshu); Kukai's Himitsu mandara jujushinron

## [bud-contemplation-sutra-takakusu] The Amitayur-dhyana-sutra (Guan Wuliangshou jing; Sutra of the Meditation on Buddha Amitayus), in Buddhist Mahayana Texts, Sacred Books of the East vol. XLIX
Anonymous (Chinese translation attributed to Kalayasas, c. 424-442 CE); 5th century CE; English 1894; classical_text; rights public_domain; access fulltext_saved; screen pass; keep; relevance 4, practicality 3, confidence medium (Digest only; section 11 on 'dimly seen' versus samadhi vision was not in the digest and is taken from the search agent's hint, unconfirmed.)
- mode: imaginal_crossing. Begins with physical gazing at the setting sun, then the image must 'remain clear and fixed, whether thine eyes be shut or open'; later steps (water, ice, lapis ground) are imagined ('thou shouldst imagine'), yet the text says the ground 'will be seen'. Later meditations are cued to 'the mind's eye', so the sutra spans afterimage-like retention and constructed imagery without marking a perceptual threshold.
- eyes: both; stimulus independence: yes
- ONSET induction: The first step is a method for someone who sees nothing: gaze at the sun about to set, fix the mind, keep the image after looking away. Stages then build step by step; the digest shows no time scale and no statement that light cannot be forced. Section 11 (dimly seen versus samadhi) was not in the digest.
- techniques: light_source_gazing, afterimage_retention, visualization_crossing, gazing_fixed_point, concentration_visual_object, devotion_or_transmission; phenomena: sun_disc, rainbow, colour_fields, body_or_objects_luminous, all_pervading_light, figures_or_forms
- conditions: posture: Sit facing west.; gaze_or_eye_position: Gaze at the sun when about to set and like a suspended drum.; prerequisites: Steady, unscattered thought; each stage completed before the next.
- stages: First Meditation: sun: Setting sun held until it stays clear whether eyes are shut or open. -> Second Meditation: water: Clear water, then ice, then lapis lazuli ground, transparent within and without, jewels with rays of many colours. -> Ninth Meditation: Amitayus: Begin with the white hair-twist between the brows; the signs and marks 'naturally appear before their eyes'.
- with the light: Stabilise the image so it persists eyes shut or open, never allow thought to scatter, then build further scenes from it; clear and distinct, like seeing the palm of one's hand.
- obstacles/fixes: Scattered thought: do not let it be lost; hold the image fixed.
- cautions: Meditation of another kind than the prescribed is called heretical meditation.
- safety flags: sun_gazing
- interpretation: The visions are a rehearsal of the Pure Land and of Amitayus as a samadhi of remembering the Buddhas, leading to rebirth there.
- practice: Sit facing west and watch the sun as it is about to set, looking like a suspended drum, with the mind fixed and unwavering. Then make that image stay clear and fixed whether your eyes are shut or open. Next take clear water, then ice, then imagine its lapis lazuli ground, transparent and shining within and without, with jewels sending out rays of many colours; keep each image from scattering. Build outward from there in order, tree by tree and lake by lake, then to the Buddha, starting with a single mark, the white hair-twist between the eyebrows, held as clearly as possible, whereupon the remaining marks are said to appear of themselves.
  - passage (onset; Part II section 9, source lines 21584-21649): The practice starts from actual gazing at the low setting sun.
  - passage (mode; Part II section 9, source lines 21584-21649): The image must persist after the eyes leave the sun, with eyes closed or open.
  - passage (stages; Part II section 10, source lines 21584-21649): Water is perceived next, then ice, then lapis lazuli ground with jewels and rays of many colours.
  - passage (development; Section 18, source lines 22095-22187): Hold one sign, the white hair-twist, as clearly as possible; the rest then appear on their own.

## [bud-dhammakaya-luang-phor-sodh-manual] Samatha-Vipassana Meditation in Accordance with the Four Foundations of Mindfulness to Reach Lord Buddha's Dhammakayas and Nibbana
Phra Mongkol-Thepmuni (Luang Phor Sodh Candasaro); presented and explained by Phra Thepyanmongkol (Sermchai Jayamanggalo); 2012 (Luang Phor Sodh's talks date from before 1959); teacher_manual; rights in_copyright; access partial_saved; screen pass; keep; relevance 4, practicality 4, confidence medium (Digest of a partial excerpt; the text only partly shows what happens to those who never see the sphere.)
- mode: imaginal_crossing. Starts as deliberate visualisation ('Visualize a bright sphere', 'imagines a transparent sphere') and the text insists the goal is 'really seeing the actual inner icon (nimitta) as a bright sphere'. The sequence is the classical preliminary, learning, then counterpart sign, with a sphere that 'arises' once the mind stops. The book frames it as light kasina.
- eyes: closed; stimulus independence: not_applicable
- ONSET both: Section 4.21 is written for one who has not yet seen the sphere: keep imagining a bright sphere at the centre of the body, recite 'Samma Arahang', and when the mind is still and calm a bright sphere arises. If it does not, recite 100 or 1,000 times; focus on the centre of the centre ten, a hundred or a thousand times. Prerequisite: a mind not murky with defilements, and merit from past lives. No calendar time scale; no statement that it cannot be forced, but the text says it is hard to see the sphere if the mind is murky.
- techniques: visualization_crossing, sound_or_mantra, concentration_nonvisual, eye_position, posture_or_body_lock, breath_control; phenomena: sphere_pearl_bindu, white, all_pervading_light, lamp_or_flame
- conditions: posture: Upright, legs crossed, right index finger touching left thumb.; gaze_or_eye_position: Eyes closed; at Base 4 roll the eyeballs gently upward under the closed lids.; duration_and_schedule: Recitation continued in all postures; 100 or 1,000 repetitions if the mind has not stopped.; prerequisites: Virtue; mind free of hindrances; merit from past lives.; other: Women begin at the left nostril, men at the right.
- stages: preliminary sign (parikamma-nimitta): Imagined transparent sphere like a polished diamond, eyeball-sized, moved through seven bases. -> learning sign (uggaha-nimitta): A transparent sphere seen briefly; marks access concentration. -> counterpart sign (patibhaga-nimitta): Bright sphere stable for long periods; first jhana. -> Dhamma Sphere: Bright sphere arises at the centre of the body (Position Seven), egg-yolk to sun size; recitation stops and attention rests on the pinpoint at its centre until radiant.
- with the light: Stop still at the centre of the centre of the sphere; once the Dhamma Sphere arises, stop reciting, do not be overjoyed, keep concentrating on its centre, then proceed to further nested spheres.
- obstacles/fixes: Drowsiness or other hindrance: keep the sign and recitation at the body centre until a light appears from inside, awakening the mind. | Mind not yet stopped: recite 100 or 1,000 times. | Giddiness at the counterpart sign: be mindful.
- cautions: Murky mind makes the sphere hard to see. | Do not be overjoyed or excited when the sphere arises. | Never move the mind to follow the breath in and out.
- interpretation: The bright sphere is the Dhamma Sphere or First Path sphere, the nucleus of the form aggregate and the first step toward paths, fruits and Nibbana; the method is framed as aloka-kasina with breath and recollection of the Buddha.
- practice: Sit upright and close your eyes. Imagine a transparent sphere like a polished diamond about the size of an eyeball at the nostril, and rest on the tiny clear spot at its centre while silently reciting 'Samma Arahang'. Never separate sphere and recitation. Move the sphere through seven bases: nostril, tear duct, centre of the head, palate (roll the eyes gently upward under closed lids here), throat opening, body centre in line with the navel, and a point two finger-breadths above the navel. Pause at each, reciting three times. At the last, stay put, keep imagining the sphere and recite until the mind stops. A bright sphere then arises; stop reciting and keep resting on its centre until it is brilliant.
  - passage (mode; Ch. 1.3 and 2.12, source lines 1880-1923): The aim is genuinely seeing the inner sign as a bright sphere, not merely thinking of it.
  - passage (method; Ch. 2.12 Base 4, source lines 3188-3402): At the fourth base the eyes are rolled up under closed lids, compared with a baby falling asleep.
  - passage (onset; 4.21 Method for one who has not yet seen the Dhamma Sphere, source lines 5040-5163): For the person who sees nothing: keep imagining the sphere and reciting at the body centre; when the mind stills, the sphere arises.
  - passage (development; Ch. 4.3 hindrances, source lines 5040-5163): Against hindrances such as drowsiness, continue sign and recitation at the body centre; a light appears from within.
  - passage (caution; Ch. 2.21, source lines 3634-3708): On arising, stay unexcited and keep stopping at the centre.
- new leads: Dhammakaya Foundation or Wat Paknam sources on the Dhamma Sphere

## [bud-fire-kasina-book-stein-ingram] The Fire Kasina: Questions and Answers on Retreat with Practice Notes and Commentary
Shannon Stein and Daniel M. Ingram; 2017; practitioner_testimony; rights in_copyright; access fulltext_saved; screen pass; keep; relevance 5, practicality 5, confidence high
- mode: perceptual. The object is a retinal after-image (red dot) seen with closed eyes, then the murk and colour fields in the visual field; Stein reports stunning colours, crab-like creatures seen even with eyes open, and light shot from a fingertip into the night sky, while Ingram notes another meditator saw the colours of a pentagram drawn in daylight. The nimitta glossary entry calls it a 'mind-created disk', but the testimony is of seen colour.
- eyes: both; stimulus independence: yes
- ONSET both: Induction is the opening instruction: stare at the flame a minute or two, close eyes, follow the after-image, repeat. Sequence then red dot, murk, colours; patience is required and 'something will organise out of that'. Beginners whose dots shift or fade are told to keep patiently returning attention to whatever is in the field. Ingram also reports doing it 'cold' with only eyelid colours and a white kasina. No cannot-be-forced statement; time scale: Stein reached second-jhana red dot within hours of starting, brilliant colour by day nine of 25.
- techniques: light_source_gazing, afterimage_retention, attention_to_spontaneous_phenomena, darkness_exposure, concentration_visual_object, sound_or_mantra, movement, flicker_or_device; phenomena: lamp_or_flame, red, black_light, smoke_mist_cloud, colour_fields, blue, green, geometric_patterns, figures_or_forms, white, body_or_objects_luminous
- conditions: posture: Sit comfortably.; gaze_or_eye_position: Stare at flame three to five feet away; then eyes closed on the after-image; later eyes open in dim rooms.; setting: Still air or a glass frame round the flame; dawn, dusk and night best for eyes-open images; keep rooms dark early on.; duration_and_schedule: Look a minute or two, close eyes, follow the image until static, repeat endlessly; retreat context of 25 days with hours of sitting.; prerequisites: Strong concentration helps; Ingram had done vipassana for days first.; other: A low-power LED can refresh the after-image between sits.
- stages: red dot: Closed-eye after-image of the flame, the object to follow. -> dissolution / murk: Dot dissolves into a vague field; where attention drifts and progress is made. -> colour with depth: Airy colours gain 3D depth, then smooth pools of blue and green, then brilliant colour; later moving scenes and radial symmetry patterns ('rainbow flux lines').
- with the light: Give full attention to the after-image, favouring anything like a red dot; follow it until static, then refresh by looking again. Carry it off the cushion and into eyes-open walking, keep extending it into daylight; notice the luminosity of the murk.
- obstacles/fixes: Dissolution phase with spacing out: keep attending; use light exercise and movement. | Image too faint in light rooms: stay in dimmer rooms or refresh with an LED. | Shifting faint dots early: patiently return attention to whatever is in the field. | Fear stage with creatures: back off and rest.
- cautions: Candle safety: clear combustibles and extinguish after each sit. | Fear stage and strange visions can be alarming on solo retreat; have a guide. | Physical tightness from striving.
- safety flags: prolonged_darkness
- interpretation: The after-image and nimitta are the object of kasina concentration, mapped onto the jhanas, with the visuals used as a diagram of what attention does across states.
- practice: Sit three to five feet from a steady candle flame. Stare at it for a minute or two, until it looks strange or purplish. Close your eyes and give full attention to the after-image, especially any red dot. Follow it for as long as it lasts, until only static remains; then open your eyes, look at the flame again, close them, and repeat many times. When the dot dissolves, keep attending to the murk and to its luminosity, giving it a minute or two, and organisation will emerge: colours, depth, then moving imagery. Be patient. Carry the image off the cushion with eyes half open in dim rooms, refreshing with an LED if necessary, and stretch eyes-open images toward daylight.
  - passage (onset; Basic instruction, source lines 1070-1111): Stare at the flame until the visual field turns strange or purplish, then close your eyes.
  - passage (method; Basic instruction, source lines 1070-1111): Take the closed-eye after-image as the object, favouring a red dot.
  - passage (development; Basic instruction, source lines 1070-1111): Cycle between looking and following until nothing organised remains, then give the remainder another minute or two.
  - passage (mode; Day Eight eyes-open practice, source lines 2947-3045): Maintain the stable image while moving with eyes open, at night and dawn first, then into daylight.
  - passage (stages; Day Ten sequence, source lines 3432-3479): Stein's reported sequence: red dot, shapes at its edges, murk, then airy colours with depth.
- new leads: firekasina.org audio diaries by Ingram and Stein

## [bud-mahasi-progress-of-insight] The Progress of Insight (Visuddhinana-katha): A Modern Pali Treatise on Buddhist Satipatthana Meditation
Mahasi Sayadaw (U Sobhana Mahathera); Burmese original 1940s; Pali 1950; English 1965; teacher_manual; rights in_copyright; access fulltext_saved; screen pass; keep_marginal; relevance 2, practicality 2, confidence medium (Read in digest; OCR in the key passage is rough in places.)
- mode: ambiguous. The translator renders it '(the mental vision of) a brilliant light will appear', likened to a lamp, lightning, moon or sun; the parenthesis suggests an inner vision, but nothing in the digest says whether it is in the visual field.
- eyes: unspecified; stimulus independence: not_applicable
- ONSET development_only: No induction. The light is a by-product that appears when noticing stays exclusively in the present process; for someone who sees nothing the text offers only continued noticing, and it presents the light as a corruption to pass through, not something to cultivate. Duration: a moment for some, longer for others.
- techniques: attention_to_spontaneous_phenomena; phenomena: lamp_or_flame, lightning_flashes, moon_disc, sun_disc, all_pervading_light
- conditions: prerequisites: Momentary concentration strong enough that noticing is exclusively in the present; the abdominal rising and falling as basic object.; other: Written for meditators already at the insight-knowledge stage, not beginners.
- stages: Knowledge of Arising and Passing Away (early, corrupted): Brilliant light with strong mindfulness, rapture, tranquillity, happiness, equanimity, subtle attachment. -> Path and Not-Path: Decision that the light is not the path; return to noticing.
- with the light: Refuse it as a goal: decide it is not the Path, then keep noticing, either noticing the light too or leaving it unheeded.
- obstacles/fixes: Mistaking the light for Path and Fruition: decide, alone or on a teacher's instruction, that it is not the Path and return to noticing.
- cautions: Delight in the light is a corruption of insight. | Believing 'I must have attained the Path' is mistaking what is not the Path for the Path. | The treatise was withheld from beginners so they would not be diverted by secondary phenomena.
- safety flags: tradition_warns_against_seeking
- interpretation: A corruption (upakkilesa) of insight arising at the early stage of Knowledge of Arising and Passing Away, not a sign of attainment.
- practice: This source gives no method for producing light. It says that when noticing of bodily and mental processes is kept exclusively to the present, without looking back or ahead, a brilliant light may appear, like a lamp, lightning, the moon or the sun, with rapture and effortless mindfulness. Do not take it for attainment and do not relish it. Decide, by yourself or on a teacher's word, that the light and its companions are not the Path, and go on with continuous noticing, either noticing the light as another object or leaving it unheeded. Passing through this stage, the light is outgrown as arising and passing away become distinct.
  - passage (onset; Ch. IV.4 Ten Corruptions, source lines 816-863): The light appears as a by-product when noticing stays in the present; it looks like a lamp, lightning, moon or sun.
  - passage (caution; Ch. IV.4, source lines 978-1075): The meditator is liable to take the light and rapture as attainment of Path and Fruition.
  - passage (development; Ch. V, source lines 978-1075): The remedy is the decision that the light is not the Path, followed by continued noticing.
  - passage (interpretation; Translator's foreword, source lines 67-130): The treatise was kept from beginners so that secondary phenomena would not divert them.

## [bud-mn128-upakkilesa-sutta] Upakkilesa Sutta (Majjhima Nikaya 128): Corruptions
Pali Canon (Majjhima Nikaya); canonical; translation c. 2018; classical_text; rights public_domain; access fulltext_saved; screen pass; keep; relevance 5, practicality 3, confidence high
- mode: perceptual. The text says 'we perceive both light and vision of forms' and that the light is perceived or not depending on which basis is attended to, for a whole night and day; the vocabulary of perceiving and seeing, with no mention of imagining or visualising.
- eyes: unspecified; stimulus independence: not_applicable
- ONSET development_only: Nothing for someone who sees nothing: light and vision of forms are already occurring in diligent, keen, resolute meditation and the sutra is troubleshooting why they vanish. The Buddha says the same happened to him before awakening. Time scale for stable light: a whole night, a whole day, even a whole night and day.
- techniques: attention_to_spontaneous_phenomena, concentration_nonvisual; phenomena: all_pervading_light, figures_or_forms
- conditions: duration_and_schedule: Light and forms sustained for a whole night and day once stable.; prerequisites: Diligent, keen and resolute practice; immersion (samadhi).
- stages: light and vision of forms that vanish: Appear while meditating but vanish before long when immersion falls away. -> light without forms or forms without light: Depends on whether attention is on the basis of the light or of the forms. -> limited then limitless light and forms: Scales with the limits of immersion.
- with the light: Do not hold the light itself; diagnose and give up the eleven corruptions so immersion stays, then use the light and forms as indicators of immersion and develop immersion in three ways.
- obstacles/fixes: Doubt | Loss of focus | Dullness and drowsiness | Terror | Elation | Discomfort | Excessive energy (like gripping a quail too tightly) | Overly lax energy (like gripping a quail too loosely) | Longing | Perceptions of diversity | Excessive concentration on forms
- cautions: Each of the eleven states is a corruption of the mind that makes immersion fall away and the light vanish.
- interpretation: Light and vision of forms are a result of immersion and a gauge of its extent: limited immersion gives limited light, limitless immersion limitless light.
- practice: When light or visions of forms appear and then vanish, the Buddha says look for what knocked immersion out: doubt, loss of focus, dullness, terror, elation, discomfort, too much or too little energy, longing, perception of diversity, or too much concentration on forms. Notice each as it arises and give it up, one after another. Energy is like holding a quail: too tight kills it, too loose lets it fly. Once the corruptions are cleared, light may be seen without forms or forms without light, depending on whether attention rests on the basis of the light or of the forms. The breadth of the light follows the breadth of your immersion, and it can last a night and a day.
  - passage (testimony; Segment 15, source lines 139-143): Anuruddha reports perceiving both light and vision of forms in diligent meditation, which then vanish.
  - passage (caution; Segment 16, source lines 144-152): The refrain behind all eleven diagnoses: loss of immersion makes the light vanish.
  - passage (method; Segment 28, source lines 218-226): Attending to the basis of the light gives light without forms, and attending to the basis of forms gives forms without light.
  - passage (stages; Segment 29, source lines 227-237): Limited immersion gives limited light and forms; limitless immersion gives limitless light and forms.
- new leads: Commentary to MN 128 on obhasa and nimitta

## [bud-pa-auk-knowing-and-seeing] Knowing and Seeing: Talks and Questions & Answers at a Meditation Retreat in Taiwan
Pa-Auk Tawya Sayadaw; talks 1998; 1st ed. 1999; 4th rev. ed. 2010; teacher_manual; rights public_domain; access fulltext_saved; screen pass; keep; relevance 5, practicality 5, confidence medium (Digest only; the four stages of breath practice and the Q&A on the light of concentration were not seen.)
- mode: ambiguous. Breath nimitta and light of concentration are described as seen (light before the nimitta, cotton wool, morning star, 'wherever you look, you see only white'); but Pa-Auk also says the nimitta is 'produced by perception', can be made long, short or red at will, and tells the yogi to 'visualize' kasinas with eyes closed until the nimitta is seen 'as clearly as if you were looking at it with your eyes open', which is imaginal crossing. He does not say that the white is in the retinal visual field.
- eyes: both; stimulus independence: not_applicable
- ONSET both: Induction: hold the breath at its touch point and ignore any nimitta far away; the nimitta comes and stays there. For kasinas, look at the object with eyes open, then close and visualize; if unable, re-establish the fourth anapana jhana and look with the light of concentration, or use a skeleton white until only a white circle remains. Time scales: hold the white sign one, two, three hours or more; the skeleton fades after one or two hours of white. The digest shows no statement that light cannot be forced, other than do not play with the nimitta; a nimitta far in front is not a real one.
- techniques: concentration_nonvisual, breath_control, concentration_visual_object, visualization_crossing, gazing_fixed_point, light_source_gazing; phenomena: white, sphere_pearl_bindu, smoke_mist_cloud, all_pervading_light, moon_disc, sun_disc, geometric_patterns
- conditions: gaze_or_eye_position: Earth kasina: eyes open a while, then closed. Fire kasina: screen with circular hole before a wood or grass fire. Light kasina: rays through a wall crack or leaves, or a lamp in an earthen pot with light falling on a wall.; duration_and_schedule: One, two, three hours or more fixed on the white sign.; prerequisites: Fourth anapana jhana gives the light needed for skeleton and white kasina.
- stages: parikamma-nimitta: Natural breath and touch point; smoky grey may appear around the nostrils. -> uggaha-nimitta: Grey becomes white like cotton wool, usually dull and opaque. -> patibhaga-nimitta: Bright, clear, radiant like the morning star; access or absorption concentration. -> expanded white kasina: White circle expanded by a set number of inches in stages until it fills all ten directions.
- with the light: Do not play with the nimitta, change its shape, or shift to it early; keep the mind on the touch point until the nimitta is stable and merged with the breath, then rest on it for hours until it brightens; then, in kasina practice, expand it in determined stages and use the light of concentration to discern body parts.
- obstacles/fixes: Nimitta appears far in front: ignore it; concentrate at the touch point. | Colour and shape changing often: ignore them and attend to it as the breath nimitta. | Expanded white unstable: return to noting 'white - white'. | Unable to visualize the kasina: re-establish the fourth anapana jhana and use its light.
- cautions: Playing with the nimitta halts progress and it will probably disappear. | A nimitta far from the nostrils is not a real breath nimitta. | Forcing the mind off a stable nimitta may lose concentration.
- interpretation: The nimitta is mind-born, produced by perception and the level of concentration; the light of concentration is the light of wisdom that lets the yogi see ultimate mentality-materiality.
- practice: Keep attention on the touch point of the breath. For some yogis a light appears first, for others a nimitta directly: grey smoke, then white like cotton wool. If it appears far out in front, ignore it; stay at the touch point and it will come there. When it is stable, merged with the breath, drop the breath and attend to it alone. Do not change or play with it. Keep your mind on the white sign for one, two or three hours until it becomes bright as the morning star. Then, for the white kasina, use that light to discern a skeleton, fix on its whitest spot as 'white - white' until only a white circle remains, and expand it in set stages until white fills all directions.
  - passage (mode; Talk 1, 'The Nimitta', source lines 2898-2957): A light may precede the nimitta, and the two are distinct, as the sun differs from sunlight.
  - passage (onset; Talk 1, source lines 2974-3035): For someone with no stable nimitta, keep at the touch point and ignore distant lights.
  - passage (caution; Talk 1, source lines 2974-3035): Altering or toying with the nimitta ends progress.
  - passage (development; Talk 2, white kasina, source lines 5623-5825): Sustained fixing on white makes the skeleton fade leaving a white circle, the uggaha-nimitta.
  - passage (method; Talk 2, earth kasina, source lines 5623-5825): Kasina sign is first taken with eyes open, then visualized with eyes closed, with a fallback of using the light of concentration.

## [bud-suttas-perception-of-light] Discourses on the perception of light (aloka-sanna): AN 4.41, AN 7.61, SN 51.20, SN 51.11, AN 6.29, AN 8.64, AN 1.51-60
Pali Canon (Anguttara and Samyutta Nikayas); canonical; translation c. 2018; classical_text; rights public_domain; access fulltext_saved; screen pass; keep; relevance 4, practicality 2, confidence high (Entire file read. English translation only: 'light', 'radiance' and 'forms' are Sujato's renderings and the Pali is not in the saved text. Onset coded 'both' on a thin basis: the formula is an instruction to begin, but with no procedure.)
- mode: ambiguous. The stock formula speaks of applying the mind to a 'perception of light' and a 'perception of day' that is the same by night as by day, giving 'a mind that's full of radiance': a perception attended to, with no statement of where or how it appears. AN 8.64 is the one passage with plainly visual language: the Buddha 'perceived light but did not see forms', then perceived light and saw forms, the forms being deities he went on to converse with. That pairs light with seeing, but the text never says eyes open or closed, nor contrasts it with imagining. AN 1.51 ('this mind is radiant') is a statement about the mind, not a visual report.
- eyes: unspecified; stimulus independence: not_applicable
- ONSET both: Induction only in the barest sense. The instruction is given to people who are not said to see anything (in AN 7.61 to a man nodding off at night): apply the mind to the perception of light, fix on the perception of day, by night exactly as by day, with a heart 'open and unenveloped'. No procedure for getting that perception is given. SN 51.20's own gloss is circular: the radiant mind is developed when one has 'properly grasped' the perception of light and of day. In AN 7.61 the step immediately before it is to get up, rinse the eyes, look round in every direction and look up at the stars; the text presents these as separate successive remedies and does not say one feeds the other. Development: AN 8.64 starts with light already perceived and adds forms 'after some time, living diligent, keen, and resolute'; no time scale beyond that phrase. Nothing on grace or on light being unforceable. Prerequisite in SN 51 is samadhi built on enthusiasm, energy, mind-development or inquiry, each neither lax nor tense, neither constricted by drowsiness nor scattered among the senses.
- techniques: other, concentration_nonvisual; phenomena: all_pervading_light, figures_or_forms
- conditions: posture: Not specified for the light perception. AN 7.61 surrounds it with seated meditation, standing up, walking, and lying on the right side.; gaze_or_eye_position: None given. AN 7.61's preceding remedy: rinse the eyes with water, look in every direction, look up at the stars.; setting: Night as well as day; quiet, remote lodgings praised at the end of AN 7.61.; duration_and_schedule: 'As by day, so by night; as by night, so by day': the same features, attributes and signs at both times (SN 51.20). No durations.; prerequisites: SN 51.20/51.11: immersion with effort that is not too lax (laziness), not too tense (restlessness), not constricted internally (dullness and drowsiness), not scattered externally (sense stimulation).; other: In SN 51 the light perception is the last of four linked perceptions: before/behind, below/above (the body parts from soles to hair-tips), day/night, and the open, unenveloped, radiant mind.
- stages: perception of light, perception of day (AN 4.41, 7.61, 6.29): Mind applied to light and to daytime, alike by night and by day; yields 'a mind that's full of radiance'. Nothing seen is described. -> perceived light but did not see forms (AN 8.64, round 1): The Buddha's pre-awakening starting point: light without forms. -> perceived light and saw forms (AN 8.64, round 2): Reached 'after some time, living diligent, keen, and resolute', prompted by the deliberate thought 'what if I were to both perceive light and see forms?' -> rounds 3 to 8 (AN 8.64): Conversing with the deities seen; knowing their order, the deeds that led them there, their food and experience, their lifespan, and whether he had lived with them before. Each round opens with a deliberate resolve and is reached 'after some time'.
- with the light: Cultivate it deliberately and keep it identical by day and by night; use it as the samadhi-development that leads to 'knowledge and vision' (AN 4.41, AN 6.29), as a remedy for drowsiness (AN 7.61), and as a component of the bases of psychic power (SN 51). In AN 8.64, extend it step by step by resolve from light alone to light with forms and then to knowledge about what is seen.
- obstacles/fixes: Drowsiness while meditating (AN 7.61), remedies in order: drop the perception you were on when you got drowsy; reflect on the teaching; recite it in detail; pinch the ears and rub the limbs; get up, rinse the eyes, look around and up at the stars; apply the mind to the perception of light; walk with senses turned inward; finally lie down in the lion's posture set on the time of rising. | Effort too lax (with laziness) or too tense (with restlessness); mind constricted internally (dullness and drowsiness) or scattered externally (five kinds of sense stimulation) (SN 51.20). | Vanity when visiting families and getting into arguments lead to restlessness, loss of restraint, and a mind 'far from immersion' (AN 7.61).
- cautions: No warning against seeking the light occurs in these texts; it is prescribed. AN 4.41 ranks it as leading to knowledge and vision, and names a different development (observing rise and fall in the five aggregates) as the one that leads to the ending of defilements.
- interpretation: The light perception is a cultivated perception that makes the mind 'full of radiance' and is the route to 'knowledge and vision', which in AN 8.64 means seeing and knowing deities; AN 1.51-52 separately calls the mind itself radiant but corrupted by visiting corruptions. The suttas do not say what the light is.
- practice: From these discourses alone the practice is one sentence long. Turn the mind to the perception of light and hold the perception of daytime, at night just as in the day and in the day just as at night, with the same features and signs at both times, keeping the heart open and unenveloped; this develops a mind full of radiance. Keep effort neither slack nor strained, the mind neither sunk in drowsiness nor scattered among the senses. If you are nodding off at night, try it after the simpler remedies have failed: changing the object, reflecting on and reciting the teaching, rubbing ears and limbs, standing up, rinsing the eyes and looking at the stars. Once light is perceived, the Buddha's own account is that he resolved to see forms as well, and after some time of diligent, keen, resolute practice did so, then extended his knowledge of what he saw round by round. How the perception of light is first obtained is not explained.
  - passage (method; AN 4.41 seg. 3.1-3.5, source lines 17-21): The whole instruction: attend to the perception of light, fix on the perception of day, as by day so by night; with an open, unenveloped heart develop a radiant mind. This is the samadhi-development that leads to knowledge and vision.
  - passage (onset; AN 7.61 seg. 6.1-7.4, source lines 62-67): Fifth remedy for drowsiness: stand up, rinse the eyes, look around and up at the stars. If that fails, the sixth is the perception-of-light formula. The text lists them as successive alternatives; it does not say the starlight is the source of the light perception.
  - passage (onset; SN 51.20 seg. 10.1-10.3, source lines 149-151): The sutta's own definition of developing a radiant mind: having properly grasped the perception of light and of day. It names the grasping as the condition and does not say how the grasping is done.
  - passage (development; SN 51.20 seg. 9.1-9.4, source lines 145-148): 'As by day, so by night' is glossed as developing the samadhi with identical features, attributes and signs at both times.
  - passage (mode; AN 8.64 seg. 1.3-3.1, source lines 275-279): First-person: before awakening the Buddha perceived light without seeing forms; he resolved to have both, and after some time of diligent, keen, resolute practice perceived light and saw forms. The pairing with seeing forms is the strongest sign in these texts that the light is something seen.
  - passage (interpretation; AN 6.29 seg. 5.1-5.3, source lines 242-244): Ananda lists the light perception as the second of five topics for recollection, practised regardless of whether it is night or day, leading to knowledge and vision; the Buddha approves the list.
- new leads: Commentarial glosses on aloka-sanna in AN 4.41 and AN 7.61 (Manorathapurani), for how the perception is said to be 'grasped' (light of sun, moon, lamp or gem)

## [bud-vimuttimagga-ehara] The Path of Freedom (Vimuttimagga)
Arahant Upatissa; c. 1st-3rd century CE; Chinese translation 6th century; English 1961; classical_text; rights in_copyright; access partial_saved; screen pass; keep; relevance 4, practicality 3, confidence medium (Digest only. The search agent's hint about eyes neither wide open nor shut and the breath section ('does not depend on colour or form') were not visible in the digest and are not recorded here.)
- mode: imaginal_crossing. The grasping sign is seen 'as it were in space'; the after-image is said to be seen with closed eyes 'only in thought' and moved at will far, near, left or right, which marks a mind-made image that is perceived rather than a retinal one.
- eyes: both; stimulus independence: yes
- ONSET induction: The procedure is for a beginner: prepare a mandala (dawn-coloured clay circle, or blue, yellow, red or white colour, or a bowl of water throwing light on a wall); gaze with undisturbed mind; grasping sign then arises; following it again and again the after-image arises. A new yogin must use a prepared object; the practised can grasp it in nature. Digest shows no time scale or cannot-be-forced statement; a teacher is stressed (protect the sign with reverence for the teacher).
- techniques: gazing_fixed_point, concentration_visual_object, afterimage_retention, light_source_gazing, visualization_crossing, devotion_or_transmission; phenomena: sphere_pearl_bindu, white, blue, gold_yellow, red, all_pervading_light, moon_disc, sun_disc
- conditions: gaze_or_eye_position: Even gazing, skilfulness and the elimination of disturbance; mandala at proper distance.; setting: Calm place, screened from sun, one fathom from the mandala; for the light kasina a wall facing east or west with a bowl of water in the sun.; duration_and_schedule: Repeatedly; increase size from a span and four fingers.; prerequisites: Consider the tribulations of sense-desires and the benefits of renunciation first.; other: Prepared object for beginners only.
- stages: grasping sign: Mandala perceived as if in space, sometimes far, near, left, right, big, small, ugly, lovely. -> after-image: Arises through following the grasping sign again and again; seen with closed eyes and placed at will in any direction. -> increase of the kasina: In access or first jhana expand the sign step by step to a wheel, field, village, city, the whole earth.
- with the light: Protect the sign with reverence for the teacher; refrain from evil, do good, keep up endeavour; do not scan the mandala; once the after-image is stable, expand it gradually.
- obstacles/fixes: Mind moving in one direction: the sign can be made to arise. | A new yogin cannot take the sign in an unprepared place and should use a prepared mandala.
- cautions: If the sign is not protected it will surely be lost.
- interpretation: The sign and after-image are mind-born objects of concentration leading to jhana; the light kasina yields seeing light everywhere.
- practice: Make a circle of dawn-coloured clay in a calm, screened place, or a colour mandala for the colour kasinas. For the light kasina, set a bowl of water in the sun so its light is reflected onto a wall facing east or west. Sit at the right distance and look steadily with undisturbed mind: even gazing, skilfulness, no disturbance. Do not scan it. The grasping sign arises, appearing as if in space, now far, now near. Follow it again and again until the after-image comes, which you can see with eyes closed and send far, near, left, right, up or down at will. Protect it by virtue and constant effort, then enlarge it gradually.
  - passage (mode; Ch. VIII section 1, grasping sign, source lines 381-440): The grasping sign is seen as if in space, shifting in distance, side and size.
  - passage (method; Ch. VIII section 1, after-image, source lines 381-440): The after-image is seen with the eyes closed as it was when looking, and exists only in thought.
  - passage (development; Ch. VIII section 1, after-image, source lines 381-440): Once the after-image is stable it can be placed at will in any direction.
  - passage (onset; Ch. VIII, red kasina, source lines 3282-3375): The standard instruction for taking any kasina sign: even gazing, skilfulness, and removing disturbance.
  - passage (method; Ch. VIII, light kasina, source lines 3282-3375): Beginners take a light sign from sun striking a bowl of water and reflecting on a wall.

## [bud-visuddhimagga-nanamoli] The Path of Purification (Visuddhimagga)
Bhadantacariya Buddhaghosa; 5th century CE; translation 1956; BPS online ed. 2011; classical_text; rights in_copyright; access fulltext_saved; screen pass; keep; relevance 5, practicality 4, confidence medium (Digest only. The full Ch. IV learning-sign instructions, Ch. VIII breath-sign passages and Ch. XX illumination were not visible in the digest and are not recorded.)
- mode: imaginal_crossing. The sign is first apprehended by constantly looking with the eye, then the counterpart sign appears 'free from the kasina disk', in space ('like a crystal fan in space'), a purified, more definitely visualised image; the translator's note says the counterpart sign is 'more definitely visualized image' than the Pitaka usage. The digest does not itself say the image lies in the visual field with eyes shut (the search agent's hint to that effect was not visible).
- eyes: both; stimulus independence: unclear
- ONSET induction: A full induction: prepare the kasina object, sit, look at it with eyes moderately open, repeat the name ('light, light'), and the learning sign then counterpart sign arise. Those with previous-life practice get the sign on seeing the object in nature; others must prepare one. A blunt statement of difficulty: the preliminary work is difficult for a beginner and only one in a hundred or a thousand can do it, and arousing the sign is again that hard. No fixed time scale in the digest.
- techniques: gazing_fixed_point, concentration_visual_object, light_source_gazing, visualization_crossing, eye_position, devotion_or_transmission; phenomena: lamp_or_flame, sphere_pearl_bindu, sun_disc, moon_disc, colour_fields, smoke_mist_cloud, all_pervading_light
- conditions: posture: Seat himself comfortably.; gaze_or_eye_position: Eyes open moderately, as if seeing one's face reflected in a mirror; not reviewing the colour or characteristic.; setting: Screened place on the outskirts of the monastery; for light kasina, a circle of sun or moonlight through a hole in a wall or a lamp in a pot with a hole facing a wall.; prerequisites: Previous-life practice makes the sign arise easily in nature; beings hindered by kamma, defilement or lack of faith cannot develop any kasina.
- stages: learning sign (uggaha-nimitta): Like the object, with its faults (embers, smoke, stamens); for the light kasina like the circle thrown on wall or ground. -> counterpart sign (patibhaga-nimitta): Purified, motionless, free of the disk: crystal fan in space, red cloth or gold fan for fire, compact bright cluster of lights for light. -> extended light for divine eye: Light kasina extended at access level; repeated entry into basic jhana consolidates light in the delimited area.
- with the light: Apprehend and develop the sign; protect and extend it; for the divine eye extend the light kasina at access level and renew it by repeatedly entering jhana and pervading with light, until it stays in the area delimited ('Let there be light here').
- obstacles/fixes: Eyes too wide: fatigue and the disk too obvious; too narrow: drowsiness and the disk not obvious. Keep them moderately open. | Light disappears when the preliminary work's turn runs out: re-enter basic jhana, emerge and pervade with light again.
- cautions: Do not review the colour or attend to its characteristic. | Kasina work is very difficult; only one in a hundred or a thousand succeeds at the preliminary work.
- interpretation: The signs are mind-made abstractions of the kasina object, the counterpart sign being the basis for access and absorption jhana; the light kasina grounds the divine eye and the torch simile shows the light as a temporary aid.
- practice: Choose your kasina: for light, take the circle thrown on a wall or floor by sun or moon through a hole, or put a lamp in a pot with a hole facing a wall. Sit comfortably and look with eyes open moderately, as if seeing your reflection in a mirror, neither strained nor drowsy. Do not review the colour or characteristic. Repeat 'light, light' and keep apprehending the sign. The learning sign comes, like the circle; then the counterpart sign appears, a compact bright cluster of lights, which leads to access and absorption. To extend it for the divine eye, keep it at access level, then enter basic jhana again and again and pervade the area with light, until it holds.
  - passage (method; Ch. IV earth kasina section 28, source lines 11062-11098): Eyes open moderately, like looking at one's reflection in a mirror.
  - passage (caution; Ch. IV section 28, source lines 11062-11098): Eye opening is a troubleshooting variable: too wide or too narrow both prevent the sign.
  - passage (method; Ch. V light kasina sections 21-23, source lines 13832-13910): The light kasina is the circle of sun, moon or lamp light on a wall; its counterpart sign looks like a compact bright cluster of lights.
  - passage (onset; Ch. XII section 8, source lines 26491-26588): The author states that kasina preliminary work, and arousing the sign afterwards, succeed for only about one in a hundred or a thousand.
  - passage (development; Ch. XIII section 96, source lines 29514-29550): When the extended light fades, re-enter jhana and pervade with light until it consolidates in the delimited area.

## [vaj-baker-tibetan-yoga-togal] Tibetan Yoga: Somatic Practice in Vajrayāna Buddhism and Dzogchen (chapter 8 of Yoga in Transformation), section 6 'Tögal: Yoga of Active Perception'
Ian A. Baker (quoting Dudjom Lingpa, Jigme Lingpa, Pema Lingpa, Padmasambhava/Khandro Nyingthik, and the Seventeen Tantras); 2018; secondary_conduit; rights in_copyright; access fulltext_saved; screen pass; keep; relevance 5, practicality 3, confidence medium (Secondary scholarly conduit read in digest; no duration, gaze-point or preliminaries beyond the quoted texts. Gaze and posture details beyond source lines 2183-2223 not seen.)
- mode: perceptual. Baker presents the visions as extending perception outward into a field of self-manifesting visions, based initially on entoptic phosphenes, floaters and blood cells within the eye; they are said to project into the field of vision. Dudjom Lingpa describes strands that appear to move, pearls and lattices as seen. Baker explicitly contrasts this with inward sense withdrawal and says the visions are neither wholly subjective nor wholly objective.
- eyes: both; stimulus independence: unclear
- ONSET both: Induction: three postures (lion, elephant, sage) each paired with a gaze (rolled upward, soft-focus sideways, lowered), with slow breath and threefold motionlessness; Pema Lingpa says the first vision arises by turning attention to naturally occurring phenomena in the watery lamp of the eye, and that visions arise only while the posture key points are held, like a snake's limbs showing when squeezed. For someone seeing nothing this gives the posture, gaze, darkness and light conditions, but no time scale, no preliminary requirements and no troubleshooting. Development: the sequence of four visions follows.
- techniques: posture_or_body_lock, eye_position, gazing_fixed_point, sky_or_space_gazing, breath_control, darkness_exposure, attention_to_spontaneous_phenomena, energy_work; phenomena: sparks_or_points, chains_or_strings, geometric_patterns, sphere_pearl_bindu, rainbow, gold_yellow, figures_or_forms, all_pervading_light
- conditions: posture: lion (eyes rolled up), recumbent elephant (soft-focus sideways), squatting sage (lowered gaze); threefold motionlessness of body, eyes and consciousness; gaze_or_eye_position: reverted, lowered and indirect gazes; eyes rolled upward in lion posture; half-closed in sage posture; setting: environments of total darkness and expansive light; duration_and_schedule: not given in the digest; prerequisites: preparatory harmonising of the winds through Trulkhor and Korde Rushen; instruction; other: breath through slightly parted lips and teeth; exhalation and inhalation ideally suspended naturally
- stages: first appearances: Hair-width vajra strands, radiant like gold, moving to and fro; then strung pearls circling bindus; vision of lattices and half-lattices transparent like crystal. -> four visions: Direct Perception of the Ultimate Nature, Increasing Experience, Perfection of Intrinsic Awareness, Dissolution into the Ultimate Nature. -> later forms: Shape-shifting phosphenes become male and female Buddhas within rainbow spheres, then four spheres around a central orb that dilates through steady foveal gaze.
- with the light: Stabilise and let it expand: hold the posture and gaze so the strands become stable, clear lattices and divine forms; the central circle dilates under steady foveal gaze. Treat the early entoptic events as the starting material.
- obstacles/fixes: Visions fail if the posture key points are not held; keep posture and gaze steady. | Early appearances are mobile and unstable; they stabilise with familiarity.
- cautions: Baker notes early Togal texts used a datura decoction in the eyes (out of scope here). | Practice is described as relying on instruction within the lineage.
- safety flags: prolonged_darkness
- interpretation: The visions are the innate luminosity of the heart projected outward through the channels of light and the watery lamp of the eye; Baker also reads the first ones as floaters and blood cells seen in retinal capillaries.
- practice: Take one of three postures, each with its own gaze: lion, with eyes rolled inward and upward; recumbent elephant, with a soft sideways gaze; or squatting sage, with a lowered gaze through half-closed eyes. Hold body, eyes and awareness motionless, breathe slowly through slightly parted lips, and let the abdomen draw in slightly. Practise in darkness or open light. Attend to the naturally occurring phenomena in the eye: fine moving golden strands, then strings of pearls, then lattices. Keep the posture, since the visions arise only while the key points are held. With familiarity the forms stabilise and enlarge into spheres, and a central circle widens under steady gaze.
  - passage (mode; Section 6, p. 370, source lines 2035-2158): Togal reverses sense withdrawal and extends perception outward into self-manifesting visions that start from phosphenes within the eye.
  - passage (stages; Section 6, p. 371, source lines 2035-2158): Dudjom Lingpa: the first lights are hair-width strands, gold-sheened and moving to and fro, becoming strung pearls and then lattices.
  - passage (onset; Section 6, p. 375, source lines 2252-2429): Pema Lingpa: because the visions are physiological they arise only while the posture key points are held.
  - passage (method; Section 6, note 108, source lines 2183-2223): The Continuity of Sound says the method is reverted, lowered and indirect gazes, matched to the three postures.
- new leads: Jigme Lingpa, Supreme Mastery of Wisdom Awareness | Dudjom Lingpa, Vajra Essence (Wallace 2004) | Pema Lingpa, Secret Key to Channels and Winds (Baker 2012, 2017)

## [vaj-geisshuesler-sky-gazing] Tibetan Sky-Gazing Meditation and the Pre-History of Great Perfection Buddhism: The Skullward Leap Technique and the Quest for Vitality
Flavio A. Geisshuesler (translating and paraphrasing the Seventeen Tantras, Vimalamitra's commentaries and Longchenpa); 2024; secondary_conduit; rights in_copyright; access fulltext_saved; screen pass_flagged; keep; relevance 4, practicality 3, confidence high (Academic conduit: passages are the author's translations and paraphrases of Longchenpa and the Seventeen Tantras, fragmentary and selected to serve a historical argument, so sequence and context of the originals are not preserved. Posture and gaze details are thin; the five-posture and five-gaze lists come from a Bon source at third hand (Reynolds). 'stimulus_independence: yes' rests on the three-or-four-days statement about eye pressure and on dark retreat; the sky or lamp remains as a visual ground in daytime practice. The candidate summary's 'where the classical texts treat dark retreat' is only an endnote list of titles. OCR renders Togal as 'Tégal' in places.)
- mode: perceptual. The lights are seen by the eyes against the sky or in a dark room. Longchenpa (Treasury of Words and Meanings) contrasts Thogal with Trekcho precisely on this point: wisdom is 'directly seen', it 'shines forth from the eyes', and seeing is 'through the faculties, independent of the words of mental analysis'. The Tantra of Unimpeded Sound: 'wisdom's key point is that it emerges from the eyes'. The opening description says the mandala finally appears 'without the slightest effort of active visualization'. Geisshuesler himself compares the first lights to entoptic floaters and ganzfeld imagery.
- eyes: open; stimulus independence: yes
- ONSET both: Explicit induction for the person who sees nothing. (1) Treasury of Words and Meanings 283.3: at the very start of actual Thogal, press the eyeballs using two fingers rather than three to give rise to appearances; after three or four days phenomena arise without any pressure (Tibetan in endnote: zhag gsum bzhi na ma ltems kyang snang). (2) Longchenpa repeatedly: if struggling to make the visions arise naturally, begin by pressing on the vitality-channel [srog rtsa], which the book identifies with the aorta (elsewhere the spinal cord); Mirror of the Heart of Vajrasattva 334.1: turn the eyes up to the sky and press this channel and the sky fills with luminous appearances. (3) The Absence of Letters Tantra says press the eyes 'firmly' [rab tu] for the linked sheep; Longchenpa rewrites this as a 'slight' pressing while gazing to the side. (4) Prerequisites: the rushen preliminaries of body, speech and mind done to exhaustion, then settling into naturalness. (5) The lights do not appear at once because they are obstructed in the body; the seal of the heart must be 'torn open'. No statement that light comes only by grace. Time scales: three or four days for the pressure-free onset; forty-nine days as the standard dark retreat. Development: stabilising the chains within the blue expanse, then the four visions.
- techniques: sky_or_space_gazing, eye_pressure_or_sense_closure, eye_position, posture_or_body_lock, breath_control, light_source_gazing, darkness_exposure, movement, sound_or_mantra, energy_work, other; phenomena: smoke_mist_cloud, mirage_shimmer, stars, sparks_or_points, lamp_or_flame, blue, sphere_pearl_bindu, chains_or_strings, colour_fields, rainbow, geometric_patterns, fireflies, figures_or_forms, fish-eye-like nuclei under eye pressure
- conditions: posture: Three postures: lion [seng ge], elephant [glang chen], sage [drang srong]; body and limbs completely still. Endnote, from the Bon Zhangzhung Nyengyu via Reynolds: five postures (dignified lion sitting like a dog on its haunches; reclining elephant on the belly; crouching ascetic; waddling goose; crystal antelope, the hardest).; gaze_or_eye_position: Key point of the gates [sgo'i gnad]: three gazes, upward, downward and sideways, pupils immobile and steady. Bon list of five gazes: upward (Dharmakaya), straight ahead (Sambhogakaya), downward (Nirmanakaya), right (Skilful Means), left (Discriminating Wisdom). Eyes unwavering and mind undistracted.; setting: Cloudless open sky; also the rays of the sun, a lamp, a reflective pool or a crystal; or a completely dark chamber [mun khang] for dark retreat [mun mtshams], also called the night-time practice.; duration_and_schedule: Eye pressure for the first three or four days only. Dark retreat standard length forty-nine days; some masters reported as spending years. No daily schedule given.; prerequisites: Nyingthig preliminaries: yoga of the sounds of the four elements; khordé rushen ['khor 'das ru shan] of body (run, shake, whirl, roll the head, act out the six classes of beings), speech (talk like a lunatic, shout phat, cry like animals until exhausted, then silence) and mind (act out thoughts and emotions); then the body-speech-mind preliminaries. The practice is described as the tradition's most secret.; other: Key point of the wind [rlung gi gnad]: breathe slowly and naturally through the mouth without altering it; Longchenpa adds the winds 'remain still' in three ways (not going out, not filling up inside, not coming and going). Speech: complete silence, 'like a mute'.
- stages: vision of reality's immediacy [chos nyid mngon sum gyi snang ba]: First something like smoke, then white wafting clouds, mirages, stars, fire sparks, butter lamps, and a great pervading blue; eventually light rays, seminal nuclei [thig le] and empty forms. Low intensity, much movement: the nuclei are 'minute and linked', fluttering, appearing and vanishing; they steady only after extended practice. -> vision of the intensification of experience [nyams gong 'phel gyi snang ba]: Light intensifies and balances into five colours; nuclei grow in number and size; linked vajra sheep [rdo rje lu gu rgyud], like strings of pearls, appear within the nuclei; also cloudbanks, lattices, checkered forms, vertical lines, wheels, round rainbows, lotuses, fireflies, stupas. Still fluctuating. -> vision of awareness reaching its limit [rig pa tshad phebs kyi snang ba]: Nuclei and chains 'mature into spiritual bodies'. Longchenpa's six steps: half a Buddha body, single bodies, Buddha with consort, five Buddhas with retinues, the five in a mandala, the great mandala of the hundred peaceful and wrathful deities. -> fourth vision: All manifestation dissolves back into the expanse. Named only as the dissolution; not described in detail in the chapters read.
- with the light: Stabilise and contain. Keep eyes unwavering and mind undistracted; when the blue expanse [dbyings] appears together with the linked sheep, subdue the chains so they do not flutter and hold them inside the enclosure of the expanse (Treasury of Words and Meanings 377.4). Blazing Lamp Tantra: place the chains in the 'prison of the expanse', without grasping. Do not separate from the appearances; remaining so, the four visions reach their full measure. Movement and conceptual reifying are the fault; control channels and winds to cut it.
- obstacles/fixes: Nothing appears at first, because the energy is obstructed in the body: press the eyeballs with two fingers for the first days; or turn the eyes up to the sky and press the vitality-channel. | Chains and nuclei flutter, come and go: their movement is due to the five winds; keep the eyes and pupils unmoving, breathe gently through the mouth, and hold the chains within the blue expanse. | Dull, bound energy: the rushen preliminaries, done to exhaustion and followed by resting in naturalness [rnal du dbab], are what rouse it. | Conceptual movement during practice accumulates karma: cut it by controlling the key points of channels and winds.
- cautions: Longchenpa softens the older tantra's 'press the eyes firmly' to a 'slight' pressing while gazing to the side. | The author notes Thogal is the tradition's most secret practice and that publishing these instructions raises an ethical issue of secrecy. | Aim is realisation of the nature of the experiences, not more experiences (Gyatso, cited).
- safety flags: eye_pressure, sun_gazing, prolonged_darkness, transmission_required_by_tradition
- interpretation: The lights are awareness [rig pa] residing in the heart, travelling through light channels (the kati crystal tube from heart to eyes) and issuing out through the eyes onto the sky; the thig le are both drops of energy in the body and specks in the visual field. Geisshuesler's own reading adds entoptic phenomena, sensory deprivation and culturally shaped meaning-making.
- practice: Prepare with the rushen preliminaries: move, shout and act out every impulse until exhausted, then rest in silence and stillness. For the main practice take a lion, elephant or sage posture and keep body and limbs motionless; stay silent; breathe slowly and naturally through the mouth. Fix the gaze upward, downward or sideways with the pupils steady, on a cloudless sky (the texts also name the sun's rays, a lamp, a pool, a crystal, or a fully dark room). If nothing appears, press the eyeballs with two fingers, Longchenpa says only slightly and while gazing sideways; after three or four days appearances come without pressure. Alternatively turn the eyes skyward and press the vitality-channel. Expect smoke, cloud, mirage, stars, sparks, lamp-like lights and a pervading blue, then restless specks and pearl-like chains. Hold eyes and mind unmoving and keep the chains inside the blue field until they steady; they then multiply, take five colours, and later form Buddha figures and mandalas.
  - passage (onset; Ch. 5, 'The Activation of Vitality', book p. 76, source lines 3909-3916; carries Longchenpa, Treasury of Words and Meanings [Tshig don mdzod] 283.3): At the first moments of actual Thogal the practitioner presses the eyeballs with two fingers, not three, to bring on visual appearances; after three or four days they arise without pressure. The endnote gives the Tibetan and parallel places in the Treasury of the Supreme Vehicle I 407.5-6 and Bima Nyingthig I 373.1.
  - passage (onset; Ch. 5, book p. 76, source lines 3916-3926; carries Mirror of the Heart of Vajrasattva 334.1 and Longchenpa (Tshig don mdzod 284.7; Theg mchog mdzod I 407.6, II 19.7; Bima Nyingthig I 373.1)): For someone struggling to make the visions arise naturally, Longchenpa says to start by pressing the vitality-channel, which the book identifies with the aorta. The tantra: turn the eyes up to the sky, press this channel, and the sky fills with luminous appearances. The source does not say where on the body the pressure is applied.
  - passage (stages; Ch. 4, 'An Experimental Study of Perception', book p. 61, source lines 3135-3149; carries Tshig don mdzod 283.2 (Tibetan in endnote, source lines 8846-8849)): The first vision: one sees something like smoke, then white drifting cloud, mirage, stars, fire sparks, butter lamps and a great pervading blue; then rays, thig le and empty forms. The specks are small, linked and restless, steadying only after long practice.
  - passage (development; Ch. 5, 'The Yogi as Hunter and Herder', book p. 81, source lines 4188-4205; carries Tshig don mdzod 377.4, with Blazing Lamp Tantra 307.4 just before): Key point of awareness: eyes unwavering, mind undistracted. When the blue expanse and the linked sheep dawn together, subdue the chains so they stop fluttering and hold them inside the enclosure of the expanse.
  - passage (method; Ch. 7, book p. 105, source lines 5371-5388; carries The Absence of Letters Tantra [Yi ge med pa] and Tshig don mdzod 386.2): The older tantra says to press the eyes firmly [rab tu] to bring on the linked sheep; Longchenpa gives it as a slight pressing of the eyes while gazing to the side, as the 'placement of awareness'.
  - passage (mode; Ch. 8, book p. 120, source lines 6061-6082; carries Longchenpa's seven distinctions of Thogal from Trekcho, citing Tantra of Unimpeded Sound [sGra thal 'gyur] 177.4): In Thogal the linked chains are seen nakedly in vision through the brightness of the light channels; conceptual thought stops at once. Wisdom is directly seen by the sense faculty, not sought through mental analysis.
- new leads: Longchenpa, Tshig don mdzod ch. 8 (pp. 271-284, 373-407 of the edition cited) and Theg mchog mdzod: the full Thogal instructions excerpted here; Germano 1992 dissertation translates much of it. | Longchenpa's two Nyingthig dark-retreat texts named in the endnote: nyin mtshan 'od kyi 'khor lo and rgya mtsho ar gtad kyi mun khrid 'od gsal 'khor lo. | Mirror of the Heart of Vajrasattva [rDo rje sems dpa' snying gi me long] and Tantra of the Introductions [Ngo sprod spras pa]: introductions by crystal held to a window, eye pressing ('thig le like fish-eyes'), crystal lenses over the eyes while sky gazing. | Reynolds 2011 (Zhangzhung Nyengyu five postures and gazes); Wangyal 2000 (dark retreat, nuclei like quicksilver); Hatchell 2014, Naked Seeing.

## [vaj-sekoddesa-summary-of-empowerment-84000] Summary of Empowerment (Sekoddeśa; Tib. dbang mdor bstan pa), Toh 361
Indian Buddhist tantra, held to be a section of the lost Kālacakra root tantra (Paramādibuddha); introduction draws on Nāropa's Sekoddeśaṭīkā; c. 11th century text; English first published 2020; classical_text; rights in_copyright; access fulltext_saved; screen pass; keep; relevance 4, practicality 2, confidence medium (Terse root verses read in digest; method details live in the commentary, which was not available.)
- mode: perceptual. Verses say the reflection is seen in the cloudless sky with eyes neither closed nor open, like a dream, 'without having imagined them'; it is not conceptually meditated, and the virgin simile in the divination mirror is used for something seen that neither she nor the officiants produce. The text insists the signs are not imagined, though the verses are terse.
- eyes: both; stimulus independence: unclear
- ONSET unclear: The verses state the signs (smoke, mirage, firefly, lamp at night; flame, moon, sun, darkness, digit of the moon, great drop by day) and that they are seen with eyes neither closed nor open in the empty sky, and that one must meditate on them constantly without conceptualising. They give no technique for getting the first sign if none appears; the introduction notes the method is mostly left to commentary. Elsewhere the same tradition (Baker) places sense withdrawal in total darkness. The text is a tantra with restrictions on reading it (publisher's warning), implying transmission.
- techniques: sky_or_space_gazing, eye_position, attention_to_spontaneous_phenomena, devotion_or_transmission; phenomena: smoke_mist_cloud, mirage_shimmer, fireflies, lamp_or_flame, moon_disc, sun_disc, black_light, lightning_flashes, sphere_pearl_bindu
- conditions: gaze_or_eye_position: eyes neither closed nor open; setting: in the cloudless sky or emptiness; night signs and day signs; duration_and_schedule: constant meditation on the reflection; prerequisites: empowerments; the tradition restricts access to the text; other: breath control immediately after the reflection is seen
- stages: night signs: Smoke, mirage, firefly, lamp. -> day signs: Flame, moon, sun, darkness, digit of the moon (the light of lightning, per a translators' note), great drop (the light of a blue moon disk illuminating all things, per a note).
- with the light: Do not conceptualise it; keep meditating on it constantly; then apply the five mental aspects (insight, examination, analysis, joy, immovable bliss) and, once the reflection is seen, do breath control immediately.
- obstacles/fixes: Imagining or conceptualising the sign spoils it; the sign must be seen unimagined.
- cautions: Tantra with restrictions and commitments; the publisher advises consulting a lineage authority.
- safety flags: transmission_required_by_tradition
- interpretation: The signs are reflections of emptiness, inconceivable because they bring all conceptions to rest, and designate the fruit of reality.
- practice: In the first limb, withdrawal, the senses are turned from outer objects to inner reflections of emptiness. The text lists the signs: at night smoke, mirage, fireflies and a lamp; by day flame, moon, sun, darkness, a digit of the moon and the great drop. They are seen in the empty, cloudless sky with the eyes neither closed nor open, as in a dream, and they are not to be imagined or conceptualised. The yogin meditates on them constantly, then applies five mental aspects. The simile of a girl who sees a figure in a divination mirror is given for seeing what has not been produced by the viewer. Once the reflection is seen, breath control is performed at once. Methods for first producing it are not in the verses.
  - passage (method; Translators' introduction, Sixfold Yoga, source lines 293-412): In withdrawal the senses are turned from outer objects to inner objects, which are reflections of emptiness appearing as night and day signs.
  - passage (mode; Verse 28, source lines 805-861): The reflection is seen in the cloudless sky and is not conceptual meditation; verse 27 gives the eyes as neither closed nor open, and verse 28 says it is seen without having imagined it.
  - passage (stages; Translators' introduction, Sixfold Yoga, source lines 293-412): The yogin's meditation on the signs is likened to a virgin seeing a magical image in a divinatory mirror (verses 29-34).
  - passage (interpretation; Glossary note on great drop, source lines 3801-3856): A glossary note identifies the great drop as the light of a blue moon disc illuminating everything.
- new leads: Nāropa, Sekoddeśaṭīkā commentary on verses 24-34 (Sferra and Merzagora 2006)


# STREAM: Hindu yoga and tantra (hindu-yoga-tantra)

## [hin-aurobindo-letters-on-yoga-3] Letters on Yoga III: Experiences and Realisations in the Integral Yoga (Part Two, The Opening of the Inner Senses)
Sri Aurobindo; letters 1927-1950; this volume 2014; teacher_manual; rights in_copyright; access fulltext_saved; screen pass; keep; relevance 5, practicality 3, confidence high (Digest only; flame-gazing letter's opening lines were cut off in the digest.)
- mode: perceptual. Aurobindo says lights are seen with the inner eye and even the outer eye, 'There is no imagination in the seeing of these lights', and that anything seen with closed eyes can be seen with open eyes once inner sight reaches the subtle physical.
- eyes: both; stimulus independence: unclear
- ONSET both: Faculty is latent and often opens unasked early in practice; first signs are sparkles/dots, rings of light round luminous objects like a star, then colours. Yogis develop it by tratak on a single luminous point or object. Quiet mind not needed, only opening of the brow centre; can be developed by effort and concentration by those with some inborn faculty. Some find it difficult. Colour/light cannot be forced by physical pressure; it is by 'psychic and spiritual, not a physical pressure'. No time scales in digest.
- techniques: light_source_gazing, gazing_fixed_point, concentration_nonvisual, attention_to_spontaneous_phenomena; phenomena: sparks_or_points, stars, colour_fields, white, gold_yellow, blue, violet, sun_disc, moon_disc, lamp_or_flame, lightning_flashes, all_pervading_light, light_in_the_room
- conditions: gaze_or_eye_position: Concentration at the centre between the eyebrows; tratak on a single point or luminous object.; prerequisites: Some latent or inborn faculty helps; opening of the Ajna centre.; other: Mind need not be quiet for seeing lights.
- stages: first signs, externalised way: Sparkles or small luminous dots, then circles of light or colour round objects (a star), then colours, though not always in that order. -> descent of Light: A flow or sea of white, golden or blue light entering and surrounding the body and illumining the whole field of consciousness; may be seen or felt.
- with the light: Let the opening of vision develop; do not over-weight individual visions until symbolic or significant; observe and describe exactly (colour shade matters); develop the consciousness by which they come. Light may eventually be lived in, filling every cell.
- obstacles/fixes: Scepticism: throw off physical explanations and develop the power and experience. | Colour reports need exact description (yellow vs gold).
