- cautions: Gazing on a flame or bright spot to cross the threshold is discouraged: it may lead to passive concentration open to all sorts of things. | Not all lights come from the paramjyotih; many lights.
- safety flags: tradition_warns_against_seeking
- interpretation: Lights are subtle, not physical, signs of forces of various planes; colour indicates the force (white Mother, gold Truth, blue spiritual, violet grace); sun is the symbol of Truth.
- practice: Aurobindo tells correspondents that sight of supraphysical light is a latent faculty, often the first to open in yoga, usually without effort and more easily with eyes shut, though it also comes with eyes open. Initial signs are sparkles or dots, rings of light around luminous objects such as a star, then colours. Indian yogis deliberately develop the power by tratak, concentrating vision on a single point, preferably luminous; a correspondent's star-gazing counted as such. A quiet mind is not required; what matters is the opening of the subtle vision at the brow centre, which can be developed by effort and concentration where some inborn faculty exists. Do not press physically; the opening comes by psychic and spiritual action. Let the vision develop, describe colours accurately, and learn their meaning over time. In another letter he advises against gazing on flame or bright spot to force the threshold, as it risks passive trance.
  - passage (onset; Part Two, 'Sensing Supraphysical Things', source lines 4519-4558): Indian yogis develop the power by tratak, concentrating vision on a single, preferably luminous, point; star-gazing was such an exercise.
  - passage (stages; Part Two, 'Sensing Supraphysical Things', source lines 4519-4558): First signs: sparkles or dots, then rings of light round objects, then colours, not always in that order.
  - passage (mode; Ch. One, 'Seeing Light', source lines 5608-5652): The lights are subtle but actually seen when inner vision is open; not imagination.
  - passage (onset; Ch. One, 'Seeing Light', source lines 5608-5652): Seeing lights depends on opening of subtle vision at the brow centre, not a quiet mind; can be developed by effort by those with some inborn faculty.
  - passage (caution; 'Subtle Physical Visions', source lines 5366-5408): Warns against using flame or bright-spot gazing to force the threshold.
- new leads: Letters on Yoga II on concentration between the eyebrows

## [hin-aurobindo-record-of-yoga] Record of Yoga
Sri Aurobindo; diary 1909-1927; first book publication 2001; practitioner_testimony; rights in_copyright; access fulltext_saved; screen pass; keep; relevance 4, practicality 2, confidence medium (Digest only of a very long diary in idiosyncratic terminology; rupa content is mostly images and scenes, light content thinner.)
- mode: perceptual. Diary distinguishes images seen with physical eye (sthuladrishti) from chittakash images with closed eyes, and notes the sun of the pranic world seen with the physical vision, warmth felt.
- eyes: both; stimulus independence: yes
- ONSET development_only: A diary of an already-advanced practitioner; no instruction for someone who sees nothing. Shows tratak of the sun for about a minute followed by coloured subtle suns, and tratak on floating colours. Later records abandoning 'the pressure of the eye on the akasha' and that perfect images arise spontaneously, to the side or above the brows, without thought or tratak. No time scales for beginners.
- techniques: light_source_gazing, gazing_fixed_point, afterimage_retention, attention_to_spontaneous_phenomena, visualization_crossing; phenomena: sun_disc, colour_fields, green, blue, gold_yellow, red, sparks_or_points, lightning_flashes, fireflies, stars, figures_or_forms, geometric_patterns
- conditions: gaze_or_eye_position: Tratak on the physical sun for about a minute, then closed eyes; eye pressure on the akasha used then abandoned.; duration_and_schedule: Tratak for a minute (June 1909).; prerequisites: Long daily yogic practice.
- stages: crude to dense to developed forms (rupa): Images on a background or in the akasha progress from crude, unstable forms to dense, developed, stable and finally perfect images placed full before the vision. -> antardarshi / bahirdarshi: Waking vision with eyes closed versus eyes open.
- with the light: Observe and record details; aim for stable, perfect images placed before the vision; interpret as symbols.
- obstacles/fixes: Forms used to vanish under tratak; this habit was gradually eliminated. | Pressure of the eye on the akasha abandoned as a method.
- safety flags: sun_gazing
- interpretation: Images and lights are siddhi of rupadrishti, symbolic of forces and planes; the sun is the symbol of vijnana.
- practice: The diary shows Aurobindo's own training rather than a method. In June 1909 he gazed (tratak) at the sun for about a minute and then saw, in the chittakash, deep green, a bluish-green or pale yellow sun with waves of subtle substance round it; with eyes closed a yellowish green with a blue patch; looking at the sky a large deep-yellow patch floated before the eyes. Next evening he practised tratak on floating colours. Later he records working with images on walls and window-frames, then in open space, with eyes closed or open. He drops pressing the eye on the akasha, notes that forms no longer vanish under tratak, and that perfect images come spontaneously at the side or above the brows.
  - passage (testimony; 17-25 June 1909, source lines 1743-1805): After a first gaze at the sun he saw deep green in the mental ether, with a zigzag coming out of the sun.
  - passage (method; 17-25 June 1909, source lines 1743-1805): After tratak and closed eyes, gazing at sky gave a floating yellow patch.
  - passage (development; 26 Nov-31 Dec 1912, source lines 6258-6297): Eye pressure on the akasha to develop imperfect forms is being abandoned.
  - passage (mode; source lines 73620-73659): With eyes closed another ether, chittakasha, appears in which images are seen; jagrat samadhi images can also appear outside as if in the physical atmosphere.

## [hin-avalon-serpent-power] The Serpent Power: being the Shat-chakra-nirupana and Paduka-panchaka
Purnananda Svami (Shat-chakra-nirupana, 1577) with Kalicharana's commentary; introduction by Arthur Avalon (Sir John Woodroffe); 1577 text; translation 1919; 2nd revised ed. 1924; classical_text; rights public_domain; access fulltext_saved; screen pass; keep; relevance 5, practicality 4, confidence high (OCR is noisy; quote cleaned of OCR errors in a few words ('Chetas', 'house which hangs'). Anchors taken from clean runs.)
- mode: ambiguous. The verse says the yogi 'sees ... sparks of fire distinctly shining', and the commentary insists 'These sparks of Fire shine quite distinctly', but the commentator also glosses them as sparks of light that 'appear before his mental vision'. The rasananda passage of the Introduction says the Light (Jyotih) 'is seen'. Jyotirdhyana proper is an imaginal practice (meditating on the candle-like flame of the jivatma in muladhara or the Om-flame between the brows) with no claim of crossing.
- eyes: closed; stimulus independence: unclear
- ONSET induction: Gives a procedure claimed to produce the sparks: Yoni-mudra (heels at anus and on foot, crow-beak inhalation, thumbs close ears, index fingers on eyes, middle fingers on nostrils, rest on mouth, kumbhaka, meditation on the mantra/pranava at the ajna), until the chetas 'by repeated practice' dissolves there; first sparks of fire above the triangle, then a steady light like the morning sun. Alternative: in a silent place close both ears with puraka and kumbhaka until nada is heard; 'by daily practice' the Anahata sound is heard and the Light seen. Prerequisite: the knowledge is gained 'by the service of Parama-guru' and 'not by ten million of Shastras'. No time scale.
- techniques: eye_pressure_or_sense_closure, breath_control, posture_or_body_lock, sound_or_mantra, concentration_nonvisual, visualization_crossing, devotion_or_transmission; phenomena: sparks_or_points, lamp_or_flame, sun_disc, all_pervading_light, lightning_flashes
- conditions: posture: Siddhasana-type seat (left heel at anus, right heel on left foot), body, neck and head straight; gaze_or_eye_position: Eyes closed by index fingers; attention at the ajna, above the triangle of the pranava; setting: Silent place (for ear-closing practice); duration_and_schedule: Repeated / daily practice; no durations given; prerequisites: Instruction from and service of a guru; other: Kaki-mudra inhalation; khechari mudra given as an alternative reading of 'closing the house'
- stages: sparks of fire (Pavana-suhridam kana): Sparks distinctly shining within and above the triangle at the ajna, once the mind dissolves there under Yoni-mudra -> Jyotih: After the sparks, a light in the form of a flaming lamp, lustrous like the morning sun, glowing 'between the Sky and the Earth', extending from muladhara to sahasrara -> nada then jyotih (rasananda): Ear-closing with kumbhaka: sounds from cricket chirp to kettledrum, then the Anahata sound, then the Light with Manas in it is seen
- with the light: Treat the light as the place where Bhagavan manifests; continue pranava-yoga until manas dissolves (unmani). No instruction to refuse it.
- obstacles/fixes: The mind cannot be steadied unless completely detached from the material sphere; Yoni-mudra does this
- cautions: Knowledge comes only through a guru's instruction, not from books
- safety flags: eye_pressure, breath_retention, transmission_required_by_tradition
- interpretation: The sparks surround the flame-like Atma shaped like the pranava; the following light is the Jyotih in which the Lord manifests in fulness.
- practice: Sit with the left heel at the anus and the right heel on the left foot, spine, neck and head straight. Draw air in through lips pursed like a crow's beak and fill the belly. Close the ears with the thumbs, the eyes with the index fingers, the nostrils with the middle fingers and the mouth with the remaining fingers; hold the breath and, senses withdrawn, meditate on the pranava at the point between the brows. With repeated practice, as the mind dissolves there, sparks of fire appear distinctly shining above the triangle at the ajna; after the sparks comes a light like a flaming lamp, bright as the morning sun. An alternative from Woodroffe's introduction: in a silent place close both ears, inhale and hold, until inner sounds appear; with daily practice the Anahata sound comes and the Light is seen. The commentator insists the method is learnt from a guru.
  - passage (onset; Shat-chakra-nirupana v. 36, file lines c. 20100-20107): When the yogi closes the house and the mind dissolves at the ajna by repeated practice, he sees sparks of fire distinctly shining.
  - passage (method; Commentary on v. 36, file lines c. 20150-20165 (Yoni-mudra definition, from Sharada Tilaka XXV)): Yoni-mudra: posture, crow-beak inhalation, fingers closing ears, eyes, nostrils and mouth, breath held, meditation on the mantra.
  - passage (mode; Commentary on v. 36, file line c. 20137): Commentator's gloss: the sparks appear before the mental vision, a hedge against the verse's 'sees'.
  - passage (stages; v. 37 commentary, file lines c. 20283-20286): Sequence: sparks first, then the steady Light like the morning sun.
  - passage (method; Introduction, Practice, printed p. 222, file lines c. 11040-11052): Rasananda yoga (Gheranda): ear closure with puraka and kumbhaka; nada, then the Light with Manas in it is seen.
- new leads: Sharada Tilaka Tantra ch. XXV vv. 45-46 (source of the Yoni-mudra definition)

## [hin-gheranda-samhita-vasu] The Gheranda Samhita
Anonymous (c. 17th-18th c.); c. 1700 text; translation 1895, reprinted 1914; classical_text; rights public_domain; access fulltext_saved; screen pass; keep; relevance 3, practicality 3, confidence medium (Digest only. Digest windows from the Siva Samhita sharing the same saved file were not used here.)
- mode: ambiguous. Jyotir dhyana is framed as contemplation ('let him contemplate on this flame'); the digest does not say the light is perceived. Trataka verse names clairvoyance only. Nose-tip gaze with 'luminous reflection of the moon' is contemplation with imagining of nectar.
- eyes: both; stimulus independence: unclear
- ONSET unclear: Gives beginner-usable steps: trataka (gaze unblinking at small object until tears), contemplate flame at muladhara or Om-light between eyebrows, gaze on nose tip contemplating moon's luminous reflection. No time scales; says nothing to someone who sees nothing beyond 'keep contemplating'.
- techniques: gazing_fixed_point, visualization_crossing, concentration_visual_object, eye_position; phenomena: lamp_or_flame, moon_disc, all_pervading_light
- conditions: posture: Padmasana for the eye/gaze exercises.; gaze_or_eye_position: Unblinking gaze until tears; gaze on tip of nose or between eyebrows.
- stages: three dhyanas: Sthula (gross, figure of guru), Jyotir (light), Sukshma (point/bindu and kundali); light contemplation ranked above gross form.
- with the light: Contemplate it continually; light as Brahma; by it all sins destroyed and Siddhas seen.
- interpretation: The light is the Luminous Brahma, the jivatma as lamp-flame, or the light of Om between the eyebrows.
- practice: Gheranda offers three light-related methods. Trataka: gaze steadily without blinking at a small object until tears flow; the fruit is Sambhavi siddhi and clairvoyance. Jyotir dhyana (6.15-17): contemplate the jiva as a lamp-flame in the muladhara as luminous Brahma, or contemplate the light consisting of Om between the eyebrows; the yogi by this attains success and sees his Self. In pranayama (5.43-44) fix the gaze on the tip of the nose, contemplating the luminous reflection of the moon there, and imagine its nectar purifying the vessels. The text frames these as contemplation and does not describe the light as seen; nothing is said about troubleshooting.
  - passage (method; Lesson 1, Trataka, source lines 8736-8780): Gaze unblinking at a small object until tears flow; Sambhavi siddhis and clairvoyance follow.
  - passage (method; Lesson 6, Jyotir Dhyana, source lines 11124-11187): Alternative light contemplation on the flame of Om between the eyebrows.
  - passage (method; Lesson 5, source lines 10558-10596): Gaze on tip of nose contemplating the moon's luminous reflection while retaining breath.

## [hin-hatha-yoga-pradipika-sinh] The Hatha Yoga Pradipika
Svatmarama (15th c.); 15th century text; translation 1914/1915; classical_text; rights public_domain; access fulltext_saved; screen pass; keep; relevance 3, practicality 3, confidence medium (Digest only; trataka verse itself states no light result. Colour/star passage is the translator's own 1915 introduction, not the Sanskrit text.)
- mode: perceptual. Sinh's introduction says one sees colours 'not with his eyes' and then stars and lightning flashes; verse 4.40 speaks of seeing the light with half-closed eyes.
- eyes: both; stimulus independence: unclear
- ONSET both: Gives induction methods: trataka (2.31) and Sambhavi/Unmani/Taraka gaze on the nose tip with half-closed eyes (4.38-40). Sambhavi is learnt only by a guru's favour. Introduction describes progression from agitated colour-waves to steady ocean of light with practice, but gives no time scale and says the highest stage is the lot of 'the favoured and fortunate few only'.
- techniques: gazing_fixed_point, eye_position, sound_or_mantra, breath_control, devotion_or_transmission; phenomena: colour_fields, stars, lightning_flashes, all_pervading_light, sparks_or_points
- conditions: posture: Seated; breath stilled in ida and pingala.; gaze_or_eye_position: Half-closed eyes fixed on tip of the nose; eyebrows raised slightly; or gaze between eyebrows.; prerequisites: Guru's favour for Sambhavi; purified nadis.; other: Introduction links nada practice to later colour/light seeing.
- stages: colours of the five elements: Various colours, then glittering stars and lightning flashes, all fleeting. -> ocean of light: Colour-waves at first agitated (unsteady mind) become steady and motionless as one deep ocean of light as the mind calms.
- with the light: Dive into the ocean of light and forget the world; fix gaze on the light to induce Unmani.
- obstacles/fixes: Agitated colour-waves reflect an unsteady mind; calm it by continued practice.
- cautions: Trataka and the six actions to be kept secret. | Highest stage the lot of the favoured few.
- safety flags: transmission_required_by_tradition
- interpretation: Light is the great seed, the all (Tattva); Sinh's note calls the ocean of light the condition of highest bliss.
- practice: Trataka: calmly gaze steadily at a small mark until the eyes fill with tears. For the light itself, the Taraka/Unmani verses direct the practitioner to fix the eyes, half closed, on the tip of the nose with mind steady and breath stilled in ida and pingala, and to fix the gaze on the light so seen, raising the eyebrows slightly while the mind rests inwardly on Brahman; Sambhavi mudra is learnt by a guru's favour. Sinh's introduction adds that during contemplation colours, stars and lightning flashes are seen, not with the physical eyes; they are first agitated and, as the mind calms, steady into one deep ocean of light.
  - passage (stages; Introduction, source lines 419-457): Colours first appear as agitated waves reflecting an unsteady mind, then become steady as one deep ocean of light.
  - passage (mode; Introduction, source lines 419-457): Colours, stars and lightning are seen not with the physical eyes and are fleeting.
  - passage (method; Ch. 2 verse 31, source lines 2224-2287): Trataka definition: steady gaze at small mark until tears.
  - passage (method; Ch. 4 Unmani, source lines 5874-5973): Fix the gaze on the light seen at the nose-tip and slightly raise the brows to produce Unmani.

## [hin-hume-thirteen-upanishads] The Thirteen Principal Upanishads (Svetasvatara 2.8-13; Maitri 6.18-22)
Anonymous (Svetasvatara and Maitri Upanishads); c. last centuries BCE; translation 1921; classical_text; rights public_domain; access fulltext_saved; screen pass; keep; relevance 4, practicality 3, confidence high (Hume 1921 (2nd ed. 1931); quotes lightly corrected for OCR ('Fire-Mies' = Fire-flies). Mode is undetermined by the text itself.)
- mode: ambiguous. The nine forms are 'preliminary appearances' (purvarupani) of yoga; the text does not say how they are seen, but they are listed as phenomena arising in practice, not objects to imagine. Hume's footnotes add no gloss.
- eyes: unspecified; stimulus independence: not_applicable
- ONSET induction: Gives the practice whose preliminary signs are the lights: steady posture with head, chest and neck erect; senses with mind drawn into the heart; breath repressed and then breathed through the nostrils 'with diminished breath'; mind restrained like a chariot of vicious horses; in a clean, level, sheltered, quiet place by water. No time scale, no guru requirement, nothing said to one who sees nothing.
- techniques: breath_control, posture_or_body_lock, concentration_nonvisual, sensory_restriction; phenomena: smoke_mist_cloud, sun_disc, lamp_or_flame, fireflies, lightning_flashes, moon_disc, white
- conditions: posture: Body steady, head, chest and neck erect; setting: Clean level spot free from pebbles, fire and gravel; sound of water; hidden retreat sheltered from wind; not offensive to the eye; other: Senses with the mind made to enter the heart
- stages: preliminary forms (2.11): Fog, smoke, sun, fire, wind, fireflies, lightning, crystal, moon: forerunners of the manifestation of Brahman in yoga -> first stage (2.13): Lightness, health, steadiness, clear countenance, pleasant voice, sweet odour, scanty excretions -> vision (2.14-15): The self, used as a lamp, beholds the nature of Brahman
- with the light: Treat the forms as preliminary signs on the way to the manifestation of Brahman; nothing about dwelling on them.
- safety flags: breath_retention
- interpretation: The appearances are forerunners (purvarupa) that produce or precede the manifestation of Brahman in yoga.
- practice: Choose a clean, level, quiet place sheltered from wind, near the sound of water. Sit with head, chest and neck in a line, body still. Draw the senses with the mind into the heart. Repress the breath, then breathe through the nostrils with diminished breath, and hold the mind back as one reins vicious horses. Fog, smoke, sun, fire, wind, fireflies, lightning, crystal and moon appear as the preliminary forms that precede the manifestation of Brahman; bodily lightness and health mark the first stage; finally the self, as a lamp, beholds Brahman.
  - passage (stages; Svetasvatara 2.11, file lines 22504-22508): The nine forms are preliminary appearances leading to the manifestation of Brahman.
  - passage (method; Svetasvatara 2.8-2.9, file lines 22480-22490): Erect posture, senses drawn into the heart, breath repressed and diminished, mind reined in.
  - passage (method; Svetasvatara 2.10, file lines 22492-22497): Setting: clean, level, quiet, sheltered.
  - passage (interpretation; Svetasvatara 2.15, file lines 22545-22548): The self as lamp beholds Brahman.

## [hin-rama-prasad-natures-finer-forces] The Science of Breath and the Philosophy of the Tattvas (Nature's Finer Forces), with a translation of the Shivagama / Shiva Svarodaya
Rama Prasad (essays); anonymous Shiva Svarodaya (translated text); 1890; 2nd ed. 1894; teacher_manual; rights public_domain; access fulltext_saved; screen pass; keep; relevance 4, practicality 3, confidence medium (Digest plus one grep. Six-month and two-year time scales mentioned by the search agent were not seen in the digest text.)
- mode: perceptual. Rama Prasad says he sees bright sheets of green and white 'in the room, in the heavens, on the table before me, on the wall in front', i.e. in the open-eyed visual field; the shadow method ends in looking up into the sky and seeing a figure; the Shiva Svarodaya says tattvas 'rise before the eyes' when senses are closed.
- eyes: both; stimulus independence: yes
- ONSET both: Author says the power 'can be made to show itself by a sustained attempt to look into space, or anything else, as the moon, a star, a jewel' after pranayama and trained attention centred on the eyes. Shadow-figure method gives a concrete trial for the beginner (gaze at the neck of the shadow during 108 repetitions of a mantra, then look up). Sense-closure verse gives a second induction. Time scales for the shadow method (six months, two years) were in the digest hints but not confirmed in the digest text.
- techniques: gazing_fixed_point, sky_or_space_gazing, afterimage_retention, eye_pressure_or_sense_closure, breath_control, sound_or_mantra, light_source_gazing; phenomena: colour_fields, green, white, red, figures_or_forms, light_in_the_room, sun_disc, stars
- conditions: posture: Standing, back to the sun, in a lonely place (shadow method).; gaze_or_eye_position: Gaze at the neck of own shadow, then look up into the sky; or sustained looking into space at moon, star or jewel.; setting: Alone, sunlit.; duration_and_schedule: As long as 108 calm repetitions of 'Om kram parabrahmane namah'.; prerequisites: Pranayama and trained attention.; other: Fingers close ears, nostrils, mouth and eyes (verse 328).
- stages: sensuous lucidity: Attention trained on the eyes sees the physiological and atmospheric colours of Prana; green washed by white forecasts rain, green streaked with red delays it.
- with the light: Read it as a colour-sign of prana and tattvic conditions; the shadow figure is read for omens of life and death.
- safety flags: breath_retention
- interpretation: The colours are tattvic vibrations of Prana (red Agni, blue Vayu, white Apas, yellow Prithivi, dark Akasha) seen when the senses are made lucid.
- practice: Rama Prasad first prescribes pranayama and training of attention, centred on the eyes, after which one sees the colours of Prana; he reports sheets of green, white and red appearing in the room, the sky, on a table or wall. He says the power shows itself by sustained looking into space or at the moon, a star or a jewel. The translated Shivagama adds two methods: stop ears, nostrils, mouth and eyes with the fingers and the tattvas begin to rise before the eyes; or stand alone with the back to the sun, look at the neck of your shadow for as long as you can calmly repeat a mantra 108 times, then look up into the sky where a figure appears. The colours and missing limbs of the figure are read as omens.
  - passage (testimony; Essay 'Yoga - the Soul', source lines 6963-7001): Author sees sheets of green washed by white anywhere about him, and uses them to forecast rain.
  - passage (onset; Essay 'Yoga - the Soul', source lines 6963-7001): Look steadily into space, or at the moon, a star, a jewel, to bring the power out.
  - passage (method; Shiva Svarodaya vv. 306-307, source lines 9759-9772): Stand with back to the sun, gaze at the neck of the shadow, for 108 mantra repetitions, then look up into the sky.
  - passage (method; Shiva Svarodaya v. 328, source lines 9850-9889): With mouth, nose, eyes and ears stopped by the fingers, the tattvas begin to rise before the eyes.

## [hin-shiva-samhita-vasu] The Siva Samhita
Anonymous (c. 15th-17th c.); c. 1500-1700 text; translation 1914; classical_text; rights public_domain; access fulltext_saved; screen pass; keep; relevance 4, practicality 3, confidence medium (Digest plus one grep. The finger-closure verses 22-26 were not in the digest and could not be confirmed; I rely on the search agent's note for them.)
- mode: perceptual. Shadow method ends in looking at the sky and seeing a grey figure; the third-eye verse says 'he then perceives a fire brilliant like lightning' and the great yogi 'beholds light, pure as holy mountain'. The third-eye verse begins with 'constantly thinks', so that one crosses from imagination to perception.
- eyes: both; stimulus independence: yes
- ONSET induction: Shadow invocation is an explicit trial for a beginner: at sunrise or by moonlight gaze at the neck of your shadow, then look into the sky; if a full grey figure appears it is auspicious. Third-eye verse: keep thinking one has a third eye in the middle of the forehead until a lightning-like fire is perceived. Daily practice; 'by practising it always, he begins at last to see it in his heart'. Teaching is a great secret, given only to qualified yogis. Three years mentioned for success of qualified practitioners.
- techniques: gazing_fixed_point, sky_or_space_gazing, afterimage_retention, visualization_crossing, eye_position, eye_pressure_or_sense_closure, devotion_or_transmission; phenomena: figures_or_forms, lightning_flashes, all_pervading_light, gold_yellow, moon_disc, sun_disc, lamp_or_flame
- conditions: posture: Padmasana for the nose-tip gaze and third-eye exercise.; gaze_or_eye_position: Steady gaze on neck of shadow, then up to sky; gaze on tip of the nose; attention between eyebrows.; setting: Rising sun or moonlight, clear sky.; duration_and_schedule: Daily; light contemplated day and night for the Siddha result; success in three years for qualified practitioners.; prerequisites: Qualified yogi; devotion to God and Guru, free of serious illness.
- stages: shadow in the sky: A full grey shadow seen in the sky is auspicious; with constant practice it is eventually seen in the heart.
- with the light: Contemplate it continually; by it sins are destroyed and Siddhas are seen; lie down and contemplate it to remove weariness.
- cautions: Yoga to be kept secret and revealed only to those with all the qualifications of a yogi.
- safety flags: transmission_required_by_tradition
- interpretation: The seen shadow is a divine reflection (Pratika) giving God's sight; the light is a spiritual light that destroys sins and gives lordship over the light.
- practice: Siva Samhita chapter 5 gives two seen-light methods. Pratikopasana: at sunrise or by moonlight, with a clear sky, fix the gaze steadily on the neck of your own shadow, then look up into the sky; seeing a full grey shadow there is auspicious, and daily practice gives long life and finally sight of it in the heart. Third-eye exercise: seated in Padmasana, constantly think that you have a third eye, the eye of Shiva, in the middle of the forehead; you then perceive a fire brilliant like lightning; contemplate this light day and night and you will see the Siddhas. Gaze at the tip of the nose in Padmasana and the yogi beholds a pure light, becoming its lord and guardian. Lie down and contemplate the light to remove fatigue.
  - passage (method; Ch. V, vv. 15-21 Invocation of the shadow, source lines 6456-6517): Gaze steadily at the neck of the shadow thrown, then look into the sky; a full grey shadow is auspicious.
  - passage (development; Ch. V, v. 21, source lines 6456-6517): Persistent practice moves the figure from the sky to the heart.
  - passage (onset; Ch. V, vv. 45-46, source lines 6657-6696): Constantly thinking one has a third eye in the forehead, one perceives a lightning-bright fire; thinking of it day and night brings sight of Siddhas.
  - passage (method; Ch. V, vv. 49-50, source lines 6706-6743): Gazing on the nose tip in Padmasana, the yogi beholds pure light and becomes its lord.
  - passage (caution; Ch. V, v. 42, source lines 6657-6696): The teaching is secret and for qualified yogis only.

## [hin-sivananda-japa-yoga] Japa Yoga: A Comprehensive Treatise on Mantra-Sastra
Swami Sivananda; undated on page (first ed. c. 1930s-40s; not verified); teacher_manual; rights in_copyright; access reader_extract_saved; screen pending; keep_marginal; relevance 2, practicality 3, confidence low (Rests on a WebFetch reader extract of the introduction only; the extract states no japa-light linkage on that page.)
- mode: ambiguous. The deity image is 'visualised' after alternating open-eye gazing and eye closure; nothing says it becomes perceived. Coloured lights are mentioned only as something to ignore.
- eyes: both; stimulus independence: unclear
- ONSET unclear: No claim that japa produces seen light. For someone who cannot visualise: picture any part, or a hazy image, and let it sharpen; failing that, fix the mind on the effulgent light in the heart. Time scale: 'some months' for a clear image.
- techniques: concentration_visual_object, visualization_crossing, sound_or_mantra; phenomena: colour_fields, figures_or_forms
- conditions: posture: Seated motionless; gaze_or_eye_position: Open-eye gaze at picture, then closed eyes; setting: Early morning (Brahmamuhurta, 4 a.m.); duration_and_schedule: A few minutes of gazing; 5-6 alternations; months
- with the light: Ignore visions and coloured lights; treat the heart-light as the form of the Ishta if the image will not come.
- obstacles/fixes: Cannot hold full image: visualise a part or a hazy image | Still failing: fix the mind on the light in the heart
- cautions: Ignore the visions and coloured lights
- safety flags: tradition_warns_against_seeking
- interpretation: Lights and visions are by-products to be disregarded; the heart-light can stand in for the deity's form.
- practice: Gaze at a picture of your Ishta-Devata for a few minutes, close the eyes and try to hold it; when it fades open the eyes and gaze again; repeat five or six times, for months, until the image is clear. If the whole image will not come, take a part or a hazy outline; if that fails, rest the mind on the effulgent light in the heart and treat it as the deity. In silent early-morning meditation, sit motionless and ignore any visions or coloured lights.
  - passage (method; Introduction, Part III (reader extract)): Alternate open-eye gazing at the picture with closed-eye holding, 5-6 times.
  - passage (onset; Introduction, Part III (reader extract)): Fallback when no image comes.
  - passage (caution; Introduction, Part III (reader extract)): Disregard lights in meditation.
- new leads: Japa Yoga ch. 4-5 (PDF es69.pdf) unread

## [hin-sivananda-kundalini-yoga] Kundalini Yoga
Swami Sivananda; 1935 (first ed.); 10th ed. 1994; WWW edition 1999; teacher_manual; rights in_copyright; access fulltext_saved; screen pass; keep; relevance 5, practicality 5, confidence high (Digest only; the sixth passage anchor spans a line break and may need adjusting.)
- mode: ambiguous. Trataka on a star or moon is stated to produce coloured lights, a huge mass of light, multiple moons, or no moon though the eyes are open, i.e. seen with eyes open. Closed-eye lights are said to 'float before the mind's eye' and he adds that visions may be subjective, objective, or purely imaginary, so he himself does not settle the mode.
- eyes: both; stimulus independence: yes
- ONSET both: For someone who sees nothing: do trataka (ten graded exercises), starting two minutes and increasing cautiously, with mind on the spot; if gaze cannot be held, close eyes and gaze at an imaginary spot between the eyebrows; daily for a month. Lights appear in meditation first as a pin-point white light at the brow. Onset in a few days to six or nine months depending on mind and concentration; some never see lights yet progress.
- techniques: light_source_gazing, gazing_fixed_point, sky_or_space_gazing, concentration_visual_object, concentration_nonvisual, posture_or_body_lock, visualization_crossing; phenomena: sparks_or_points, white, gold_yellow, red, blue, green, smoke_mist_cloud, lightning_flashes, moon_disc, sun_disc, stars, lamp_or_flame, light_in_the_room, figures_or_forms
- conditions: posture: Siddhasana or Padmasana; lying on an open terrace for the star/moon exercise; no shaking of the body.; gaze_or_eye_position: Unwinking gaze on object; brow point or nose tip; closed-eye imaginary spot between eyebrows for weak gaze.; setting: Open terrace for star or moon; morning or evening for open sky; guide for sun.; duration_and_schedule: Start with two minutes, increase cautiously; practise a month and report; meditation of 2-3 hours makes lights steady; lights last 10-15 minutes.; prerequisites: Mind must stay on the spot; sun-gazing only with an experienced guide.; other: Breathe slowly during light visions.
- stages: trataka on star or moon: Different coloured lights, then one colour with other stars vanishing, then a huge mass of light all around, then two or three moons or none though eyes are wide open. -> closed-eye lights: A pin-point white light at the brow; coloured lights, flashes, sparks; small floating balls of white or red; after months a full blaze of white light bigger than the sun, at first unsteady, later steady for 10-15 minutes.
- with the light: Do not shake the body; keep steady and breathe slowly; adore the Devatas that appear; ignore the visions as encouragements and do not waste time looking at them; discriminate subjective from objective.
- obstacles/fixes: Cannot gaze steadily: close eyes and gaze at an imaginary spot between eyebrows. | Weak eyes: practise with eyes closed on an imaginary spot; do not over-practise. | Light too dazzling: withdraw from looking and break the meditation; some are afraid. | Mind wandering makes hours of gazing worthless.
- cautions: Sun-gazing without an experienced guide may cause serious trouble; use cooling oil and honey on the eyes with a guide. | Do not mistake visions for realisation or stop sadhana. | Do not waste time looking at visions.
- safety flags: sun_gazing, tradition_warns_against_seeking
- interpretation: Lights are Tanmatric lights, signs that the mind is steadying and that one is transcending physical consciousness; encouragement from the Ishta Devata; may be subjective or objective.
- practice: Sivananda lists ten trataka exercises: picture of a deity, black dot on white wall, Om, a bright star or full moon watched while lying on an open terrace, open sky, own pupil in a mirror, brow point or nose tip, inner chakras, ghee-lamp flame, and, only with a guide, the rising sun. Start with two minutes, gaze unwinking until tears flow, keep the mind on the spot, then close the eyes and hold the image. Star or moon gazing brings coloured lights, a single colour, a huge mass of light, multiple moons. If you cannot hold the gaze, close the eyes and gaze at an imaginary spot at the brow. In meditation a pin-point white light appears at the brow, then coloured lights and balls, later a blaze bigger than the sun. Sit perfectly still, breathe slowly, and do not linger on the visions.
  - passage (method; Yoga Sadhana, 5. Trataka, source lines 3788-3945): Lie on an open terrace and gaze at a star or full moon; coloured lights, then one colour, a mass of light, doubled or absent moons appear.
  - passage (onset; Yoga Sadhana, 5. Trataka instructions, source lines 3788-3945): Begin with two minutes and extend gradually; the mind must be on the spot.
  - passage (onset; Yoga Sadhana, 5. Trataka instructions, source lines 3788-3945): If steady gazing is impossible, close the eyes and gaze at an imaginary spot between the eyebrows.
  - passage (stages; Yoga Addenda, Mystic Experience - Visions of Lights, source lines 6553-6617): Closed-eye meditation begins with a pin-point white light at the brow, then coloured lights, then balls, then a blaze bigger than the sun.
  - passage (caution; Yoga Addenda, source lines 6623-6664): Visions are encouragement only; ignore them, and discriminate whether they are subjective or objective.
  - passage (onset; Yoga Addenda, source lines 6623-6664): Visions may come within days for some, six or nine months for others; some never have them but progress.

## [hin-thirty-minor-upanishads-aiyar] Thirty Minor Upanishads (Mandalabrahmana and other Yoga Upanishads)
Anonymous (medieval Yoga Upanishads); medieval texts; translation 1914; classical_text; rights public_domain; access fulltext_saved; screen pass; keep; relevance 4, practicality 3, confidence medium (Digest only; quote lines are tidied OCR-sensitive text in anchors, validate checks anchors only. Several signs lists are brief.)
- mode: perceptual. Mandalabrahmana describes colours 'seen' at set distances before the nose with eyes fully open, streaks of light at the corners of the eyes while looking at space, and a graded sight with closed, half-open and fully open eyes; Sambhavi occurs while 'the physical eyes are seeing externally without winking'.
- eyes: both; stimulus independence: unclear
- ONSET induction: Gives three lakshyas as procedures: close ears with forefingers, fix mind on the booming sound, blue light between the eyes and in the heart; look at external space at 4 to 12 digits before the nose and moving the eyes see streaks of light at the corners, after which vision steadies; or practise fully opened-eye gaze at the nose tip, first deep darkness at the palate root, then a sphere of light. No time scales; 'the great science concealed in all the tantras'; a guru's words referenced elsewhere.
- techniques: gazing_fixed_point, eye_position, sound_or_mantra, eye_pressure_or_sense_closure, sky_or_space_gazing, breath_control, concentration_nonvisual; phenomena: colour_fields, blue, red, gold_yellow, sphere_pearl_bindu, stars, moon_disc, sun_disc, lamp_or_flame, fireflies, smoke_mist_cloud, lightning_flashes, all_pervading_light, black_light
- conditions: posture: Neck and body erect, mouth closed.; gaze_or_eye_position: Fully open eyes aimed at the nose tip (purnima); eyes moved while looking at space; ears closed with forefinger tips for the internal lakshya.; prerequisites: Taraka yoga practised with introvision accompanied by manas.; other: Prana and Apana united and kumbhaka for the western light.
- stages: signs of Sambhavi (purva): Seen in order: a star, a dazzling diamond, the sphere of the full moon, the brightness of nine gems, the midday sun, a flame of fire. -> uttara (western) light: Lustres of crystal, smoke, bindu, nada, kala, star, firefly, lamp, eye, gold, and nine gems.
- with the light: Fix the gaze and let the light-sequence unfold; behold the endless sphere of light as Brahman; absorb mind in the bliss it gives.
- safety flags: breath_retention, transmission_required_by_tradition
- interpretation: The taraka light is Brahman, the Sachchidananda; Brahma-tejas, the spiritual effulgence.
- practice: The Mandalabrahmana Upanishad gives three targets. Internal: close the ears with the tips of the forefingers, fix the mind on the booming sound, and a blue light is seen between the eyes and in the heart. External: look into space before the nose and one sees at 4, 6, 8, 10 and 12 digits successively blue space, indigo-black, a red wave, then yellow tones; looking at space while moving the eyes, streaks of light show at the eye corners and vision becomes steady; a light 12 digits above the head brings the nectar state. Brahmana II says to practise with fully open eyes aimed at the nose tip: first a deep darkness at the palate root, then an endless sphere of light. Signs come in order: star, diamond, full moon, nine gems, midday sun, flame.
  - passage (method; Mandalabrahmana Upanishad, Brahmana I, source lines 11560-11786): With ears closed by the forefingers a booming sound is heard; fixing the mind on it, a blue light is seen between the eyes and in the heart.
  - passage (onset; Mandalabrahmana Upanishad, Brahmana I, source lines 11560-11786): Moving the eyes while looking at space, streaks of light appear at the eye corners, after which vision can be made steady.
  - passage (mode; Mandalabrahmana Upanishad, Brahmana II, source lines 11560-11786): Three sights by eye aperture; the fully open-eyed purnima gaze aimed at the nose tip is to be used.
  - passage (stages; Mandalabrahmana Upanishad, Brahmana II, source lines 11560-11786): Signs of progress appear in order from star to diamond, full moon, nine gems, midday sun, flame.
- new leads: Other Yoga Upanishads in the volume (Yogatattva, Dhyanabindu, Tejobindu) not read beyond the digest

## [hin-vijnana-bhairava-tantra] Vijñānabhairava
Anonymous (Trika tantra, attributed to the Rudrayāmala); c. 7th to 9th century; classical_text; rights public_domain; access fulltext_saved; screen pass; keep; relevance 4, practicality 4, confidence high (Read whole in Sanskrit. The English renderings are the reader's own; mode is ambiguous except in v. 36.)
- mode: ambiguous. Verse 36 is explicit that the bindu is seen (dṛṣṭe bindau, 'when the bindu has been seen') after the eyes are closed with the hands; verse 76 has the gaze fixed on space made variegated (śabalīkṛte) by the brilliance of sun or lamp. Elsewhere the verbs are dhyāyet / bhāvayet (meditate, contemplate), so most dhāraṇās do not say whether anything is seen; the text's interest is the dissolution of mind, not the light itself.
- eyes: both; stimulus independence: unclear
- ONSET induction: Short self-contained methods, each claimed to work on its own (v. 140: by joining with any one, one becomes Bhairava). V. 36 is the clearest induction of seen light: close the eyes (and other openings) with the hands, pierce or press at the brows, and a bindu appears, which gradually dissolves. No time scales except 'by degrees' (krameṇa, 34, 36) and 'within days' for related practices (51, 107). Nothing is said to someone who sees nothing; the text assumes any one method suffices and asks only steadiness (dārḍhya).
- techniques: eye_pressure_or_sense_closure, darkness_exposure, sky_or_space_gazing, light_source_gazing, gazing_fixed_point, eye_position; phenomena: sphere_pearl_bindu, sparks_or_points, colour_fields, black_light
- conditions: posture: seated; v. 34 sitting with eyes closed, mind placed within the skull; gaze_or_eye_position: closed with the hands (36); fixed and unwavering on clear sky (84); fixed on space lit by sun or lamp (76); eyes simply motionless (113); setting: a dark night of the waning fortnight for darkness contemplation (87); duration_and_schedule: not stated; 'by degrees'; prerequisites: none stated; the text is to be given only to devoted disciples (158-159)
- stages: bindu (36): After closing the eyes with the hands and piercing at the brows, a point (bindu) is seen; it dissolves by degrees, and in its midst is the supreme state. -> fiery tilaka (37): A bindu shaped like a subtle fiery tilaka, arising from inner agitation, meditated on at the crown or heart, leads to absorption.
- with the light: Let the seen bindu dissolve and rest in what is in its midst (36); in all methods the aim is the mind's dissolution, not the light.
- cautions: The text is to be given only to devoted disciples of the guru line (vv. 157-160).
- safety flags: eye_pressure, transmission_required_by_tradition
- interpretation: Light, darkness and open space are doorways: the seen bindu or the luminous space is where the mind dissolves into Bhairava's own form (bhairavaṃ vapuḥ), which is consciousness itself.
- practice: Several of the 112 methods use the visual field. Sit, close the eyes with the hands and press or attend at the brows, shutting the other openings: a point of light is seen; let it dissolve gradually and rest in what lies within it (v. 36). Alternatively, fix an unwavering gaze on a clear sky until the body is still (84); fix the gaze on space made bright and dappled by sun or lamp (76); on a moonless night contemplate the darkness itself for a long time, or with eyes closed attend to the blackness before them and expand it (87-88); or simply hold the eyes motionless (113). Each is said to suffice on its own. The text asks only steadiness, gives no time scale, and treats the light as a threshold for the mind's dissolution, not a goal.
  - passage (onset; v. 36): With the eyes closed by the hands, piercing at the brows and the openings shut, when the bindu is seen and gradually dissolves, in its midst is the supreme state. The one verse that names light as seen, and an induction by sense closure and pressure.
  - passage (stages; v. 37): Meditating on a bindu shaped like a subtle fiery tilaka, arising from agitation within, at the crown or in the heart, one is absorbed.
  - passage (method; v. 76): When space is made dappled by the brilliance of the sun, a lamp and the like, fix the gaze right there: one's own nature shines forth. A light-source gazing method.
  - passage (method; v. 84): Looking at the clear sky with unbroken gaze, the body motionless, one at once attains Bhairava's form. Sky gazing, eyes open.
  - passage (method; vv. 87-88): On a dark night of the waning moon, contemplating the darkness for long, one reaches Bhairava's form; likewise, first closing the eyes, expanding the black expanse before one and contemplating it as Bhairava, one becomes it. Darkness gazing, eyes open and closed.
  - passage (stages; v. 32): Meditating on the five voids through circles variegated like peacock feathers, one enters the supreme void in the heart. Whether the coloured circles are seen or imagined is not said.
- new leads: Kṣemarāja's and Śivopādhyāya's commentaries (Vivṛti) on vv. 32, 36-37 for how the bindu is said to appear


# STREAM: Sant Mat and light-and-sound lineages (sant-mat)

## [snt-dlm-knowledge-session] The Knowledge Session (old Divine Light Mission session from the 'Mahatma's handbook'; current first-technique instructions; scriptural parallels)
ex-premie.org (unsigned site pages; scripture commentary attributed on the page to Michael Dresbach); undated web pages; describes 1970s DLM practice and the later video Knowledge session; teacher_manual; rights in_copyright; access reader_extract_saved; screen pass_flagged; keep_marginal; relevance 3, practicality 3, confidence medium (reader_extract_saved: model summaries of web pages; no first-person reports of seen light.)
- mode: perceptual. The handbook says the Divine Light is seen, but only through the third eye, not ordinary eyes; no description of what is actually seen.
- eyes: closed; stimulus independence: unclear
- ONSET induction: Old form: gentle eyeball pressure, relax, and 'after some time' light should appear. Current form drops pressure and the word light. The handbook says the light is revealed only through the third eye opened by the Master's grace, so it is not forced. No time scale beyond 15 minutes per sitting.
- techniques: eye_pressure_or_sense_closure, eye_position, devotion_or_transmission, posture_or_body_lock; phenomena: all_pervading_light
- conditions: posture: Back straight, arms propped on a T-shaped rest (baragon, later banned) or elbow on armchair, chest or cushion.; gaze_or_eye_position: Eyes closed, gaze toward centre of forehead; forefinger on the third-eye point.; setting: Group Knowledge session with instructors correcting hand position.; duration_and_schedule: 15 minutes per technique; about 30 each for a two-hour sitting; no timer.; prerequisites: Initiation by a Master or mahatma.; other: Thumb and middle finger on the closed eyes: gentle pressure (old), steadying only (current).
- with the light: Relax and follow whatever you feel.
- obstacles/fixes: Fatigue: lower the hand, relax, start again. | Sleep: choose a time and posture that avoid it.
- cautions: Current form: no need to press; keep the fingers steady so the eyeballs do not move.
- safety flags: eye_pressure, transmission_required_by_tradition
- interpretation: The handbook calls it God's Divine Light, seen only through the third eye opened by a Perfect Master's grace.
- practice: Sit with the back straight, arms supported. Close the eyes and keep them closed. Place the forefinger at the centre of the forehead. Rest the thumb and middle finger on the closed lids at the inner corners. In the 1970s form they pressed each eyeball gently; the current form says not to press, only to keep the eyeballs from moving. Turn the gaze inward toward the centre of the forehead and relax. After some time light is expected to appear; when it does, relax and follow what you feel. Practise about 15 minutes, with no timer, and if tired lower the hand and begin again. The source does not describe what is seen. It adds that the light is seen only through the third eye, opened by the Master's grace.
  - passage (onset; KSession.htm, Divine Light section): Light is expected to appear after some time of gentle eye pressure and relaxation.
  - passage (mode; KSession.htm, Divine Light section): The light is not seen with ordinary eyes; it depends on the Master's grace opening the third eye.
  - passage (method; aspir5.htm, First Meditation Technique): The current version removes pressure and only steadies the eyeballs.
- new leads: Strasburg's journey on this page set reports the same change from pressing to steadying.

## [snt-expremie-journey-ajw] Journeys: A. J. W. (Anth Ginn)
Anth Ginn (handle AJW); 1998, with additions signed 2001 (recounting 1972-1998); practitioner_testimony; rights in_copyright; access fulltext_saved; screen pass; keep_marginal; relevance 2, practicality 1, confidence high (Full page text read. Corrections to the candidate record: (1) the page never describes how any technique is done, so the candidate's expected 'eye_pressure_or_sense_closure' is not supported by this text; (2) the 'first technique' passage is linked by the author to remembering the Holy Name and stopping thought, not to light; (3) the null result is explicit only for the initiation; absence of later light is by silence across a 25-year account, not by direct statement.)
- mode: perceptual. The light is defined only by the promise made to him before initiation: on meditating he would 'see this light, brighter than a thousand suns, better than acid', alongside music, nectar and the Word. That is a seen light compared with a drug vision. His own report is negative: at initiation he did not see it. No positive visual experience of his own is described anywhere in the text.
- eyes: unspecified; stimulus independence: not_applicable
- ONSET unclear: No method is given. He says only that Mahatma Umeshfee judged him 'ready' and that he 'was shown the four meditation techniques'; the techniques themselves are not described. Prerequisite for being shown them: accepting that there is one Master and one method and that one must practise Knowledge and dedicate one's life to Maharaji. Result at initiation (March 1973): no light, no music, 'something was buzzing'. What the organisation offered the person who sees nothing, in his later sarcastic summary: it is your own fault, you are too impure for the Grace to land, keep going. He records a 1976 phase of 'workshops' in which premies admitted they did not 'blast off into God consciousness' on sitting to meditate. He stayed twenty-five years (1973 to 1998), with ashram years of meditation each morning after rising at six; he never mentions light arriving later, but also never states outright that it never did.
- techniques: devotion_or_transmission, other; phenomena: none_seen, buzzing_sound
- conditions: posture: Sitting, formally (late-1980s instruction for the 'first technique').; setting: Initiation in a room with a mahatma, March 1973; thereafter ashram and community life of satsang, service and meditation.; duration_and_schedule: Ashram: up at six, a hymn to Maharaji, meditation, then breakfast and work. 1970s commandment to 'constantly meditate and remember the Holy Name' through the day; from the late 1980s the 'first meditation technique' only when sitting formally. Durations not given.; prerequisites: Being judged 'ready': one Master, one method, life dedicated to Maharaji. Initiation by a mahatma, later by instructors, from the 1990s by Maharaji himself in large meetings.; other: Before joining he meditated half an hour each morning on a Sai Baba mantra; no light reported from that either.
- with the light: Not addressed; no light arose. The organisation's line as he gives it was to keep practising and purify oneself.
- obstacles/fixes: Seeing nothing: explained inside the organisation as the meditator's own impurity or lack of surrender; the remedy offered was more practice, devotion and service. | Thinking and 'the mind' were taught as what takes you from the Truth; the 'first technique' was said to stop thought.
- cautions: The author's own conclusion is that the promised experience did not come and that failure was turned back on the practitioner; he calls the organisation a cult and regrets the twenty-five years. | Peers who reported 'bliss' at events are compared by him to the crowd in 'The Emperor's New Clothes': everybody was pretending.
- safety flags: transmission_required_by_tradition
- interpretation: As taught to him, the light is one of four inner revelations of God ('Knowledge'), given by the grace of the living Master. By 2001 he treats the whole as cult belief.
- practice: This page contains no usable instruction. It is a negative testimony. A friend promised that after receiving 'Knowledge' from Guru Maharaj Ji he would see a light brighter than a thousand suns when he meditated. In March 1973 a mahatma judged him ready, which meant accepting one Master and one method, and showed him the four techniques. He did not see the light or hear the music; something buzzed. He then lived the full regime for years: ashram life, rising at six, daily meditation, satsang, service, and the instruction to meditate and remember the Holy Name constantly. He reports no later arrival of light across twenty-five years. In 1976 workshops premies admitted among themselves that they did not blast off when they sat. Those who experienced little were told it was their own impurity. The techniques are not described, so the account cannot show what he actually did with his eyes or attention.
  - passage (mode; 1998 memoir, friend Tom's explanation before initiation, source lines 108-113): The promise: on meditating you will see a light brighter than a thousand suns, 'better than acid', hear music, taste nectar and know the Word; the Guru shows you all this and it is called Knowledge.
  - passage (testimony; 1998 memoir, initiation March 1973, source lines 115-122): Having been shown the four meditation techniques, he saw no such light and heard no music; there was only a buzzing.
  - passage (onset; 1998 memoir, initiation March 1973, source lines 120-121): The stated precondition for being shown the techniques was doctrinal: one Master, one method, practise Knowledge and dedicate your life to Maharaji.
  - passage (testimony; 1998 memoir, 1976 'workshops', source lines 265-269): In the 1976 workshop phase premies admitted to one another that sitting to meditate did not produce the advertised experience, and that this was not only a matter of insufficient surrender.
  - passage (caution; 2001 addition, 'Wasting Time', source lines 1093-1098): His summary of how non-experience was explained: Knowledge is experienced through the Master's grace, and if you do not experience it the fault is your impurity; the Grace keeps circling until you clean up.
  - passage (method; 1998 memoir, late-1980s Birmingham meeting, source lines 559-568): Late-1980s change: the 'first meditation technique' is to be done only in formal sitting, no longer through the day. He ties this technique to the 1970s commandment to constantly meditate and remember the Holy Name and says it 'stopped thought'; he does not call it the light technique.

## [snt-expremie-journey-strasburg] Journeys: Tor Strasburg
Tor Strasburg; 2012 (recounting 1972 onward); practitioner_testimony; rights in_copyright; access reader_extract_saved; screen pass; keep_marginal; relevance 3, practicality 2, confidence medium (reader_extract_saved: model summary of the page.)
- mode: perceptual. He speaks of the 'light' and a faint luminosity as things he saw, tied to eye pressure; no detailed description.
- eyes: closed; stimulus independence: no
- ONSET unclear: Receiving Knowledge in 1972 gave an explosive experience, which he attributes to pushing on the optic nerve. When the instruction changed to gentle stabilising, the light largely faded. No guidance for someone who sees nothing.
- techniques: eye_pressure_or_sense_closure, devotion_or_transmission; phenomena: all_pervading_light
- conditions: gaze_or_eye_position: Eyes pushed on (1972), later only gently stabilised (1974).; setting: Frankfurt ashram, after two weeks of satsang.; duration_and_schedule: Daily, diligent practice for two years.; prerequisites: Initiation by a mahatma.
- with the light: Not stated.

In 1974 the instruction was changed to gently stabilising the eyes without pushing. After that the light mostly disappeared, leaving a faint luminosity. He concluded the earlier light came from pressure on the optic nerve. The page gives no further method, and he later practised only occasionally.

  - passage (mode; Receiving K paragraph): Without pressure the light largely vanished, leaving faint luminosity, which shows the strong light depended on the stimulus.

## [snt-faqir-chand-unknowing-sage] The Unknowing Sage: The Life and Work of Baba Faqir Chand
Faqir Chand (1886-1981); edited with introduction by David Christopher Lane; 1981; fifth edition 2014; practitioner_testimony; rights in_copyright; access partial_saved; screen pass; keep; relevance 4, practicality 2, confidence medium (Read in digest only (excerpts); no onset detail beyond the letter.)
- mode: perceptual. He reports seeing white light and coloured light 'within' after silent repetition and concentration on the guru's image; he also reports forms 'projected' onto it. Whether seen in the field or inwardly is not specified.
- eyes: closed; stimulus independence: not_applicable
- ONSET both: The 1978 letter gives a sequence: leave the senses by silent repetition of the name, meditate on the guru's image to stop mental vibration, 'then comes light and sound'. Prerequisites: dispassion for worldly life; sadhana beyond the mental region. No time scale. He says the light is 'revealed' in everyone, but offers nothing for someone who sees nothing beyond this sequence.
- techniques: sound_or_mantra, visualization_crossing, concentration_nonvisual, devotion_or_transmission, attention_to_spontaneous_phenomena; phenomena: colour_fields, white, red, green, sun_disc, moon_disc, stars, figures_or_forms, all_pervading_light
- conditions: duration_and_schedule: Seventy years of practice; deep meditation sometimes only every two or three months reached separation of Self from Light and Sound.; prerequisites: Initiation, dispassion for the world, celibacy in his own early years.
- stages: Light, then Sound, then surat: Beyond mind and forms one sees Light; beyond that Sound; the seer of the light is something other than the light. He reports a fifth state of merging that he could not reach.
- with the light: Do not take it as the goal or trust forms seen in it; ask who sees the light and who hears the sound.
- obstacles/fixes: Attachment to the joy of a region (e.g. a devotee seeing the guru in red light): look instead for the seer. | Falling asleep while attuned to light and sound.
- cautions: Forms and colours seen within are projections of one's own impressions and faith, like a film magnified on a screen. | Those who take light and sound as ultimate do not attain Nam. | Lights can frighten and bewilder if not recognised as projections (quoting the Bardo Thodol).
- safety flags: tradition_warns_against_seeking
- interpretation: The light is the soul, white in itself, coloured according to the individual's nature and desires; the seer of the light is beyond it.
- practice: From his 1978 letter: go within by leaving the physical senses. Repeat the holy name given by the guru silently, without moving the tongue. Meditate on the guru's image until mental vibrations cease. Then light and sound come. At that point do not settle in them. Ask who it is that sees the light and hears the sound, and keep pursuing the seer. He says the colours you see depend on your nature (red, green, sun, moon, stars), the white light is the soul, and any form of the guru or deity appearing in it is your own projection. Forms seen in meditation are not the goal; he himself, after seventy years, treats lights and sounds as phantasmagoria.
  - passage (onset; Lane intro / 1978 letter, OCR lines 669-687): After silent repetition and concentration on the guru's image, light and sound arise; the task then is to find the seer.
  - passage (stages; Teachings, Three, OCR lines 2596-2626): The colour of the light depends on the individual's nature.
  - passage (caution; Teachings, OCR lines 2891-2911): The holy form appearing within is one's own self, not the external guru; attachment to that centre is a trap.

## [snt-kirpal-light-of-kirpal] The Light of Kirpal
Kirpal Singh (1894-1974); questions by Western disciples; talks 1969-1971; first printing 1980; fourth printing 1996; teacher_manual; rights public_domain; access fulltext_saved; screen pass; keep; relevance 5, practicality 4, confidence high (Digest only; the many disciple-by-disciple corrections in the full text were not read.)
- mode: perceptual. He insists 'you cannot visualize, you see', that the light is seen at the seat of the soul behind the eyes, and that the blind also see it; he rejects visualizing the Master's face.
- eyes: closed; stimulus independence: not_applicable
- ONSET both: For someone who sees nothing: withdraw attention from the body and outgoing faculties, still the mind, fix attention at the eye centre (Shiv Netra). He compares seeing no light with climbing a dark staircase: many steps in the dark, then glimpses near the top. Initiation is given as the start: the Master 'gives something to start with', and some see star, moon or sun on the first day. Sitting in a dark room with nothing is a failure of attention, and he blames the mind standing between. Regular, continuous, uninterrupted attention is required; the diary and not digging up the seed are advised.
- techniques: concentration_nonvisual, gazing_fixed_point, eye_position, devotion_or_transmission, sound_or_mantra, attention_to_spontaneous_phenomena; phenomena: stars, moon_disc, sun_disc, colour_fields, gold_yellow, white, red, blue, figures_or_forms
- conditions: gaze_or_eye_position: Attention fixed at the seat of the soul behind the eyes, the Shiv Netra or single eye; look continuously like a child.; setting: Quiet; be cheerful rather than brooding.; duration_and_schedule: Regular and continuous; keep a diary; results come and recede.; prerequisites: Initiation by a Master; rising above body consciousness.; other: Do not use a photo or visualised face.
- stages: sky, star, big star, moon: A beginner's regular way: sees the sky, then stars, then the big star, transcends it, then the moon, transcends it. Those with background start higher.
- with the light: Stay with it and go through: look into the middle of the bright light and it will open; transcend star and moon. Do not leave early.
- obstacles/fixes: Seeing nothing or only darkness: attention not withdrawn, the mind is in between; keep attending, do not give up. | Intellect and doubt interrupt progress; follow instructions implicitly. | Seeing something but leaving too soon, so no more comes. | Red light goes with a pushing attitude: do not push.
- cautions: Never visualise the Master's face; if he is not perfect you become what he is. | Drug experiences are hallucinations. | Do not dig up the seed to check on it; judging each moment spoils progress.
- safety flags: transmission_required_by_tradition
- interpretation: The inner light is God's expression and the soul's own nature; seeing it is the only proof of a Master.
- practice: Sit and withdraw attention from the body and outgoing faculties until the mind is still. Fix the attention at the seat of the soul behind the eyes, the single or third eye, and look there continuously, like a child with a fixed gaze. Do not visualise a face or a candle; the light comes of itself. Expect nothing at first and keep attending: many steps may pass in the dark before glimpses. A beginner sees the sky, then stars, a big star, then the moon; go through each. If a bright light appears, look into its middle and it opens. Colours vary: golden-yellow and white are spiritual, red goes with pushing. Practise regularly, do not interrupt with intellect, and do not stop early.
  - passage (onset; Printed p. 71, source lines 4497-4586): After withdrawing attention and stilling the mind, fix it at the eye centre behind the eyes and you will see light; the many dark steps before the first glimpses are normal.
  - passage (mode; Printed p. 29, source lines 2174-2213): He rejects visualisation of the Master; what appears of itself is true. Most see something but leave too early.
  - passage (stages; Printed p. 73, source lines 4497-4586): A beginner's sequence is sky, stars, big star, moon, each to be transcended.
  - passage (method; Source lines 18121-18157): Look continuously like a child; a fixed gaze fixes the mind; regularity and a diary are needed.

## [snt-kirpal-spiritual-elixir] Spiritual Elixir
Kirpal Singh (1894-1974); letters and answers 1950s-1960s; first published 1967 (Ruhani Satsang); edition used 1993; teacher_manual; rights public_domain; access fulltext_saved; screen pass; keep; relevance 5, practicality 5, confidence high (Digest only; letters are individual replies and some are summarised by the search agent as 'purple veil to gold' which I did not see.)
- mode: perceptual. Letters answer disciples' reports of seen darkness, blue patches, stars, sun, moon and golden light 'in your visual practice'; one writer reports 'varying experiences with your eyes open'. He says gaze 'penetratingly' and look into the middle of what you see; he forbids following sound but not seeing, and elsewhere forbids visualising.
- eyes: closed; stimulus independence: not_applicable
- ONSET both: To someone seeing darkness only: fix the inner gaze penetratingly into the darkness between and behind the eyebrows, with no awareness of the body or breath; you will see the Light of heaven within. Simran repeated mentally, slowly, at intervals, withdraws sensory currents to the eye focus. Light is 'always there'; it appears when attuned and goes at the slightest dispersion. Dry spells are 'a blessing in disguise'; he says to sit at the door and wait without clutching, and the light comes at the opportune moment by the Master's grace. Initiation gives the first sight for many. No time scale beyond 'practice, more practice'; early morning, up to two hours.
- techniques: gazing_fixed_point, eye_position, sound_or_mantra, concentration_nonvisual, devotion_or_transmission, attention_to_spontaneous_phenomena, posture_or_body_lock; phenomena: colour_fields, blue, gold_yellow, white, stars, sun_disc, moon_disc, figures_or_forms, sphere_pearl_bindu
- conditions: posture: Unflinching, easy pose, relaxed, no tension in the body.; gaze_or_eye_position: Eyes closed; inner gaze into the middle of what is seen in the space between and behind the eyebrows; no strain on eyes or forehead.; setting: Early morning is best; avoid sleep.; duration_and_schedule: Regular daily; some sit about two hours; keep a diary.; prerequisites: Initiation and the five charged names; pure conduct.; other: No awareness of the breathing; repeat names mentally at intervals so the gaze is not disturbed.
- stages: stationary light, brighter, bursts: Light first appears stationary, then brighter, then bursts to give a way up. Stars, sun, moon are crossed the same way; then the radiant form of the Master.
- with the light: Look intently into its middle (a big star, the sun, the moon) so it brightens and bursts; do not stay with scenes and figures; absorb in the Master's radiant form.
- obstacles/fixes: Darkness only: keep the inner gaze into the darkness; do not strain. | Light fading: attention slackened or mind drops to the body; hold the gaze constantly. | Clutching: sit at the door and wait. | Dry spells: renewed zeal and regular time. | Sleep: avoid it during meditation.
- cautions: Staying long with scenes, figures or colours delays progress. | Colour does not indicate a plane; it reflects the aspirant's background. | Do not follow the sound to find its source (for the sound practice). | Do not rely on one's own effort to pierce the light.
- safety flags: transmission_required_by_tradition
- interpretation: Light and Sound are the first manifestations of God as the Word; the lights are the Master's astral forms.
- practice: Sit relaxed with the body still. Close the eyes and leave all thought of the body and the breath. Fix the inner gaze into the middle of what you see in the space between and behind the eyebrows, with no strain on the eyes or forehead. If you see only darkness, gaze penetratingly into that darkness. Repeat the charged names mentally, very slowly and at intervals, so the gaze is not disturbed. When light appears, look into its middle and it will grow brighter and burst, opening a way up. Do the same with stars (pick a big one), sun and moon. If the radiant form of the Master appears, absorb yourself in his face. Do not stay with scenes. Practise daily, early morning if possible, keep a diary.
  - passage (onset; Meditation letters, source lines 5434-5472): The way to break through the light is to gaze penetratingly into the middle of the light or whatever is seen, without relying on one's own effort.
  - passage (onset; Meditation letters, source lines 6194-6255): To open inner vision, fix the inner gaze into the darkness between and behind the eyes with no awareness of the body; you will then see the Light of heaven.
  - passage (stages; Meditation letters, source lines 6305-6379): Light is first stationary, then brighter, then bursts; scenes and figures should not be stayed with; sun, moon or stars are crossed the same way.
  - passage (development; Meditation letters, source lines 6751-6789): The light appears when attuned and vanishes on slight dispersion; keeping the gaze constantly fixed keeps it.
  - passage (method; Meditation letters, source lines 5674-5713): Pick out a big star and fix the inner gaze in its middle; it brightens and bursts. The same for sun and moon.

## [snt-kirpal-teachings-meditation] The Teachings of Kirpal Singh (5 books in one file; Book Three: Meditation)
Kirpal Singh (1894-1974); compiled by Ruth Seader; 1974-1976 (Book 1 1974, Books 2-3 1975, Books 4-5 1976); this edition 2017; anthology_or_compilation; rights public_domain; access fulltext_saved; screen pass; keep; relevance 5, practicality 5, confidence high (Digest only; much overlaps with Spiritual Elixir letters.)
- mode: perceptual. He says the references to light are 'not figurative' but literal, inner transcendent light seen by fixing the inner gaze; says do not visualise results.
- eyes: closed; stimulus independence: not_applicable
- ONSET both: For someone who sees nothing: sit in the position held longest, stay wide awake, direct attention to the seat of the soul between and behind the eyebrows, look sweetly and serenely into the middle of the darkness in front, repeat the five names slowly and at intervals. Withdrawal happens by 'effortless effort', not by watching. The stairs image: you have more light the nearer the roof. Light cannot be forced: sit at the door and wait; do not clutch; the Master grants it at the opportune moment. Time factor, patience and perseverance are necessary. The light is given at initiation.
- techniques: gazing_fixed_point, eye_position, sound_or_mantra, concentration_nonvisual, devotion_or_transmission, posture_or_body_lock; phenomena: lamp_or_flame, all_pervading_light, stars, sun_disc, moon_disc, figures_or_forms, colour_fields
- conditions: posture: Any comfortable position in which one can sit longest without moving; wide awake.; gaze_or_eye_position: Steady gaze fixed in between and behind the two eyebrows, without exertion or strain on eyes or forehead.; setting: Quiet solitude helps; night hours praised.; duration_and_schedule: Fixed hours daily; sit extended hours; diary kept.; prerequisites: Initiation, pure life, diary and self-introspection.; other: Do not be conscious of breathing; numbness or stiffness is to be eliminated by not watching withdrawal.
- stages: stars, sun, moon, then Guru Dev: The spirit crosses stars, sun and moon at the border of the subtle region, where the luminous Radiant Form of the Master meets it.
- with the light: Look into the middle of what is seen; fix the inner gaze on your own light; when the Radiant Form appears, stay with it.
- obstacles/fixes: Aware of breathing: look sweetly within, oblivious of body and breath. | Numbness or stiffness: do not watch the withdrawal; keep the gaze fixed. | Nothing seen: you stand in your own way; eliminate yourself and wait at the door. | Mind will not still: simran not constant enough. | Light darkened by impure life: keep life pure and regular.
- cautions: No clutching tendency; do not presuppose or visualise results. | Pranic practices cannot go beyond the astral heaven. | Impure living lets the curtain of darkness obscure the light again.
- safety flags: transmission_required_by_tradition
- interpretation: The light and sound are the primal manifestations of God as Shabd; the light patterns correspond to five planes (the five fires in the head).
- practice: Choose the posture you can hold longest without moving, and stay wide awake. Close the eyes. Direct attention to the seat of the soul behind and between the eyebrows and look sweetly and serenely into the middle of the darkness in front of you, with no strain on the eyes or forehead. Repeat the five charged names mentally, slowly, at intervals; the gaze stays constant while the repetition is intermittent. Do not watch the breath, the body or the withdrawal. If you see something, look into its middle. Do not visualise or expect. If nothing comes, sit at the door and wait, do not clutch, keep regular hours, keep a diary and keep life clean. Light is said to come at the opportune moment.
  - passage (method; Book Three, ch. 16, source lines 10264-10304): Look sweetly and serenely into the middle of the darkness in front of you while repeating the five names slowly and at intervals.
  - passage (onset; Ch. 13 Withdrawal, source lines 9540-9579): When withdrawn completely or partially, you may see into the middle of what is before you, and the inner vision will open once the intellect is stilled.
  - passage (mode; Self-introspection, three restraints, source lines 7612-7653): Fixing the inner gaze midway between and behind the eyebrows, you see your own light inside, which is already there.
  - passage (caution; Ch. 15, source lines 8921-8961): Hold the steady gaze with no exertion or strain; be passive, do not visualise results, they come of their own accord.

## [snt-mehi-philosophy-of-liberation] The Philosophy of Liberation (Moksha Darshan): A Manual of Sant Mat Mysticism
Maharshi Mehi Paramhans (1885-1986); Hindi original (as part four of Satsang-Yoga) 1940; English translation 1998; teacher_manual; rights in_copyright; access fulltext_saved; screen pass; keep; relevance 4, practicality 4, confidence medium (Digest only; the manual says little about what is actually seen.)
- mode: ambiguous. Drishti Yoga is called the yoga of inner Light and said to open 'subtle or Divine sight' and 'Divine vision dawns'; but the bindu is said to be something that cannot be marked or imagined, only 'visualized' by the converging gaze. The text never describes what is seen. The translators' appendix says the Point 'blossoms into inner Light or visions of Light'.
- eyes: closed; stimulus independence: not_applicable
- ONSET induction: A graded sequence for beginners: mental repetition (manas japa) and visualising a holy form (manas dhyan) to concentrate the mind, then drishti yoga: unite the rays of both eyes on the focal point and gaze on it with a steady mind that has no other activity. Constant practice opens the divine sight. Prerequisites: moral conduct (yama/niyama), a steady posture, the guru's service and grace, daily practice at fixed hours (3 a.m. preferred, mid-morning, evening). No time scale. Grace (krpa) is listed as essential.
- techniques: concentration_nonvisual, visualization_crossing, gazing_fixed_point, eye_position, sound_or_mantra, posture_or_body_lock, devotion_or_transmission; phenomena: sphere_pearl_bindu, colour_fields, black_light, red, white, blue, gold_yellow
- conditions: posture: Comfortable asana, head, neck and trunk straight and steady for prolonged periods.; gaze_or_eye_position: Eyes shut comfortably, no turning or pressing of the eyeballs; the rays of both eyes united on the point; no straining of pupils.; setting: Eyes and mouth closed; ears also closed only for the sound practice.; duration_and_schedule: Daily; preferred 3 a.m., then mid-morning and evening; also while falling asleep.; prerequisites: Yama and niyama; avoid drugs, alcohol, meat; guru's service; initiation.; other: Alert, not drowsy; return the mind repeatedly to the focus (pratyahara).
- stages: four stages: Manas japa, manas dhyan, drishti yoga (one-pointedness on the bindu), then sound (nada). Once one-pointedness is steady, drop the point for the sound.
- with the light: Use it only to attain one-pointedness; once steady, leave the bindu and take up the inner sound (light is said to be within the sound).
- obstacles/fixes: Drowsiness: stay alert. | Wandering mind: pratyahara, bring it back to the focus repeatedly. | Eye pain or abnormality from straining: do not strain or press.
- cautions: Straining pupils or eyeballs brings pain and abnormality in the eyes. | Do not press the eyeballs. | Staying with Drishti Yoga after one-pointedness is steady is undesirable (more time, harder path). | Divine light alone cannot reach the Essential Sound.
