- with the light: Remain seated, 'effacing thyself entirely in the marvels of that unknown world'; the colourless sphere replaces the images. Macdonald records that some teachers held lights to belong to the body and to be dangerous to attend to.
- obstacles/fixes: Darkness of the heart: the shaykh says the gaze of the two adepts could not clarify it; the master himself had to act. | Other novices were prevented by other mystical exercises, the master says, from receiving the first apparition.
- cautions: Macdonald reports that some Sufi teachers rejected the seven coloured-light scheme and thought it dangerous for the neophyte to pay too great attention to the lights, which belong to the body.
- safety flags: sleep_deprivation, transmission_required_by_tradition
- interpretation: The first apparition is the image of the master; the images belong to the sphere of colours and forms, which gives way to the sphere beyond colour.
- practice: Tawakkul Beg's account, in practitioner terms: Recite sura 112 a hundred thousand times. Sit through the night facing Mecca, attention on the heart, while the master and senior adepts direct their attention at you. If nothing opens for several nights, the master intervenes: sit before him, bandage the eyes, reproduce his image within, and concentrate everything on the heart. The heart opens, a replica of the room and the master appears within, and the image can be seen equally with the bandage on or off. A radiant figure then comes and speaks inwardly. Stay seated and efface yourself in the unfolding images; after about three months the images fall away into a colourless sphere. The key factor is the living master's directed attention.
  - passage (onset; Lecture VII, printed p. 197, source lines 7586-7663): After four nights of concentrating on his own heart under the master's directed attention, a little light and clearness showed in the heart, without colour or form.
  - passage (method; Lecture VII, printed p. 198, source lines 7586-7663): With the eyes bandaged and told to reproduce the master's image, he concentrates on the heart; it opens at once by the master's spiritual assistance.
  - passage (mode; Lecture VII, printed p. 198, source lines 7586-7663): He sees the master equally with the physical organ and with spiritual sight, whether eyes are uncovered or bound.
  - passage (caution; Lecture VII, printed p. 200, source lines 7732-7769): Macdonald notes teachers who rejected the seven-colour scheme: lights might be met, but attending to them is dangerous; they belong to the body.


# STREAM: Christian contemplative (christian)

## [chr-cloud-of-unknowing] A Book of Contemplation the which is called the Cloud of Unknowing, in the which a Soul is Oned with God
Anonymous English author, late 14th century; late 14th century; this edition 1922 (first 1912); teacher_manual; rights public_domain; access fulltext_saved; screen pass; keep; relevance 3, practicality 2, confidence medium (Digest only; anchors may span line breaks.)
- mode: ambiguous. The 'cloud' is explicitly not seen: it is a lack of knowing, not pictured darkness or imagined light. The only seen lights mentioned are the 'quaint lights and shining in their eyes' of deceived seekers, which the author calls false.
- eyes: unspecified; stimulus independence: not_applicable
- ONSET unclear: Offers nothing for someone who sees no light; the goal is a naked intent to God, not light. The sole light is an occasional 'beam of ghostly light' sent by God when He wishes, not producible by effort.
- techniques: concentration_nonvisual, devotion_or_transmission; phenomena: smoke_mist_cloud, lightning_flashes
- conditions: gaze_or_eye_position: Warns against staring upward at stars or straining to see inwards with bodily eyes.; prerequisites: Humility and charity; amendment of life.; other: One-syllable word such as God or love, held in a naked intent.
- stages: cloud of unknowing: At first only darkness and a naked intent in the will; sometimes God sends a beam of ghostly light through it.
- with the light: Refuse seeking it; treat bodily lights as accidents or hallucination and as a possible fiend's deceit; accept a ghostly beam only as given.
- obstacles/fixes: Imagination and curiosity of wit produce false images: leave them and do not work with them. | Thoughts pressing above the cloud: answer that God alone is sought, put them under a cloud of forgetting.
- cautions: Staring at stars and straining inwards produce false lights, sounds and smells. | The devil has his contemplatives as God has His.
- safety flags: tradition_warns_against_seeking
- interpretation: Light is a rare grace from God, not a goal; the darkness is a lack of knowing, and sensory lights are suspect.
- practice: The Cloud teaches no light technique. The work is a loving, naked intent toward God, held with a short word such as God or love, with all created things put beneath a cloud of forgetting and all thoughts pressed down. At first you find only darkness. Do not picture this darkness, and do not imagine a shining light in it. Do not stare upward, turn the bodily senses inward or strain to see inwardly with the bodily eyes: this overworks the imagination and invites false lights, sounds, smells and burnings. If a beam of ghostly light ever pierces the cloud, it is God's doing, on His timing, and not to be reached by effort. Keep working and ignore it. Real illumination arises within the soul as gladness, not through the windows of the wits.
  - passage (mode; Ch. IV, source lines 2456-2574): The darkness is not an imagined darkness, and one should not imagine a clear shining light; he calls both falsehood.
  - passage (onset; Ch. XXXIV, source lines 4614-4651): Occasionally God, when He pleases, sends a beam of ghostly light through the cloud; it follows devotion and is not produced.
  - passage (caution; Ch. LVII, source lines 7116-7212): Those who stare at the stars and strive for visions are deceived by quaint sounds, lights and shining in their eyes, and smells.

## [chr-diadochos-hundred-chapters] One Hundred Gnostic Chapters (Kephalaia gnostika)
Diadochos of Photiki; 5th c.; ed. 1893; classical_text; rights public_domain; access fulltext_saved; screen pass; keep; relevance 5, practicality 3, confidence medium (Two-column OCR interleaves lines; passages reconstructed by reading both columns; meaning clear.)
- mode: ambiguous. The mind 'sees its own light abundantly' (τὸ ἑαυτοῦ φῶς ... ὁρᾶν) and becomes transparent; Diadochos contrasts this formless light with lights 'with shape', 'as light or as fire', which suggests visible appearances; no reference to eyes.
- eyes: unspecified; stimulus independence: not_applicable
- ONSET development_only: The mind's own light comes when the soul's power has mastered the passions and the mind is frequently energised by divine light. Diadochos forbids taking up asceticism in hope of seeing anything: 'we walk by faith, not by sight', and in the body we cannot see God or his wonders visibly. Nothing for the person who sees nothing; seeing nothing is presented as normal.
- techniques: sound_or_mantra, attention_to_spontaneous_phenomena; phenomena: all_pervading_light, lamp_or_flame, figures_or_forms
- with the light: Formless self-luminosity of the mind: accept as fruit of purification. Any light with shape, fiery form or voice: refuse outright.
- obstacles/fixes: Dreams: divine ones keep one shape and bring calm joy; demonic ones shift shapes, frighten, laugh or scowl suddenly.
- cautions: If light, a fiery form or a voice appears to a struggler, he must on no account accept the vision: it is plain deceit of the enemy, by which many have strayed. | Do not take up the ascetic life in the hope of such things, lest Satan find the soul ready to be seized.
- safety flags: tradition_warns_against_seeking
- interpretation: The mind's own light, revealed by grace when the passions are mastered, is formless; shaped light belongs to the angel of light, i.e. Satan disguised.
- practice: Diadochos gives no technique for producing light. Pursue purification and love of God with full feeling of the heart; keep the mind free of imagery. When the passions are mastered and the mind is often moved by divine light, it becomes transparent and sees its own light: accept this. Anything appearing with form, whether a light, a fire-like shape or a voice, refuse without examination. Do not practise in order to see.
  - passage (caution; Chapters, source lines ~975-990): If light, or some fiery shape, or a voice appears to any of those who struggle, let him on no account accept such a vision: it is manifest deceit of the enemy, by which many strayed from the way of truth.
  - passage (mode; Chapters, source lines ~1077-1080): When the mind begins to be frequently energised by the divine light it becomes wholly transparent, so as to see its own light abundantly; this happens when the soul's power masters the passions.
  - passage (caution; Chapters, source lines ~1083-1088): Everything that appears to the mind in a shape, whether as light or as fire, comes from the enemy's craft, as Paul teaches (Satan disguised as an angel of light).

## [chr-evagrius-on-prayer] On Prayer (153 chapters), printed under Neilos the Ascetic; with Evagrius chapter on the sapphire state of the mind
Evagrius Ponticus; late 4th c.; ed. 1893; classical_text; rights public_domain; access fulltext_saved; screen pass; keep; relevance 5, practicality 3, confidence high (Read in Greek; chapter numbers not reliably visible in OCR, so locators use source lines.)
- mode: ambiguous. The mind 'will see its own state' sapphire- or sky-coloured during prayer; the demon is said to act 'through the brain' to alter the light around the mind, which treats the light as a quasi-sensory phenomenon; no eyes specified.
- eyes: unspecified; stimulus independence: not_applicable
- ONSET development_only: The sapphire light comes when the mind has stripped off the old man and put on that of grace, i.e. after apatheia; Evagrius forbids desiring to see angels, powers or Christ sensibly and blesses the mind that attains 'complete formlessness'. No induction method; the condition is imageless prayer and freedom from thoughts.
- techniques: concentration_nonvisual, attention_to_spontaneous_phenomena; phenomena: blue, sapphire_or_sky_blue, figures_or_forms, smoke_mist_cloud
- with the light: Accept only the formless, sky-coloured self-luminosity of the mind; refuse any sudden form; suspect light that flatters vainglory.
- obstacles/fixes: Demon alters the temperament of the body to produce fantasies when it cannot move memory: those used to dwelling on thoughts are easily bent.
- cautions: Do not long to see angels, powers or Christ sensibly, or you will go mad, taking a wolf for the shepherd. | The beginning of the mind's delusion is vainglory, moving it to circumscribe the divine in shapes and forms. | A demon touching 'that place' through the brain changes the light around the mind as it wishes.
- safety flags: tradition_warns_against_seeking
- interpretation: The mind's own light in pure prayer is the 'place of God'; formed or flattering lights are demonic manipulations, possibly via the brain.
- practice: In prayer, do not picture God and do not let the mind take any shape; stand guard, keeping the mind free of thoughts. Do not seek forms or wish to see angels or Christ. If a strange form appears suddenly during pure prayer, treat it as a trap meant to make you think you have located God. Beware of a light that flatters you: Evagrius thinks a demon can alter the light around the mind through the brain. When the mind has put on grace, it sees its own state in prayer as sapphire or sky-coloured: this is the light that counts.
  - passage (caution; On Prayer, source lines ~1283-1287): Do not long to see angels or powers or Christ sensibly, lest you go wholly mad, receiving a wolf instead of the shepherd and worshipping the enemy demons.
  - passage (mode; On Prayer, source lines ~910-926): I think the demon, touching that place, turns the light around the mind as he wishes ... acting cleverly through the brain, altering the light joined to it and giving it shape, so that the vainglorious suspect a divine manifestation.
  - passage (caution; On Prayer, source lines ~847-854): When the envious demon cannot move memory in prayer, it acts on the body's temperament to make a strange fantasy and shape the mind; one hastening toward formless knowledge is deceived, holding smoke instead of light.
  - passage (development; appended Evagrius chapter, vol. 1 OCR lines ~5667-5673): When the mind has put off the old man and put on the one of grace, it will see its own state at the time of prayer like sapphire or sky colour, which Scripture calls the place of God seen by the elders on Sinai.

## [chr-gregory-sinai-philokalia] Gregory of Sinai: Chapters, On Stillness, On Prayer (Philokalia texts)
Gregory of Sinai; 14th c.; ed. 1893; classical_text; rights public_domain; access fulltext_saved; screen pass; keep; relevance 5, practicality 3, confidence high (Greek OCR has Cyrillic look-alike letters; passages read directly in Greek, meaning clear.)
- mode: ambiguous. Lights and fire are spoken of as things one 'sees' (ἴδῃς φῶς ... ἔξωθεν ἢ ἔσωθεν), outside or inside, so perceptual appearances are in view; but Gregory treats all such forms as suspect products of the imaginative faculty (φανταστικόν) or demonic, while the genuine light is formless and goes with the senses being withdrawn. He does not classify by eyes open/closed.
- eyes: unspecified; stimulus independence: not_applicable
- ONSET development_only: No induction: Gregory explicitly tells the practitioner not to seek lights; true contemplation of light (ὑποστατικὴ τοῦ φωτὸς θεωρία) is 'impossible to find in our generation' because of the passions, and seeking things before their time makes one a fantasist instead of a hesychast. Light is the late fruit of long heart-prayer under obedience, given by grace; the beginner's sign is warmth/'fire of joy', the end is 'fragrant light'.
- techniques: sound_or_mantra, breath_control, posture_or_body_lock, attention_to_spontaneous_phenomena; phenomena: lamp_or_flame, light_in_the_room, figures_or_forms, all_pervading_light
- conditions: posture: seated (treatise on how the hesychast should sit); gaze_or_eye_position: not specified in passages read; setting: stillness, cell; duration_and_schedule: years; under a guide; prerequisites: humility, obedience, purification of passions; other: Jesus Prayer in the heart
- stages: πῦρ εὐφροσύνης: in beginners prayer is felt as a fire of joy rising from the heart -> φῶς εὐωδιάζον: in the perfect it is a fragrant light
- with the light: Refuse: do not accept any light, fire or figure, outside or inside; do not let the mind form images; return the mind to the work of prayer.
- obstacles/fixes: Mind drawn outside or upward by an invisible power: do not trust it, pull the mind back to its work at once. | Mind's natural tendency to form images: hold it formless (ἀνείδεον, ἀσχημάτιστον).
- cautions: Lights at the beginning are the means by which delusion (πλάνη) deceives the inexperienced. | Seeking these things before their time makes one a 'fantasist, not a hesychast' and can cost one's sanity.
- safety flags: tradition_warns_against_seeking
- interpretation: Genuine divine light is formless, comes by grace to the purified, and accompanies withdrawal of the senses; formed lights and fires are imagination or demonic.
- practice: Sit in stillness and practise the Jesus Prayer in the heart, seeking only its warm, formless energy. Do not try to see anything. Expect, as a beginner, warmth or a 'fire of joy' rising from the heart rather than light. If during the work you see light or fire, outside or inside, or a figure of Christ, an angel or a saint, do not accept it and do not let your own mind shape such images; return to the prayer. If the mind feels pulled out or upward by some power, do not trust it; lead it back. Gregory holds that true contemplation of light is the far end of a long, guided, humble life of prayer and that in his day it is very rarely found.
  - passage (caution; Peri planes, source lines ~4115-4125): If while doing the work you see light or fire outside or within, or a supposed form of Christ, an angel or anyone else, do not accept it, lest you suffer harm; and do not yourself let the mind shape such images.
  - passage (onset; chapters, source lines ~3425-3440): Seek only the heart's energy, wholly formless; neither imagine forms of saints nor contemplate lights, for delusion at the beginning especially deceives the inexperienced with such false phantasms.
  - passage (stages; chapters, source lines ~3450-3455): Prayer is in beginners the ever-moving noetic energy of the Spirit: at the start like a fire of joy rising from the heart, at the end like fragrant light.
  - passage (mode; chapters, source lines ~2145-2160 and ~2211-2236): In true prayer the mind is seen wholly formless, sees nothing gross, the senses often drawn in by 'that light'; but the hypostatic contemplation of light is now nearly impossible to find, and one who seeks it before its time becomes a fantasist instead of a hesychast.

## [chr-hildegard-letter-guibert] Letter to Guibert of Gembloux on the mode of her visions (Epistola 'de modo visionis suae')
Hildegard of Bingen; c. 1175 (Pitra: 1171); ed. 1882; practitioner_testimony; rights public_domain; access fulltext_saved; screen pass; keep; relevance 5, practicality 1, confidence high (Read in Latin from Pitra's 1882 edition; OCR errors minor ('vive7itis').)
- mode: ambiguous. She sees 'in my soul, with outer eyes open', awake, never in ecstasy, and explicitly 'not with outer ears nor by thoughts of my heart nor by any combination of my five senses'. So it co-exists with open-eyed vision but she denies it is sensory or imaginative: a third mode. It is seen 'like a starless firmament in a bright cloud'.
- eyes: open; stimulus independence: not_applicable
- ONSET development_only: Present 'from infancy, before my bones, nerves and veins were strengthened' and continuous to her seventies; no practice produced it and none is offered. She attributes it entirely to God working where he wills; her own part is humility, fear, counting herself as nothing.
- techniques: attention_to_spontaneous_phenomena; phenomena: all_pervading_light, white, colour_fields, figures_or_forms
- conditions: gaze_or_eye_position: eyes open; setting: ordinary waking life, day and night; duration_and_schedule: constant from childhood; prerequisites: none stated; humility; other: accompanied by chronic illness
- stages: umbra viventis lucis: constant, non-local light brighter than a sunlit cloud, without dimensions; scriptures, virtues and human works shine in it like reflections in water -> lux vivens: another light within the first, seen rarely; while seen, all sadness leaves her
- with the light: Report what is seen and heard in it; refer everything to God; remain humble.
- cautions: Many wise men, confounded by marvels, ascribed them to themselves from vainglory and fell.
- interpretation: A light from God in which divine teaching is shown; she cannot know its form, as one cannot look fully at the sun.
- practice: Hildegard gives no method. Her letter is valuable as a description: a light seen with open eyes while fully awake, never in ecstasy, constant for decades, not located anywhere, without measurable extent, brighter than a sunlit cloud, which she distinguishes both from sense perception and from thinking. Within it a second, rarer 'living light' appears and removes all sorrow. Her only counsel is humility: count oneself as nothing and leave everything to God.
  - passage (mode; Epist. II, Pitra p. 332, source lines ~19715-19721): I neither hear these things with outer ears nor perceive them by thoughts of my heart or any combination of my five senses, but only in my soul, with outer eyes open, so that I never suffer the failing of ecstasy in them, but see them awake, day and night.
  - passage (testimony; Epist. II, Pitra p. 332, source lines ~19725-19729): The light I see is not local, but far brighter than a cloud that carries the sun; I can consider no height, length or breadth in it, and it is called to me the shadow of the living light; scriptures, virtues and works shine in it as sun, moon and stars appear in water.
  - passage (stages; Epist. II, Pitra p. 333, source lines ~19745-19750): In that same light I sometimes, not often, see another light called the living light; I cannot say when or how I see it; while I see it all sadness and anguish are taken from me.
  - passage (testimony; Epist. II, Pitra p. 333, source lines ~19757-19760): My soul is at no hour without that light called the shadow of the living light, and I see it as if I were looking at a starless firmament in a bright cloud.
  - passage (onset; Epist. II, Pitra p. 332, source lines ~19710-19712): From my infancy, before my bones, nerves and veins were strengthened, I have always seen this vision in my soul up to the present, now over seventy.

## [chr-hilton-scale-of-perfection] The Scale of Perfection
Walter Hilton (d. 1396); late 14th century; this edition 1923; classical_text; rights public_domain; access fulltext_saved; screen pass; keep; relevance 3, practicality 3, confidence medium (Digest only.)
- mode: ambiguous. Distinguishes bodily-eye lights (suspect, Book I ch. 11) from ghostly light seen by the inner eye (Book II); the latter is explicitly not bodily and not imagined: 'not as some ween' a bodily light. Glimmering through the eyelid is used as an analogy.
- eyes: unspecified; stimulus independence: not_applicable
- ONSET induction: To someone who sees no light: abide in the 'lightsome darkness', withdraw thought from earthly things by desire for Jesus with a blind thinking on Him, and the light springs 'privily by little and by little'. Do not turn back to the world; wait. The image of entering a dark house from the sun gives the time scale: stand still and sight returns gradually. Light is also called a grace he did not work for.
- techniques: concentration_nonvisual, devotion_or_transmission, sensory_restriction; phenomena: light_in_the_room, lightning_flashes, all_pervading_light
- conditions: setting: Withdrawal from noise and fleshly affections.; duration_and_schedule: Often assay to be at home in the murkness; for a short time, repeated.; prerequisites: Meekness, self-knowledge, purified conscience; amendment of life.; other: Constant prayer and thought of Jesus with desire.
- stages: night / lightsome darkness: Thought withdrawn from earthly things; painful at first, later restful, a 'rich nought'. -> small sudden lightings: Glimpses of Jerusalem through crannies; then fuller light of grace.
- with the light: Test it by its effect on the heart: accept if it increases prayer and humility, refuse if it draws away from prayer or breeds pride; do not seek bodily lights.
- obstacles/fixes: Night is painful at first for the unpractised: keep desiring, do not return to the world. | Light that comes with presumption and disdain is the midday fiend's: judge by pride, backbiting and unobedience.
- cautions: Bodily lights may be good or evil; test by the stirring of the heart. | The false sun shows only between two black rainy clouds, presumption and disdain.
- safety flags: tradition_warns_against_seeking
- interpretation: The true light is the love of Jesus felt through grace, ghostly knowing; God is light as truth, not bodily light.
- practice: Hilton gives a practice that leads towards light by dwelling in a murk. Forsake worldly love and withdraw thought and affection from creatures by a longing for the love of Jesus, holding a blind thinking on Him. This is the night: at first it may be painful and seem blind, as when one comes from the sun into a dark house, but if you stand still and keep praying and desiring, sight grows. Keep assaying to be at home there. Light will spring privily, first as small sudden lightings, later fuller. Do not turn back to the world if it is dark. Test any light, whether bodily or imaginal, by its effect: if it draws you from prayer or makes you proud, refuse it.
  - passage (caution; Book I ch. 11, source lines 3598-3639): If you see light with the bodily eye or in imagining, other than ordinary light, watch what it does to your heart before trusting it.
  - passage (onset; Book II ch. 24-25, source lines 13841-13919): Keep dwelling in the murkness and light will spring, not all at once but little by little; small sudden lightings are glimpsed first.
  - passage (caution; Book II ch. 26, source lines 13957-14215): False light shows only between the black clouds of presumption and disdain; the true sun appears when the firmament is clear.

## [chr-ignatius-autobiography] The Autobiography of St. Ignatius
Ignatius of Loyola (dictated to Luis Gonçalves da Câmara, 1553-1555); dictated 1553-1555; this edition 1900; practitioner_testimony; rights public_domain; access fulltext_saved; screen pass; keep; relevance 4, practicality 2, confidence medium (Digest only; anchors cross line breaks.)
- mode: ambiguous. The Manresa shape appeared 'in the courtyard before him' on moonlit nights and he 'gazed at it' (outward, perceptual-sounding); the white rays and the white body of Christ are seen 'with the eyes of his soul'; the meat dish 'he appeared to see it with his eyes'. Ignatius himself does not settle where the seeing happens.
- eyes: both; stimulus independence: yes
- ONSET development_only: No induction is taught. Visions arose during seven hours of daily prayer, scourging, fasting and vigils, and at night on retiring. A lamp-like illumination came while sitting gazing at a river with no image. The only use for a seer is discernment, not onset.
- techniques: fasting_or_austerity, sleep_threshold, devotion_or_transmission, attention_to_spontaneous_phenomena; phenomena: white, figures_or_forms, sun_disc, all_pervading_light, lamp_or_flame
- conditions: posture: Prayer on bended knees seven hours a day.; gaze_or_eye_position: Once sat and gazed at deep flowing water.; setting: Hospital courtyard in moonlight; cell; church at Mass.; duration_and_schedule: Months at Manresa; the white body seen perhaps twenty or forty times.; prerequisites: Severe penance, fasting, vigils.; other: Learned discernment of spirits from experience.
- stages: the beautiful shape: Indistinct, symmetrical, serpent-like with glittering 'eyes that were not eyes', gave consolation; later seen gleaming with many eyes. -> illumination at the river: No vision, but a lasting new light of understanding.
- with the light: Test by effect and colour: the shape lost its colour near the cross, so he judged it of the devil and dispelled it with his staff. The white body and rays he accepted and trusted.
- obstacles/fixes: Doubted whether night illuminations were from the good spirit: shortened vigil and gave more time to sleep.
- cautions: A consoling, beautiful vision can be the devil's; discern by its effects and by what it does near holy signs.
- safety flags: fasting, sleep_deprivation
- interpretation: Visions are grace or diabolic according to discernment of effects; the river illumination is light of understanding.
- practice: Ignatius gives testimony, not a method. At Manresa he prayed seven hours daily on his knees, fasted, scourged himself and kept vigils. On retiring, illuminations and consolations came, to the point that he cut sleep. In moonlight a beautiful indistinct shape appeared in the courtyard; the oftener he saw it the greater his joy. Later he saw white rays at the elevation of the Host and the humanity of Christ as a white body, many times, with the eyes of the soul. He treated them by discernment: he asked whether he was left joyful or sad, doubted vigils that took his sleep, and when the shape lost its colour near a cross, he judged it the devil's and drove it off with his staff. Seated by a river he received a deep illumination without any image.
  - passage (testimony; Ch. II 'Spiritual Trials', source lines 941-1021): A recurring beautiful indistinct shape on moonlit nights at the hospital gave increasing consolation and left sadness when it went.
  - passage (mode; Ch. III 'Heavenly Favors', source lines 1336-1458): White rays seen at the elevation, marked as seen with the eyes of the soul, as was the white body of Christ.
  - passage (caution; Ch. III, source lines 1336-1458): The shape lost colour near a cross, so he judged it diabolical and dispelled it with his staff.
  - passage (onset; Ch. III, source lines 1230-1329): At night on retiring after long prayer, illuminations came, so that he cut his sleep.

## [chr-john-of-the-cross-ascent] The Ascent of Mount Carmel
John of the Cross (1542-1591); written c. 1579-1585; this edition 1906; classical_text; rights public_domain; access fulltext_saved; screen pass; keep; relevance 3, practicality 2, confidence medium (Digest only; chapter XI not seen.)
- mode: ambiguous. John distinguishes visions through the bodily senses, imaginary visions and spiritual visions; the digest shows only the last class, compared to lightning in a dark night, plus the sun-ray-in-dust analogy.
- eyes: unspecified; stimulus independence: not_applicable
- ONSET unclear: None. The soul is told to empty itself, to remain in darkness and faith, and not to seek visions; light is God's gift. Pure light is unseen by the understanding and shows as darkness.
- techniques: concentration_nonvisual, devotion_or_transmission; phenomena: light_in_the_room, lightning_flashes, all_pervading_light
- conditions: prerequisites: Mortification of desire; detachment from forms and images.; other: Loving attentiveness to God without meditating on particulars.
- stages: spiritual visions: As if a door opened on a marvellous light, like lightning flashing in a dark night, then gone but the forms remain.
- with the light: Refuse it and take no pleasure in it; rely on faith in darkness.
- obstacles/fixes: Forms and figures of meditation block the pure light: let go of particular objects.
- cautions: The devil mimics visions by natural light; delight in them blinds the soul and leads it to take darkness for light. | The more pure the light the more it is dark to the understanding.
- safety flags: tradition_warns_against_seeking
- interpretation: The divine light is pure knowledge that appears as darkness to the understanding; visions are secondary.
- practice: John of the Cross teaches no technique for seeing light and warns against pursuing it. The soul is to be emptied of desires, images and clear perceptions, and to rest in loving attentiveness to God in the dark of faith. When meditation ceases, one attends lovingly without wishing to feel or see more; any particular forms interposed block the pure light, as a thing placed before the eyes. The pure divine light is not perceived by the understanding and may appear as darkness. Supernatural visions, when they come, are like lightning in a dark night; the devil can imitate them by a natural light. Therefore take no pleasure in them, esteem them little and walk by faith.
  - passage (mode; Book II ch. XXIV, source lines 10922-10987): Spiritual visions are like a door opening on marvellous light, as lightning in a dark night; the devil can mimic them with a natural light.
  - passage (interpretation; Book II ch. XIV, source lines 7509-7570): By the sun-ray analogy, pure light cannot be seen unless it falls on objects; so the purest divine light shows as darkness to the understanding.
  - passage (method; Book II ch. XV, source lines 7787-7828): Once meditation ceases, the soul only attends lovingly; particular forms interposed obstruct the pure light.
  - passage (caution; Book II ch. XXIV, source lines 13922-13962): Taking pleasure in apprehensions makes the soul blind and prey to the devil's gradual deception.

## [chr-seraphim-motovilov-conversation] Beseda prepodobnogo Serafima Sarovskogo s N. A. Motovilovym o tseli khristianskoi zhizni (Conversation on the Aim of the Christian Life)
N. A. Motovilov, recording Seraphim of Sarov; conversation Nov. 1831; first published 1903; brochure ed. 2011; practitioner_testimony; rights public_domain; access fulltext_saved; screen pass; keep; relevance 4, practicality 2, confidence high (Modern reprint of the 1903 text; read in Russian.)
- mode: perceptual. Seen with open eyes outdoors in daylight on an overcast day: the face 'brighter than the sun', light that 'pricks' and hurts the eyes, then light remaining around the face over a wide space; Motovilov contrasts it explicitly with the absent sun.
- eyes: open; stimulus independence: not_applicable
- ONSET unclear: Light came during conversation by the elder's prayer and touch (hands on the shoulders); Seraphim says Motovilov could see it only because he too was 'in the same grace-filled light'. It is presented as gift (благодать) acquired by the whole Christian life of prayer and alms, not as a technique; nothing for the person who sees nothing beyond 'acquire the Holy Spirit'. Effectively transmission/grace.
- techniques: devotion_or_transmission; phenomena: body_or_objects_luminous, lightning_flashes, all_pervading_light, white
- conditions: posture: seated on a tree stump / standing in a forest clearing; gaze_or_eye_position: looking at the elder's face; setting: outdoors, snowing, overcast November day; duration_and_schedule: one occasion; prerequisites: Seraphim's prayer; the 'acquisition of the Holy Spirit' through prayer; other: accompanied by warmth, fragrance, peace, sweetness, joy
- with the light: Use it as a sign of being 'in the Spirit of God': attend to the accompanying peace, joy, warmth and fragrance.
- safety flags: transmission_required_by_tradition
- interpretation: The visible grace of the Holy Spirit, the same light as at the Transfiguration, shared by both participants.
- practice: No method for producing light. Seraphim teaches that the aim of Christian life is acquiring the Holy Spirit, chiefly through prayer and good deeds done for Christ, and that one should constantly test whether one is 'in the Spirit'. The light came when he prayed for his disciple and laid hands on him: Motovilov, eyes open in daylight, saw the elder's face brighter than the sun and light filling the space around, and Seraphim told him he saw it because he was himself in that light. The sign-set to check is peace beyond words, sweetness, joy, warmth inside despite snow, and fragrance.
  - passage (testimony; source lines ~1063-1066): Motovilov cannot look Seraphim in the eyes because lightning shines from them and his own eyes ache; the elder is brighter than the sun.
  - passage (mode; source lines ~1068-1070): 'I know there is no sun and the day is overcast, but your face has become brighter than the sun and my eyes hurt; this light pricks them.'
  - passage (onset; source lines ~1074-1077): Seraphim: you too are in exactly the same grace-filled light, otherwise you could not see me so.
  - passage (interpretation; source lines ~1130-1134): The warmth felt in falling snow is not in the air but in us: the warmth of the Holy Spirit.

## [chr-symeon-chapters-philokalia] Practical and Theological Chapters (Philokalia selection)
Symeon the New Theologian; c. 1000; ed. 1893; classical_text; rights public_domain; access fulltext_saved; screen pass; keep_marginal; relevance 4, practicality 2, confidence medium (Only the light-dense chapters were read; first-person Discourses (Catecheses) not in this file.)
- mode: ambiguous. Light is 'seen' (ὁρᾶν) and overwhelms (he cannot bear to see it, falls prostrate), which suggests perceptual experience, but there is no statement of visual field or eyes.
- eyes: unspecified; stimulus independence: not_applicable
- ONSET development_only: The path to light is moral and affective: fear of judgement, accepted heart-pain, obedience to a spiritual father, abstinence; then tears flow ceaselessly; only 'for those being perfected' does the spring become light, 'suddenly'. No technique for the one who sees nothing, and repeated insistence that before mourning and tears no one should deceive himself.
- techniques: devotion_or_transmission, fasting_or_austerity; phenomena: all_pervading_light, lamp_or_flame
- stages: πόνος / δάκρυα: heart-pain from fear of judgement turns into joy and a spring of tears -> φῶς: for the perfected the spring suddenly becomes light; the person falls prostrate, burns, and finally 'becomes light'
- with the light: Not addressed as instruction; the light intensifies through continued tears.
- cautions: Before mourning and tears let no one deceive us with empty words, nor deceive ourselves.
- interpretation: The light of the Holy Spirit within, uniting the person to God.
- practice: Symeon gives no light technique. Accept and even tighten the 'bond' of fear and heart-pain under a spiritual father, eat and dress only for need, renounce self-will. This pain becomes joy and a constant spring of tears; in the advanced, the heart is suddenly changed and the spring becomes light, so overwhelming that one falls to the ground. Do not claim any of this before real mourning and tears.
  - passage (stages; Chapters, source lines ~767-772): For those being perfected this spring (of tears) becomes light, the heart being suddenly changed and transformed.
  - passage (testimony; Chapters, source lines ~770-780): Having within the light of the Spirit and unable to bear seeing it, he falls face down, cries out in amazement and great fear, as having seen and suffered something beyond nature, and is like a man whose entrails are set on fire.
- new leads: Symeon, 'Three Methods of Prayer' (Μέθοδος τῆς ἱερᾶς προσευχῆς καὶ προσοχῆς): present in printed Philokalia vol. 2 after Symeon's chapters but not located in this OCR

## [chr-teresa-interior-castle] The Interior Castle, or The Mansions
Teresa of Avila (1515-1582); written 1577; this edition 1921; teacher_manual; rights public_domain; access fulltext_saved; screen pass; keep; relevance 3, practicality 2, confidence medium (Digest only.)
- mode: ambiguous. She says the image of imaginary vision is 'seen by the interior sight alone', not bodily apparitions, of which she can say nothing. The seventh-mansion illumination is 'like a most dazzling cloud of light'.
- eyes: unspecified; stimulus independence: yes
- ONSET development_only: Nothing for someone who sees nothing; God gives or withholds. She advises against forcing the mind to stop thought, and warns against holding the breath, since effort excites the imagination.
- techniques: devotion_or_transmission, concentration_nonvisual; phenomena: lightning_flashes, all_pervading_light, figures_or_forms
- conditions: duration_and_schedule: Vision passes as quickly as a flash of lightning.; prerequisites: Advanced mansions of prayer.
- stages: imaginary vision: Christ's humanity in an infused light like the sun veiled by a diamond-clear veil; brief; usually leads to ecstasy. -> seventh mansion illumination: Preceded by an illumination like a dazzling cloud of light, the Trinity revealed.
- with the light: Do not desire it; take it as God's gift; serving God is better than visions.
- obstacles/fixes: Forced stilling of the mind or holding the breath makes imagination worse.
- cautions: Visions must never be desired; imagination can counterfeit them. | Painful effort such as holding the breath does more harm than good.
- safety flags: breath_retention, tradition_warns_against_seeking
- interpretation: Divine light is the Sun of Justice in the centre of the soul; visions are secondary favours.
- practice: The Castle offers no technique for producing light. The soul is a crystal with the sun at its centre; sin or distraction blocks it, as dust in the eyes blinds one in a bright room. In prayer one should not force the mind to stop thinking or restrain the breath, since effort stirs the imagination; one rests in God's hands and lets Him give light and knowledge when He chooses. Imaginary visions, when given, come without warning, last about as long as lightning, and are seen by interior sight in an infused light like the sun under a veil of diamond. Never desire them. Test them by their fruits, and distinguish them from the imagination of the weak-minded.
  - passage (mode; Sixth Mansions ch. IX, source lines 9349-9407): The vision image is seen by interior sight alone; she cannot speak of bodily apparitions.
  - passage (stages; Sixth Mansions ch. IX, source lines 9349-9407): The vision is brief as lightning and cannot be gazed on longer than the sun.
  - passage (caution; Fourth Mansions ch. III, source lines 4166-4203): Painful effort such as holding the breath harms inward prayer; abandon the soul to God.
  - passage (testimony; Seventh Mansions ch. I, source lines 10448-10507): In the seventh mansion the Trinity is revealed after an illumination like a dazzling cloud of light.

## [chr-teresa-life] The Life of St. Teresa of Jesus, of the Order of Our Lady of Carmel, Written by Herself
Teresa of Avila (1515-1582); written 1562-1565; this edition 1904; practitioner_testimony; rights public_domain; access fulltext_saved; screen pass; keep; relevance 4, practicality 2, confidence medium (Digest only.)
- mode: ambiguous. She says the imaginary vision is not seen with bodily eyes but with the eyes of the soul; yet it is a light 'before our eyes', gives delight 'to the eyes', and 'whether our eyes be open or shut, it makes no difference'. In the intellectual vision she sees a 'light not seen'. Another account says 'I saw a light within me'.
- eyes: both; stimulus independence: yes
- ONSET development_only: Explicitly no induction: the light cannot be attained 'by any diligence or efforts of our own'; it comes instantaneously and when the Lord wills. For the person who sees nothing, the only advice is humility and not seeking; the light is withheld or given.
- techniques: devotion_or_transmission, attention_to_spontaneous_phenomena; phenomena: white, colour_fields, figures_or_forms, all_pervading_light
- conditions: gaze_or_eye_position: Eyes open or shut makes no difference.; setting: Often after Communion or in prayer.; duration_and_schedule: Brief, instantaneous; once more than an hour.; prerequisites: Prayer of union and quiet; humility.; other: Confessors tested the visions.
- stages: intellectual vision: Presence of Christ at her side; nothing seen by body or soul, yet a 'light not seen' illumines understanding. -> imaginary vision: Christ's glorified form seen in a delicate whiteness and infused brilliancy, beside which the sun is dim.
- with the light: Neither seek nor resist; test by fruits (humility, peace, no pride); submit to confessors. She was told to scorn the vision and did, yet it persisted.
- obstacles/fixes: Doubt whether it is fancy: she could not picture such beauty by years of effort, which showed it was not imagination. | Fear of the devil: judge by the fruits left in the soul.
- cautions: Visions are dangerous trials, not proofs of sanctity; Satan transforms into an angel of light. | The will of the Lord is that the soul be in darkness and not see this light.
- safety flags: tradition_warns_against_seeking, transmission_required_by_tradition
- interpretation: A gift of God showing Christ's humanity; its light is natural and never-night, unlike any earthly light.
- practice: Teresa gives no method; she says this light cannot be reached by one's own diligence. In prayer of quiet and union the Lord sometimes shows Christ's glorified form in an instantaneous, delicate whiteness and infused brilliancy that makes the sun look dim. Eyes open or shut make no difference, and she cannot resist it or add to it. First comes the intellectual vision, a felt presence with a light not seen. The practitioner's part is humility, not desiring visions, submitting them to a learned confessor, and judging them by their fruits: peace, humility and love show them true. Efforts to imagine such beauty fail, which is one test that it is not fancy.
  - passage (onset; Ch. XXVIII 8, source lines 13124-13180): The light of the imaginary vision cannot be reached by any effort; the Lord imposes it when He wills.
  - passage (mode; Ch. XXVIII 7-8, source lines 13124-13180): The light is seen equally with eyes open or shut, instantaneously.
  - passage (stages; Ch. XXVII 5, source lines 12535-12591): In the intellectual vision no sun or brightness is seen, yet a light not seen illumines the understanding.
  - passage (testimony; Ch. XXXVIII, source lines 18420-18456): Compared with vision light, the sun looks loathsome; imagination cannot conceive it.

## [chr-xanthopoulos-century] Method and exact rule for those who choose the hesychast life (Century in 100 chapters)
Kallistos and Ignatios Xanthopoulos; late 14th c.; ed. 1893; classical_text; rights public_domain; access fulltext_saved; screen pass; keep; relevance 5, practicality 4, confidence high (Read in Greek; OCR noisy but sense clear.)
- mode: ambiguous. 'The light of the mind' is said to be 'seen' (ὁρᾷν), and false light is described in sensory terms (fiery, smoky, like sensible fire), so a seen light is in view; but the text never says eyes open/closed and the divine light is defined by absence of all images.
- eyes: unspecified; stimulus independence: not_applicable
- ONSET development_only: The light is a by-product of long, unbroken practice of the Name in the depth of the heart ('they can at times see the light of the mind'); the practitioner must not seek God's appearance. No steps for someone who sees nothing; the breath method aims at prayer, not at light. Discernment requires a teacher who has himself experienced illumination.
- techniques: breath_control, sound_or_mantra, posture_or_body_lock, attention_to_spontaneous_phenomena; phenomena: sapphire_or_sky_blue, blue, lamp_or_flame, smoke_mist_cloud, all_pervading_light
- conditions: posture: seated, with breath led in through the nose; gaze_or_eye_position: not specified in passages read; setting: cell, stillness; duration_and_schedule: continual, over long periods; prerequisites: teacher, obedience, purity
- with the light: If light appears unsought: neither accept nor reject; flee to God in hope; ask an illumined, undeluded teacher; if none, better not to accept it and to count oneself unworthy.
- obstacles/fixes: Turning aside into imaginings: keep the bare words of the prayer constantly in mind.
- cautions: Do not seek God's manifestation, lest you receive darkness pretending to be light. | Light of the opposing power is fiery and smoky, like sensible fire; a purified soul finds it distasteful. | Pray formlessly or you become a fantasist instead of a hesychast.
- safety flags: breath_retention, tradition_warns_against_seeking, transmission_required_by_tradition
- interpretation: The mind's own light, seen when purified, is a work of grace; Evagrius's sapphire 'state of the mind' is cited as the place of God.
- practice: Sit and pray 'Lord Jesus' constantly in the depth of the heart, with the breath drawn gently through the nostrils into the heart, holding to the bare words so the mind does not wander into images. Do not look for any appearance of God. Those who persist in this can at times see 'the light of the mind'. If light comes unsought, neither embrace it nor curse it: entrust it to God and consult a teacher who has himself been illumined. As a test, the false light is fiery and smoky, like ordinary fire, and leaves distaste; the true is pure and fills the soul with joy, cheerfulness and gentleness. Without a teacher, decline it humbly.
  - passage (onset; chapter on the light of the mind, source lines ~4405-4412): Those who meditate unceasingly on the glorious Name in the depth of the heart can at times see the light of the mind.
  - passage (caution; 'Περὶ ἐνεργείας θείας, καὶ ἐναντίας', source lines ~5170-5185): When the mind sees light without seeking it, let him neither accept it nor annul it (citing Mark the Ascetic), but flee to God and ask one who has grace to teach and discern.
  - passage (development; 'Περὶ φωτισμοῦ ἀληθινοῦ, καὶ ψευδοῦς', source lines ~5210-5232): Paul of Latros: the light of the opposing power is fiery and smoky like sensible fire and a purified soul loathes it; the light of the good is most gracious and pure, sanctifies, fills the soul with light, joy and cheerfulness, and makes it gentle.
  - passage (mode; catena on formless prayer, source lines ~5470-5495): Citing 'Neilos' (Evagrius): whoever wishes to see the state of the mind should strip himself of all thoughts and will then see it like sapphire or sky colour; Isaac: the mind clothed in grace sees its own purity like the heavenly colour; pray purely, without images, or become a fantasist.


# STREAM: Western esoteric, theurgic and occult (western-esoteric)

## [wes-ae-candle-of-vision] The Candle of Vision
AE (George William Russell); 1918; practitioner_testimony; rights public_domain; access fulltext_saved; screen pass; keep; relevance 5, practicality 3, confidence medium (Read in digest only; the concentration object and any breath or posture detail were not visible.)
- mode: perceptual. Russell contrasts the ordinary cloudy gloom behind closed lids with a luminous field, says the pictures have motion, light and colour 'perfect as things seen passing before the physical eyes', and spends the Analytic chapters arguing they are seen, not remembered or composed. He also admits some experiences were at the 'inner eyes' in the brain, so the boundary with imaginal seeing is not sharp.
- eyes: closed; stimulus independence: not_applicable
- ONSET both: To the person who sees nothing: sustained meditation and focusing the will 'to a burning-point'. First comes stupor and blankness (seeing, hearing and imagining nothing), which he says clears after weeks or months of persistence, and then the brain grows luminous. He says he knows others with no natural vision who acquired it this way, and that the highest ecstasy is conditioned by law and attainable by all. Prerequisite: unwavering daily concentration and aspiration; no grace or transmission claim in the digest.
- techniques: concentration_nonvisual, posture_or_body_lock, attention_to_spontaneous_phenomena, energy_work; phenomena: lamp_or_flame, colour_fields, all_pervading_light, figures_or_forms, gold_yellow, body_or_objects_luminous, sparks_or_points, sphere_pearl_bindu
- conditions: posture: often lying on a hillside or sand dunes; sitting also implied; gaze_or_eye_position: eyes of the body shut; setting: alone, outdoors in nature or in quiet meditation; duration_and_schedule: weeks or months of persistence before the stupor lifts; prerequisites: intense will and aspiration; he had some natural imagination but says others without it succeeded; other: visions often arrived in meditation on a theme, or while passive on the earth
- stages: stupor: On reversing habitual outward motions, nothing is seen, heard or imagined; the meditation seems barren. -> luminous brain: After weeks or months the dark caverns of the brain grow luminous; the luminous quality became normal and at times an almost intolerable lustre broke in. -> visions: Shining faces, processions, landscapes, self-shining valleys, light-filled figures; at times a flash of light opens like a gate on a figure. -> awakened fire: At the apex of intense meditation fire ran up the spine, with pulsations as of wings about the head and a cymbal-like sound.
- with the light: Let it deepen as meditation proceeds; he cautions against being lured from the highest meditation by the beauty of the forms, and he stayed contemplative rather than drive the awakened fire.
- obstacles/fixes: Initial stupor and blankness: persist through weeks or months | Being lured by the mirage of forms away from the highest meditation | Recoil into darkness and misery after vision
- cautions: Danger of 'misdirection' of the awakened spinal fire; he stayed contemplative in half terror | Meditating on new symbolic associations would encourage madness if they do not become intellectually coherent | Chasing forms tempts one from the highest meditation
- interpretation: Russell takes the light to be a self-created luminosity of the subtle aethers transmuted by will and aspiration, a new vesture for the soul that opens onto real planes of the Earth-spirit and the Many-Coloured Land.
- practice: Sit or lie alone with the eyes shut and hold the mind on a single chosen object of meditation, aspiring toward the Self, without wavering and as a daily discipline. Expect the first weeks or months to bring only a stupor in which nothing is seen or imagined. Persist. The gloom behind the lids begins to glow, first as a general luminousness and later as bursts of light, figures and landscapes. Do not follow the pretty forms away from the meditation itself. Passive receptivity in quiet places (hillside, shore, pool) also lets coloured scenes appear. If strong fire rises along the spine, do not push it; stay contemplative. Russell says others with no natural vision acquired it by sustained meditation and by focusing the will to a burning-point. The digest does not show the exact object he recommends.
  - passage (onset; Ch. Meditation, source lines 1096-1158): After weeks or months of persistence the initial stupor passes and the faculties readjust; the dark caverns of the brain then begin to grow luminous.
  - passage (mode; Ch. The Many-Coloured Land, source lines 1220-1284): Ordinary closed-eye experience is cloudy gloom; the meditation produced a luminousness that became normal and sometimes an almost intolerable lustre.
  - passage (onset; Ch. The Mingling of Natures, source lines 1826-1866): He knows others without natural vision who gained it by sustained meditation and concentrated will.
  - passage (testimony; Ch. The Many-Coloured Land, source lines 1443-1539): On the dunes, after seeing nothing for a time, a flash of light widened like a gate and showed a figure streaming light.
  - passage (caution; Ch. on the fires, source lines 4708-4746): When the spinal fire rose he feared misdirecting the energy and stayed contemplative.
- new leads: Russell's own 'Meditation' chapter text for the exact object of concentration

## [wes-demotic-magical-papyrus] The Demotic Magical Papyrus of London and Leiden, vol. I (translation): lamp and vessel divinations
Anonymous (Egyptian demotic handbook, 3rd century CE); papyrus c. 3rd century CE; translation 1904; classical_text; rights public_domain; access fulltext_saved; screen pass; keep; relevance 4, practicality 3, confidence medium (Read in digest; the translation is fragmentary and the ointment recipes are of doubtful identification.)
- mode: perceptual. The operator or boy opens his eyes toward a lit lamp or oil vessel and is asked 'Do you see the light?'; the light and then the god or its shadow are reported as seen about the lamp. This is lamp-gazing, so the seen light is tied to a physical flame; whether it persists apart from it is not stated.
- eyes: both; stimulus independence: no
- ONSET induction: For a medium who sees no light: close the eyes again, recite 'O darkness, remove thyself... bring the light in to me', or have the boy himself address the lamp: 'Grow, O light, come forth'. Invocations are repeated seven or nine times 'until the light appear'. Eye ointments are prescribed for solo work. Prerequisites: ritual purity, a clean dark place, a new lamp with clean wick and oil. No claim of grace; failure is blamed on impurity or obduracy of the god.
- techniques: light_source_gazing, sound_or_mantra, darkness_exposure, devotion_or_transmission, other; phenomena: lamp_or_flame, figures_or_forms, light_in_the_room
- conditions: posture: boy seated on a new brick or standing; operator lies on a rush or reed mat without speaking afterward; gaze_or_eye_position: eyes closed during recitation, then opened toward the lamp or oil; setting: clean dark cell or recess, entrance often east or south; midday in a place without light for one rite; duration_and_schedule: invocation seven or nine times; moon rite on the 15th; ointment preparation takes about sixty days; prerequisites: purity (no intercourse), natron-purified place, new white lamp free of red colouring, clean linen wick, genuine oil; other: operator's finger laid on or striking the boy's head while the spell is spoken 'down into his head'; incense burned
- stages: the light appears: After the eyes are opened the seer says he sees the light in the flame; the operator cries out and proceeds. -> the god or his shadow: Then the god, or the shadow of the god, is seen about or behind the lamp, and answers questions.
- with the light: Invoke it to grow and come forth, then question the god seen in it, and dismiss the god afterward.
- obstacles/fixes: Light does not appear: close eyes, recite the darkness-removal formula, or make the seer speak to the lamp himself | God goes away and the boy ceases to see: recite a restoring invocation so he is not frightened | Lack of purity makes the rite fail
- cautions: Purity is the chief matter; without it the rite does not succeed | The seer is to be protected from fright
- interpretation: The light is the arrival of the invoked god, whose shadow or form then appears about the lamp.
- practice: Go to a clean dark recess, purified with natron water, free of other light. Set a new white lamp, clean linen wick and genuine oil on a new brick. The seer sits with eyes closed while the operator recites the invocation seven times down into his head, with incense. The seer then opens his eyes toward the lamp and is asked whether he sees the light. If not, close the eyes again and repeat the call for the darkness to depart and the light to come in; the seer may also speak to the lamp: grow, come forth, rise. Once the light is seen, the god or his shadow is seen about the lamp and questioned. Solo operators anoint the eyes with a prepared ointment, recite with eyes shut, open them, and lie down silent afterward.
  - passage (onset; Col. XVII, source lines 6464-6521): If the light fails to come, the boy addresses the lamp himself with a formula bidding the light grow, rise and come forth.
  - passage (method; Col. VI, source lines 2960-2998): Eyes closed during recitation, then opened toward the lamp; the shadow of the god is seen about it.
  - passage (method; Col. VI, source lines 2960-2998): The rite is done at midday in a place without other light.
  - passage (method; Col. V, source lines 2620-2660): Setup: dark clean recess, purified with natron-water, new lamp, frankincense.
  - passage (testimony; Col. V, source lines 2843-2883): The solo operator who anoints his eyes with the prepared ointment and recites over the lamp sees a figure of a god.
- new leads: Greek Magical Papyri lamp rites (PGM VII) as parallel

## [wes-goodrich-freer-essays] Essays in Psychical Research, by Miss X: 'On the Faculty of Crystal-Gazing' (with related essays)
Ada Goodrich-Freer ('Miss X'); 1899; practitioner_testimony; rights public_domain; access fulltext_saved; screen pass; keep_marginal; relevance 2, practicality 3, confidence medium (Digest only; no seen-light content, kept marginally for the visualisation-to-perception crossing.)
- mode: imaginal_crossing. She treats crystal pictures as externalised mental imagery: 'we shall find nothing in the crystal but what we ourselves put there', and every gazer she met was a strong visualiser. Her graduated exercises move from closed-eye visualisation to seeing the same picture in the crystal, which is a crossing of image into apparent outer space. No seen light as such is reported.
- eyes: both; stimulus independence: unclear
- ONSET induction: Gives a graduated course for someone who sees nothing in the crystal: look at a room object, shut the eyes and visualise it, then try to see it in the crystal, expecting success after half a dozen tries if one has any visualising gift. She says that for non-visualisers the attempt is a waste of time. No light-specific onset.
- techniques: scrying_surface, visualization_crossing; phenomena: figures_or_forms
- conditions: gaze_or_eye_position: quiet gaze into a polished surface; setting: shady corner, guard against reflections (per search-agent summary, not confirmed in digest); duration_and_schedule: repeat exercises again and again with increasing complexity; prerequisites: strong visualising ability; other: a flawless clean crystal if one wants to exclude reflection-seeded pictures
- stages: graduated exercises (a) to (e): Visualise a room object with eyes shut then see it in the crystal; a recent scene; a described scene; an imagined scene; finally look to the crystal directly without prior visualisation.
- with the light: Not about light; the picture is examined for memory, fancy or possible telepathic content.
- obstacles/fixes: Fatigue or preoccupation yield only fancy and memory pictures: put the crystal away | Reflections can seed the picture: use a clean flawless crystal if that matters
- cautions: Most crystal pictures are memory or fancy rather than psychical | Nonsense and commonplace pictures are as likely as significant ones
- interpretation: The crystal is a convenient means of externalising images already in the mind, including unconsciously acquired knowledge.
- practice: Take a clean crystal or polished surface and guard against reflections. First train visualisation: look carefully at part of the room, shut your eyes and visualise it, then try to see it in the crystal. Move through a recently witnessed scene, a described scene and an imagined scene, increasing detail each time. When these work, look straight into the crystal without prior visualisation. Expect it to come easily after about half a dozen tries if you visualise well. If you do not make mental pictures of what you remember or read, she judges the attempt a waste of time. Put the crystal away when tired.
  - passage (method; Essay III, exercise (a), source lines 6743-6826): First visualise a room object with closed eyes, then try to see it in the crystal.
  - passage (onset; Essay III, source lines 5353-5389): Those who do not habitually picture what they think of will, in her experience, waste their time with the crystal.
  - passage (mode; Essay III, source lines 6357-6395): She attaches no virtue to the crystal and treats it as a means of externalising images already in the mind.

## [wes-iamblichus-de-mysteriis] On the Mysteries (De Mysteriis), Book III ch. 14 on photagogia, with Book II chs. 3-9 on the lights of divine epiphanies
Iamblichus; c. 300 CE; translations 1821 and 1911; classical_text; rights public_domain; access fulltext_saved; screen pass; keep; relevance 4, practicality 2, confidence medium (Digest only; theological, with no operational instruction for the seer.)
- mode: ambiguous. Iamblichus says the divine light shines on the luminous vehicle of the soul and moves the faculty of imagination (phantasia), while attention and reason stay conscious. The light is thus received in the imaginal faculty yet also named as light brought through sun, moon, air, water or a wall. Book II separately grades luminosity and fire seen at epiphanies as visible appearances.
- eyes: open; stimulus independence: unclear
- ONSET unclear: No technique for breaking through is given. Methods listed (darkness, sun or moon light, open air, water, a prepared wall, chants) are only vehicles; the light comes from the will of the gods, who shine through whatever instruments they choose. The Chaldean Oracles quoted by Wilder say one should not invoke the vision before the body is perfected or initiated.
- techniques: darkness_exposure, light_source_gazing, sky_or_space_gazing, scrying_surface, sound_or_mantra, devotion_or_transmission; phenomena: lamp_or_flame, all_pervading_light, figures_or_forms, lightning_flashes
- conditions: setting: darkness, open air, sun or moon light, water, or a wall prepared with sacred engraved figures; prerequisites: the gods' will; priestly preparation; initiation per the Oracles; other: the light is fixed in a solid place so it is not widely diffused
- stages: grades of light at epiphanies: Images of gods are replete with fulgid light, archangels supernaturally luminous, angels luminous, demons a turbid or smouldering fire, souls partial gleams; the fire of the gods is stable, that of angels in motion, that of demons unstable.
- with the light: Receive it; the seer stays attentive and awake. It is not to be produced by technique.
- cautions: Chaldean Oracles: do not invoke the Autoptic Image of Being before the body is perfected or initiated | The light is from the gods, not the operator's art
- safety flags: transmission_required_by_tradition
- interpretation: A single faculty, the 'bringing of light' (photagogia): divine light illuminating the soul's aether-like vehicle and moving the imagination, whatever the outward medium.
- practice: Iamblichus describes rather than prescribes. Diviners who stay conscious are lit from the gods by one faculty, the bringing of light. The outward means are darkness as an auxiliary, the light of sun or moon, the open air, chants prepared for the god's coming, conducting light through water, or letting it shine on a wall prepared with sacred engraved figures and fixed in a solid place so it does not diffuse. Attention and reason remain aware while the fancy is moved by the gods. The light is external, undivided and subject only to the gods' will, so none of these means compels it. A Damascius note describes a woman who poured pure water into a glass cup and saw luminous appearances of future events in it.
  - passage (mode; Taylor, Book III ch. 14, source lines 6289-6401): The seer's attention and reasoning remain conscious because the divine light does not touch them; only the imaginative faculty is divinely moved.
  - passage (method; Taylor, Book III ch. 14, source lines 6289-6401): Light-bringers use darkness as an auxiliary and employ sun, moon or open air.
  - passage (method; Taylor, Book III ch. 14, source lines 6289-6401): Light is made to shine on a wall prepared with sacred characters and fixed in a solid place so it is not widely diffused.
  - passage (onset; Taylor, Book III ch. 14, source lines 6289-6401): The illumination comes from outside and is subject only to the will of the gods, however it is brought.
  - passage (testimony; Taylor, Book III ch. 14 footnote, source lines 6289-6401): Damascius reports a gifted woman who saw luminous appearances in a cup of water.

## [wes-mead-corpus-hermeticum] Corpus Hermeticum I (Poemandres), X (The Key), XIII (Secret Sermon on the Mountain), in Thrice-Greatest Hermes vol. II
Hermetic authors; trans. G. R. S. Mead; 2nd-3rd c. CE; trans. 1906; classical_text; rights public_domain; access fulltext_saved; screen pass; keep_marginal; relevance 3, practicality 2, confidence medium (Read in Mead's English, not the Greek; Mead's brackets and interpretive glosses are his.)
- mode: ambiguous. CH I: 'I see a Vision limitless, all things turned into Light' in a sleep-like suspension of bodily senses. CH X explicitly calls it 'the radiance that the mind alone can see', seen with 'my mind's eye', and contrasts it with sunlight on the physical eyes; so it is framed as noetic seeing, not visual-field light.
- eyes: closed; stimulus independence: not_applicable
- ONSET unclear: The only practical directions are sense-withdrawal: meditating until the body's senses are held back (CH I), stilling every sense and motion (CH X), 'withdraw into thyself... will, and it comes to pass; throw out of work the body's senses' (CH XIII). But CH XIII also says 'the way to do this is not taught' and rebirth comes to one 'taken pity on by God'; CH X says 'we are not yet strung to the Vision'. Mixed: will and withdrawal plus grace.
- techniques: sensory_restriction, concentration_nonvisual, sleep_threshold; phenomena: all_pervading_light
- conditions: gaze_or_eye_position: eyes closed ('closing of the eyes, true vision'); prerequisites: purging the twelve 'tormentors' (vices); other: sleep-like suspension of bodily senses
- with the light: Gaze in silence; the light draws the soul out of the body and transforms it.
- cautions: We are not yet strong enough for the vision (CH X).
- interpretation: The light is Mind (Nous), God; the soul is of Light and Life.
- practice: The treatises describe rather than prescribe. Meditate on 'the things that are' until the body's senses fall still as in heavy sleep; still every sense and motion and keep silence, for 'gnosis of the Good is holy silence and a giving holiday to every sense'. Withdraw into yourself and will it. What is promised is a limitless light seen by the mind, not by the eyes, which does not dazzle but increases seeing, shines round the mind and draws the soul out of the body. The texts also say the way 'is not taught' and depends on divine mercy.
  - passage (testimony; CH I.1-4, Mead pp. 3-4, source lines ~315-358): While meditating, with thought raised high and bodily senses held back as in heavy sleep, Hermes is addressed by Poemandres and then sees a limitless vision in which all things became sweet, joyous light.
  - passage (mode; CH X.4, Mead pp. 143-144, source lines ~6090-6097): The vision of the Good does not blaze on the eyes like sunlight and make them close; it increases the eye's seeing, the radiance that the mind alone can see.
  - passage (method; CH X.5-6, Mead pp. 144-145, source lines ~6114-6140): Gnosis of the Good is holy silence and a holiday to every sense; the gazer perceives nothing else, stills every sense and motion, and the light shines round his mind and through his soul, drawing it out of the body.
  - passage (onset; CH XIII.7, Mead p. 225, source lines ~9593-9597): To Tat's fear he is incapable: withdraw into yourself and it will come; will, and it happens; put the body's senses out of work and your divinity will be born.

## [wes-mead-mithriac-ritual] A Mithriac Ritual (Echoes from the Gnosis, vol. VI): translation of the 'Mithras Liturgy', Paris Magical Papyrus (PGM IV. 475-829)
Anonymous (Greek magical papyrus, 4th century CE copy); ed. and trans. G. R. S. Mead after Dieterich; papyrus c. 4th century CE; translation 1907; classical_text; rights public_domain; access fulltext_saved; screen pass; keep; relevance 3, practicality 2, confidence medium (Digest only; a ritual script with no accounts of how the visions arise in practice.)
- mode: ambiguous. The text reports the operator 'seeing' disk, stars, doors of fire, lightnings and a fiery god in the air, with the eyes open or shut at different stages. Mead's commentary reads these as projections of the operator's own soul-powers ('appearances will differ according to the mind-content'), 'visions... presented to the inner eye', so the boundary between perceptual and imaginal is left open.
- eyes: both; stimulus independence: unclear
- ONSET development_only: The rite is explicitly for those already prepared: the Father hands it to a worthy initiate son. Mead says it is 'intended only for those who have already made themselves ready'. No instruction for someone who sees nothing, apart from the breath step; the first result (feeling light, rising in the air) is predicted, not troubleshot. Transmission from Father to Son is stated as a condition.
- techniques: breath_control, light_source_gazing, sound_or_mantra, devotion_or_transmission, posture_or_body_lock; phenomena: sun_disc, stars, lightning_flashes, figures_or_forms, lamp_or_flame, gold_yellow
- conditions: posture: body reduced to absolute quietude before the breathing (Mead); gaze_or_eye_position: gaze at the sun's rays; later eyes closed for one utterance and then opened; 'gaze intently' in the air at the god; setting: sunlight, in the open air; prerequisites: initiation; purification; worthy to receive the craft from a Father; other: silence gesture (finger on lips), hissing and puffing sounds, a prolonged 'bellowing' with belly pressure to start the senses going
- stages: ascent: After three deep breaths from the sun's rays the operator seems lifted into mid-air and sees the gods ascending and descending through the sun's disk. -> disk expands: After the second utterance, a mighty host of five-pointed stars emerges from the disk and fills the air; then doors of fire fast closed. -> the god: A youthful god with locks of flame, white tunic, scarlet mantle and crown of fire; later lightnings leap from his eyes and stars from his body.
- with the light: Stop hostile or blurred appearances with the Silence utterance; gaze intently at the god; salute and address him.
- obstacles/fixes: Gods gazing sternly and bearing down: finger on lips, 'Silence', hiss and puff, and they turn benign | Operator confused by the panorama: another utterance of Silence | Senses not started: bellow with belly-pressure
- cautions: Mead: the first visions are so overwhelming that the appearances are blurred and seem hostile | The rite is restricted to the prepared and initiated
- safety flags: breath_retention, transmission_required_by_tradition
- interpretation: A theurgic ascent: the sun's light and breath are used to lift the operator to see the Lord of the cosmos. Mead reads the ascending and descending gods as the man's own soul-powers.
- practice: In sunlight, after purification and with the body still, draw breath from the sun's rays by inhaling three times as deeply as possible. The text promises a sense of rising into the air and sight of the gods moving through the sun's disk and a pipe hanging from it. If the gods seem hostile, lay a finger on the lips, say Silence, hiss and puff. Repeat with the second utterance and the disk expands, releasing five-pointed stars; doors of fire appear. Close the eyes for the long invocation, open them, and gaze intently into the air for lightnings and the god, finishing with a bellow to bring the senses into play. It is given only to a prepared initiate.
  - passage (method; The Ritual, III, source lines 458-613): Draw breath from the sun's rays, inhaling three times as deeply as possible, and the ascent is seen.
  - passage (stages; The Ritual, VI, source lines 618-720): After the second utterance a host of five-pointed stars comes out of the disk and fills the air.
  - passage (onset; Comments, III, source lines 1331-1522): Mead: the rite presupposes prior preparation; it is not for a beginner who sees nothing.
  - passage (caution; Comments, IV, source lines 1606-1663): The first visions shake the operator so that the appearances blur and seem hostile; Silence calms them.
  - passage (testimony; Ritual, source lines 895-952): Gazing intently in the air, the operator is to see lightning flashes, trembling lights and a descending god.

## [wes-melville-crystal-gazing] Crystal-Gazing and the Wonders of Clairvoyance, embracing Practical Instructions in the Art, History, and Philosophy of this Ancient Science
John Melville; 1896; new and revised edition 1910; teacher_manual; rights public_domain; access fulltext_saved; screen pass; keep; relevance 4, practicality 5, confidence high
- mode: ambiguous. The sequence he describes is perceptual in form: the crystal itself looks hazy, clouded, then 'exceeding bright', with pin points of light like stars, and then a blue ocean of space on which the vision appears. He also speaks of the 'interior vision, or eye of the soul' and says some pictures are mere visualisation, so the mode is not settled.
- eyes: open; stimulus independence: no
- ONSET both: For one who sees nothing: sit calmly with a steady gaze for ten minutes at the first sitting, fifteen at the second, same place, position and hour. Nothing is usually seen on the first occasion and possibly not for several sittings; perseverance, calm and patience are the 'royal road'. Time scales: ten to fifteen minutes per sitting, sometimes one or two hours before a result. Once haze and pin-points appear the instruction is to persevere with confidence.
- techniques: scrying_surface, gazing_fixed_point, darkness_exposure, concentration_visual_object, fasting_or_austerity, breath_control; phenomena: sparks_or_points, stars, smoke_mist_cloud, white, blue, black_light, light_in_the_room
- conditions: posture: sitting comfortably; gaze_or_eye_position: eyes fixed on the crystal with a steady calm gaze, not a fierce stare; look lengthwise through it for distant events; setting: a room not dark but shadowed, like a cloudy or wet day, about 60-65 F; crystal on a black velvet cushion, partly wrapped with black silk to cut reflections; no direct light on it; duration_and_schedule: 10 minutes first sitting, 15 the second, then longer; same place and time; best at sunrise, midday, sunset; not soon after a meal; prerequisites: calm, patience, light diet; fasting 24 hours is said to help; cleanliness; other: quiet company seated at a distance, low voices if questions are asked
- stages: hazy or dull: The crystal looks hazy or dull: a sign that you are likely to see. -> pin points: Dull or cloudy with small pin points of light glittering like tiny stars; the crystal may alternately appear and disappear as in a mist. -> clouded or black: Immediately before the apparition the crystal clouds or darkens ('black'). -> bright: It then becomes exceeding bright, as if lit by an effulgence from its interior. -> blue ocean of space: Blindness to all else but a blue or bluish ocean of space, against which the vision is clearly apparent.
- with the light: Keep gazing and persevere with confidence through the hazy and star-speckled stages; the vision appears against the blue.
- obstacles/fixes: Nothing seen at first: repeat sittings with the same time and place; do not despair | Reflections: screen with black silk, keep light off the face of the crystal | Impatience, anxiety, ill-health and heavy diet hinder
- cautions: Ceremonial evocation of spirits is dangerous and only for trained votaries | If the seer's purpose is evil it reacts upon him sooner or later | Direct sunlight is said to ruin the crystal's susceptibility
- safety flags: fasting
- interpretation: Melville attributes the brightness to magnetism and iron in the crystal plus an efflux of magnetism from the gazer's eyes (citing Reichenbach), and he separates mere visualisation from true clairvoyant crystal vision.
- practice: Choose a shadowed, quiet room, not dark, comfortably warm. Set a crystal on a black velvet cushion, partly screened with black silk so that no light or reflection falls on it. Sit comfortably and gaze steadily, without a fierce stare, for ten minutes at the first sitting, fifteen at the second, at the same time and place. Expect nothing at first and possibly for several sittings. The signs of progress are haze or dullness in the crystal, pin points of light like tiny stars, a mist that comes and goes, a clouding or blackening, then a brightness as if lit from within, and finally a blue ocean of space on which the vision appears. Keep calm, eat lightly, avoid sittings after meals, and persevere.
  - passage (stages; Hints on the Use of the Crystal, rule 3, source lines 914-954 area (lines 515-522 of file)): After clouding or blackening, the crystal clears and becomes exceedingly bright, as if from an inner effulgence.
  - passage (stages; Summary of Practical Directions, rule 5, source lines 1556-1594): Dull or cloudy with pin points of light like tiny stars is the sign that crystalline vision is beginning; persevere.
  - passage (development; Summary of Practical Directions, rule 5, source lines 1556-1594): The hazy phase gives way suddenly to a blue ocean of space as the background of the vision.
