## [shm-boas-eskimo-baffin-land-hudson-bay] The Eskimo of Baffin Land and Hudson Bay: from notes collected by Capt. George Comer, Capt. James S. Mutch, and Rev. E. J. Peck (Bulletin of the American Museum of Natural History, vol. XV, part I)
Franz Boas (compiling field notes of George Comer, James S. Mutch and E. J. Peck from Cumberland Sound and west Hudson Bay informants); 1901; ethnographic_report; rights public_domain; access fulltext_saved; screen pass; keep; relevance 3, practicality 1, confidence medium (Read in digest; short testimony passage only, onset not confirmed.)
- mode: ambiguous. The angakok's light is said to cover the body and to make him see supernatural things, with a film removed from the eyes when its intensity rises; whether it is a visible light, a felt state or a metaphor for vision is not stated. Boas's informants say the light 'covers his body' and is 'always present', but do not describe what is seen.
- eyes: unspecified; stimulus independence: not_applicable
- ONSET unclear: The digest passage gives no way to acquire the light for a person who has none; it says only that a light covers the body when one becomes an angakok, and that at death it passes into another person. The search agent's summary mentions transmission by an old angakok's laying on of hands or an ordeal of worms, but that passage is not in the digest and is unverified here. The text implies a received power, not a technique.
- techniques: devotion_or_transmission; phenomena: body_or_objects_luminous, all_pervading_light
- conditions: setting: seances held with lights out (per summary, not seen in digest); prerequisites: being or becoming an angakok
- stages: light increases: The body feels well; at higher intensity he feels strong pressure and a film seems removed from his eyes, so that he sees clearly. -> very great pressure of light: In this state he visits Sedna (the visit is called nikkatto).
- with the light: Nothing prescribed: the light is always present, guides him, and lets him see far, under the earth and inside people; the stronger it is, the deeper and farther he sees.
- obstacles/fixes: Limit stated: he cannot see through drifting snow, fog or rain.
- safety flags: transmission_required_by_tradition
- interpretation: The light is the source of the angakok's supernatural sight and power and passes to another person when he dies.
- practice: This source gives testimony, not a method. In the Cumberland Sound account, on becoming an angakok a light covers his body, makes him feel well, and lets him see supernatural things; the stronger the light within, the deeper and farther he sees. When its intensity rises he feels strong pressure and it seems a film is lifted from his eyes. The light is always with him, guides him, shows past and future, and lets him see under the earth and into persons, though not through snow, fog or rain. Under very great pressure of light he visits Sedna. At death the light leaves him and enters someone else. How the light first arises is not described in the part read.
  - passage (testimony; Religion, the angakok, printed p. 133, source lines 5307-5346): On becoming an angakok a light covers his body and he can see supernatural things.
  - passage (stages; Religion, the angakok, printed p. 133, source lines 5307-5346): The stronger the light within him, the deeper and farther he sees; when intensity rises there is strong pressure and a film seems removed from his eyes.
- new leads: Boas printed pp. 153-154 on how the power is received

## [shm-czaplicka-aboriginal-siberia] Aboriginal Siberia: a study in social anthropology
M. A. Czaplicka; 1914; secondary_conduit; rights public_domain; access fulltext_saved; screen pass; keep_marginal; relevance 3, practicality 2, confidence medium (Two short passages in a large compilation; primary sources (Castrén, Bogoras) not read.)
- mode: perceptual. 'His eyes were dazzled as with too much light', while blindfolded, then 'he saw demons dancing on his arms and feet': phrased as visual experience under the blindfold.
- eyes: closed; stimulus independence: unclear
- ONSET induction: A teaching procedure applied to a novice: blindfold, blows on the back of the head and shoulders until dazzling light appears, then spirit-visions. Prerequisite: prior instruction in the Samoyed spirit world (Czaplicka stresses this). Whether the light persists without the blows is not said, so stimulus-independence is unclear; the visions that followed suggest development beyond the stimulus.
- techniques: eye_pressure_or_sense_closure, sensory_restriction, devotion_or_transmission; phenomena: white, figures_or_forms
- conditions: gaze_or_eye_position: blindfolded; setting: initiation by two shamans; duration_and_schedule: single session described; prerequisites: prior teaching about the spirit world; shamanic family; other: blows to occiput and shoulders
- with the light: Not stated; the light gives way to visions of spirits.
- safety flags: transmission_required_by_tradition
- interpretation: Part of shamanic education: dazzling light opens sight of spirits.
- practice: Reported, not recommended: in one Samoyed initiation two shamans blindfolded the novice and struck him on the back of the head and on the shoulders until his eyes were dazzled 'as with too much light'; he then saw demons dancing on his limbs. Czaplicka notes he had been taught beforehand about the spirit world. Separately, Chukchee hold that shamans' eyes are 'very bright' and see spirits in the dark. Head blows are dangerous; this is catalogued as a historical induction, not a practice to follow.
  - passage (onset; p. 190, source lines ~10712-10717): Two shamans blindfolded the novice and beat him on the back of the head and the shoulders till his eyes were dazzled as with too much light and he saw demons dancing on his arms and feet; he had been taught about the spirit world beforehand.
  - passage (interpretation; p. 174, source lines ~9881-9883): Chukchee explain shamans' ability to see spirits even in the dark by saying their eyes are very bright.
- new leads: M. A. Castrén, Nordische Reisen und Forschungen (1853-62), p. 191: Samoyed shaman training

## [shm-rasmussen-iglulik-intellectual-culture] Intellectual Culture of the Iglulik Eskimos (Report of the Fifth Thule Expedition 1921-24, vol. VII no. 1)
Knud Rasmussen (reporting Aua, Igjugarjuk and other Iglulik and Aivilik angakkuit); 1929; ethnographic_report; rights public_domain; access fulltext_saved; screen pass; keep; relevance 4, practicality 2, confidence medium (Read in digest; teacher-procured route and its time scale rest on the search summary and the opening of the passage.)
- mode: perceptual. The qaumaneq is described as a mysterious light felt in the body, inside the head and brain, 'a luminous fire' by which the shaman sees through darkness even with closed eyes; Aua adds that the same light shone out from him, imperceptible to humans but visible to spirits, and that vision reached through house, earth and sky. It is described as seen and felt, but the text does not describe how the light itself looks.
- eyes: closed; stimulus independence: yes
- ONSET both: Two routes. (1) A teacher 'procures' it for the pupil (the older shaman's task after instruction; details of time scale are in the summary, not confirmed in the digest). (2) Aua's own account: instruction from famous shamans failed; he sought solitude, became melancholy, wept without cause, then felt an inexplicable joy so strong he had to burst into song, and in the midst of that he became a shaman and had the light. Uvavnuk received it without seeking, when a ball of fire struck her, and lost it when the trance ended. The innEriugjat luminous bags are another source. Nothing is said to be forceable; it comes through spirits, ordeal or sudden grace.
- techniques: sensory_restriction, devotion_or_transmission, sound_or_mantra, fasting_or_austerity, attention_to_spontaneous_phenomena; phenomena: body_or_objects_luminous, lamp_or_flame, all_pervading_light, figures_or_forms
- conditions: gaze_or_eye_position: eyes closed for seeing in the dark; during exorcisms the lamps are put out and all must keep eyes closed on pain of blindness; setting: solitude; seance with lamps extinguished; duration_and_schedule: not given in the digest; Aua's solitude lasted until the change came; prerequisites: confession of taboo breaches; for some, a life of obligation from birth; other: helping spirits; amulets; song
- stages: solitude, melancholy, joy-song: Weeping without cause, then overwhelming joy that breaks into song; in that state Aua gained the light. -> first helping spirit: The house passage and roof seemed lifted up and he could see through the house, into the earth and up into the sky. -> light lit by the spirit (Uvavnuk): A ball of fire entered her; everything within her grew light; when the trance ended the light left her body.
- with the light: Use it for seeing far and hidden things and for calling spirits; it is kept alive by the helping spirit rather than by technique (Uvavnuk's light was lit only when the meteor spirit lit it).
- obstacles/fixes: Instruction from others failed for Aua; solitude and the joy-song succeeded.
- cautions: Opening the eyes while the lamps are out during an exorcism would mean blindness. | The light can leave when the trance ends (Uvavnuk).
- safety flags: prolonged_darkness, tradition_warns_against_seeking
- interpretation: The qaumaneq (anak'ua) is the shaman's enlightenment, the element that makes a man an angakoq, given by a spirit such as the Moon Spirit, the meteor spirit or the dead.
- practice: The qaumaneq is not something taught directly. The elder shaman is said to procure it for the pupil after a short period of instruction and confession of taboo breaches. Aua's own route was solitude: he went alone, became melancholy and wept without cause, then was overtaken by a joy so great it burst into song, and in the midst of it he became a shaman with the light of brain and body. He saw through house, earth and sky, and the light shone out to the spirits. Uvavnuk was struck by a ball of fire that entered her and lit her from within. Once it comes, the shaman can see in darkness even with closed eyes.
  - passage (mode; Chapter on shamans, printed p. 112-113, source lines 5648-5693): The light lets the shaman see in the dark, even with closed eyes, and perceive hidden and coming events.
  - passage (onset; Aua's account, printed pp. 118-119, source lines 6024-6082): After instruction from others failed, Aua sought solitude and became melancholy, which led to weeping and then overwhelming joy.
  - passage (testimony; Aua's account, printed p. 119, source lines 6024-6082): In the midst of mysterious delight he became a shaman and gained his qaumaneq, the shaman-light of brain and body.
  - passage (stages; Aua's account, printed p. 119, source lines 6024-6082): With the first helping spirit the house seemed lifted and he could see through the house, into the earth and up into the sky.
  - passage (caution; Uvavnuk, printed p. 123, source lines 6290-6331): Uvavnuk's light left when the trance ended; it was lit again only when the meteor spirit lit it.
- new leads: Rasmussen on Igjugarjuk (Caribou Inuit) ordeal | seeing oneself as a skeleton test, printed p. 114 onward


# STREAM: Modern non-lineage methods and testimony (modern-nonlineage)

## [mod-bates-perfect-sight] The Cure of Imperfect Sight by Treatment Without Glasses (Perfect Sight Without Glasses)
William H. Bates; 1920; teacher_manual; rights public_domain; access fulltext_saved; screen pass; keep_marginal; relevance 3, practicality 3, confidence high (Relevant chapters read whole. Kept as marginal because the source aims to abolish the lights and denies they are seen; its value is as a counter-instruction and as a clinician's list of what feeds and what quenches closed-eye light. Bates's physiology and his safety claims about the sun are not reliable. The observation that the lights are commoner with worse eyesight is his clinical impression only.)
- mode: perceptual. The source documents the dispute itself. Patients palming (eyes closed and covered, all light excluded) report lights and colours 'so vivid that they seem to be actually seen with the eyes', and some could not be argued out of the belief that they saw them with their eyes. One woman was 'positive' she saw grey with her eyes. Bates classes every such appearance as an 'illusion' of an imagination out of control, on the ground that closed eyes cannot see. So: perceptual by the percipients' report, imaginary by the author's theory. He draws no line between mind's-eye imagery and seen light; for him sight, memory and imagination are one function.
- eyes: both; stimulus independence: yes
- ONSET both: Everything here must be read in reverse, since Bates's goal is a perfect black and the lights are his failure condition. Read that way it is an induction report: simply closing the eyes and covering them with the palms, without pressure, produces lights and colours at once in most of his patients, and a perfect black is rare ('some never succeed in doing it until they are cured'). Stated intensifiers: poor eyesight (the worse the sight the more numerous, vivid and persistent), fatigue, hunger, anger, worry, depression, illness, pain, and above all effort, namely trying to concentrate on black and to ignore the colours, which made them 'worse and worse'. Stated extinguishers: relaxation, a remembered small black object held 'one part best', rapid mental shifting, deliberately recalling many colours in turn. He says nothing to the person who sees only black except that this is the resting state. No prerequisites; no grace or transmission language. Time scale: immediate. Ch. XVI adds that illusions of colour, multiple lights and after-images can be produced at will by straining (staring, trying to see a whole letter equally well, looking above a light and trying to see it as well as when directly regarded).
- techniques: eye_pressure_or_sense_closure, darkness_exposure, attention_to_spontaneous_phenomena, concentration_visual_object, visualization_crossing, afterimage_retention, light_source_gazing; phenomena: colour_fields, smoke_mist_cloud, lightning_flashes, red, blue, green, gold_yellow, grey field, moving clouds of black, kaleidoscopic appearances, floaters (muscae volitantes), sometimes rainbow-coloured or like white bubbles, after-images in complementary colour, coloured or black spots after sun-gazing, sun seen red, blue, green, yellow or black
- conditions: posture: Not specified in the chapters read; the figure shows a seated patient.; gaze_or_eye_position: Eyes closed, covered with the palms, fingers crossed on the forehead, no pressure on the eyeballs.; setting: Any; light fully excluded by the hands.; duration_and_schedule: A few minutes up to half an hour in the cases given; one patient palmed about twenty hours in a day. Some gain by long sessions, others begin to strain if they keep it up too long.; prerequisites: None.; other: Fatigue, hunger, anger, worry, depression, illness and pain all make black harder to see, i.e. increase the lights.
- stages: imperfect black: Grey, or black that holds for a few seconds and is replaced by another colour; streaks or floating clouds of grey; clouds of black moving across the field. -> lights and colours: Flashes of light, patches of red, blue, green, yellow. -> kaleidoscopic appearances: The most vivid end of the range, taken by the patient for real seeing; intensified by the effort to ignore them. -> perfect black: Bates's goal: a field so black that nothing blacker can be remembered, imagined or seen. Rare; equated with perfect relaxation and cure.
- with the light: Abolish it, and not by force. Regard it as an illusion, stop trying to ignore or suppress it, and replace it by an easy memory of black; if that fails, stop palming and work with the eyes open.
- obstacles/fixes: Cannot see black at all: look at a black object at the distance where its colour is seen best, close the eyes and remember it, repeat until the memory seems equal to the sight, then palm while holding it; if the field is not black within a few seconds, open and look again. | Black lasts only three to five seconds: remember a smaller area (one part of the object darker than the whole), or shift mentally from one black object to another, holding each a fraction of a second. | Persistent grey field taken for real: imagine a black C on it, then two, then a multitude of overlapping C's. | Memory of black fails: run through bright red, yellow, green, blue, purple and especially white, each in its most intense shade for no more than a second, for five or ten minutes; then remember a half-inch piece of white starch and note that the background has turned black. | Colours grow worse the more they are ignored: stop palming and, eyes open, deliberately recall as many colours as possible, so that the mind wanders on purpose. | Much difficulty: drop palming until sight has improved by other means.
- cautions: Effort and 'concentration' on the black defeat themselves; a patient who tried to see black only and ignore the colours went into convulsions and was under a physician for a month. | A minority only increase their strain by palming. | Sun-gazing in people with imperfect sight can give blind areas, patches of colour lasting seconds to months (one case a year or more), hours of total blindness, and inflammation; Bates insists these are always temporary and recommends the practice with 'discretion'. That reassurance is his own claim and is not accepted by ophthalmology.
- safety flags: sun_gazing
- interpretation: The lights are illusions of an imagination beyond the patient's control, a sign of mental strain acting on the visual centres; closed eyes cannot see, so nothing retinal is involved. Depth of black behind closed eyes is his gauge of relaxation.
- practice: Bates's procedure, and what it shows in reverse. Close the eyes and cover them with the cupped palms, fingers crossed on the forehead, with no pressure on the eyeballs and no light admitted. Most people do not then see black: they see grey streaks and clouds, flashes, patches of red, blue, green and yellow, up to kaleidoscopic displays that look fully seen. These are stronger with fatigue, hunger, worry, anger, illness or pain, and grow steadily worse when one tries hard to ignore them or to concentrate on blackness. Bates wants them gone: look at a black object, close the eyes, remember it easily with one small part blackest, shift lightly between black objects, and the field darkens; or cycle quickly through vivid remembered colours and then a small white patch, after which the ground turns black. Never force. For a practitioner seeking light, the report is that covered eyes yield lights readily, that strain, depletion and suppression feed them, and that relaxed attention to a small dark point quenches them. His sun-gazing advice should not be followed.
  - passage (onset; Ch. XII 'Palming', printed pp. 123-124, source lines 5056-5097): Even with eyes closed and covered so that all light is excluded, patients do not see black but lights and colours, from an imperfect black to kaleidoscopic appearances that seem actually seen with the eyes. The worse the sight, the more numerous, vivid and persistent. Fatigue, hunger, anger, worry or depression make black harder to see. Reported forms: streaks or floating clouds of grey, flashes of light, patches of red, blue, green, yellow, moving clouds of black, black replaced after a few seconds by another colour.
  - passage (mode; Ch. XII 'Palming', printed pp. 125-126, source lines 5102-5124): Some patients were so struck by the vividness of the colours that no argument could convince them they were not seeing them with their eyes. They granted that other people's closed-eye lights would be illusions but held their own to be reality, until they 'demonstrated the truth' for themselves.
  - passage (method; Ch. XII 'Palming', printed p. 126, source lines 5126-5141): The antidote: look at a black object where its colour is seen best, close the eyes and remember the colour, repeat until memory seems equal to sight, then palm while holding the memory. If the memory is perfect the whole field goes black; if not within a few seconds, open the eyes and look again. Note that this is a remembered colour taking over the seen field.
  - passage (caution; Ch. XII 'Palming', printed p. 130, source lines 5282-5301): Effort and concentration on the black cannot succeed. A patient who tried to see only black and ignore the kaleidoscopic colours found them growing worse the more she ignored them, went into convulsions from the strain, and needed a month's medical attendance. The fix was to stop palming and, eyes open, recall as many colours as possible, making the mind wander on purpose; after that she could palm for short periods.
  - passage (caution; Ch. XVII 'Vision Under Adverse Conditions', printed pp. 186-188, source lines 7389-7460): After sun-gazing by people with defective sight: blind areas, the sun seen slate, yellow, red, blue or black, and patches of colour that persist from seconds to months, in one case a year or more after a few seconds' look; also hours of total blindness and inflammation. Bates calls all of it temporary and a mental illusion, and still recommends sun-gazing with care, even focusing the sun on the eye with a burning glass. Dangerous advice.
  - passage (interpretation; Ch. XVI 'Illusions of Imperfect and of Normal Eyesight', printed pp. 178-180, source lines 7112-7189): Bates rejects retinal fatigue as the cause of after-images: closed eyes cannot see, so anything seen then is a mental illusion showing loss of mental control. He adds that anyone can produce an after-image at will by straining (trying to see the big C 'all alike'), and multiple lights by looking above a light and trying to see it as well as when looked at directly.

## [mod-galton-inquiries-human-faculty] Inquiries into Human Faculty and its Development (sections 'Mental Imagery' and 'Visionaries')
Francis Galton; 1883; practitioner_testimony; rights public_domain; access fulltext_saved; screen pass; keep; relevance 4, practicality 3, confidence high (Both relevant chapters read in full in the saved text. Two limits. First, Galton's own method occupies a single paragraph; the candidate summary is accurate but there is no more detail than quoted here, and he does not say whether his eyes were open or closed in the dark. Second, the 'Mental Imagery' chapter is almost wholly about mind's-eye visualising (vividness returns, blindfold chess, training of visual memory, 'retaining a retinal picture', projecting an image onto paper) and does not claim that trained visualisation becomes perceived light; I tagged visualization_crossing only for Henslow, who can 'usually summon up any object thought of' and then sees it take on a life of its own. Henslow, Mrs. Haweis and the unnamed correspondents are quoted by Galton, 'extracted and condensed' in Henslow's case.)
- mode: perceptual. Galton places his own phenomena in 'the field of view in the dark' and 'the optical field', and classes them with after-images, phosphenes and light-dust rather than with his (weak) voluntary visualising. He states as a finding that 'visions' and vivid visualisation are different processes that can coexist in one person, and prints a correspondent's criteria: the mental image is voluntary, forms and fades slowly, seems far off 'in some far-off recess of the mind', and is only stage-lit; the waking vision is involuntary, instantaneous, close, 'filling as it were the whole head', and as distinct as a street in broad daylight. Henslow's closed-eye objects are 'highly brilliant', refuse his will, and end in a 'bright point of light' that shines 'like a star'. Mrs. Haweis as a child tried to touch her roses. Note that most of what is seen is forms, patterns and objects; plain light (a star-like point, gold sparks, a light-purple cloudiness) is a minor part of the reports.
- eyes: closed; stimulus independence: not_applicable
- ONSET both: This is the book's most useful content. Galton presents himself as the hard case: he visualises only with effort, is 'peculiarly inapt' to see after-images, phosphenes or light-dust, and would have 'emphatically declared' his dark field uniform black apart from an occasional light-purple cloudiness. What changed this was (1) perfect darkness, (2) carefully watching the field of view, (3) habituating himself to do so, and (4) a particular quality of looking: 'the same sort of strain that one tries to decipher a sign-post in the dark'. He then found a continual kaleidoscopic change of patterns and forms. He says many persons will find the same, and that the phenomena are 'certainly present and yet habitually overlooked'. No time scale is given beyond 'habituating'. For Henslow, a natural seer, the instruction is only to shut the eyes and wait: something comes 'within a minute or two' without thinking of any object. Limits stated by Galton: the visionary tendency is a natural, strongly hereditary gift, unequal between people; but it is blunted by repression in childhood and by abstract, verbal habits of thought, and faint fantasies 'increase in definition through being habitually dwelt upon'. Nothing about grace or transmission. He names fasting, want of sleep and solitary musing as each conducive to visions, as observation, not as recommendation.
- techniques: darkness_exposure, attention_to_spontaneous_phenomena, sleep_threshold, visualization_crossing; phenomena: geometric_patterns, figures_or_forms, smoke_mist_cloud, violet, sparks_or_points, gold_yellow, stars, colour_fields, kaleidoscopic_change, faces
- conditions: posture: Not specified by Galton. Goethe (as reported by Galton) bent his head and closed his eyes. Phantasmagoria usually come in bed before sleep.; gaze_or_eye_position: Galton: watch the dark field itself with a deciphering strain, as when making out a sign-post in the dark; he does not say whether his eyes were open or shut. Henslow: eyes shut. Henslow looks to the right or left within the vision to find adjoining parts of an object.; setting: Galton: 'perfect darkness'. Mrs. Haweis: 'whenever it was dark or darkish, in bed or otherwise'. Phantasmagoria: sometimes at the instant of putting out the candle, more often in bed, 'preparing to sleep, but by no means yet asleep'; occasionally in broad daylight.; duration_and_schedule: Galton: repeated until habitual; no figures. Henslow: onset within a minute or two, then continues as long as he cares to watch; he repeated the experiments many times and drew the results.; prerequisites: None stated for Galton's method. Natural endowment varies and runs in families; abstract verbal thinking and 'book-learning' dull the faculty.; other: The mind must not move to another subject: Galton's patterns vanish from sight and memory the instant he thinks about anything.
- stages: uniform black (Galton's prior belief): Before careful trial the dark field seems a uniform black with an occasional light-purple cloudiness and small variations. -> kaleidoscopic change of patterns and forms: After habituated, strained watching: a perpetual automatic series of changes, fugitive and elaborate, too quick to draw, gone the instant thought intervenes. Galton stays at this level. -> clear image of some object (Henslow): Within a minute or two of shutting the eyes a clear object appears, usually with one part exaggerated; it oscillates, rotates and transforms by suggestion into further objects, often brilliant (cut glass, gold and silver filigree, coloured carpets). -> 'visual cycle' (Henslow): By continued effort of will the series is brought back to its starting image; the first 'concession' to the will is partial and indirect. -> hazy obscurity: Henslow's objects all finally vanish into 'the usual hazy obscurity'; in one case via a bright point that shines intensely like a star and then dims.
- with the light: Watch and examine without thinking of anything else. Galton offers no further use. Henslow examines parts 'cautiously', tries to steer by will (largely refused while the series is 'in full career', sometimes yielding after sustained effort), and draws what he saw afterwards. Galton's general remark is that such fantasies gain definition by being habitually dwelt upon.
- obstacles/fixes: Thinking about anything else abolishes the patterns at once (Galton). Implied fix: keep attention on the field alone. | Habitual overlooking: the phenomena are present but unnoticed; the fix is deliberate, strained scrutiny repeated until habitual. | Scrutiny destabilises the image: when Henslow scrutinised the gun-stock it oscillated and crumpled; when he concentrated on one rotating flower it became a 'rotating haze'. He gives up the part and moves 'cautiously' to another. | Willed objects will not come cleanly: a 'watch' arrives as a hybrid and lapses into a warming-pan. Continued effort sometimes obtains a partial concession and then the wanted form. | Mental images generally do not grow stronger by dwelling on them; the first impression is usually the most vigorous (Mental Imagery chapter). | The faculty fades with age and disuse in some: Mrs. Haweis's roses shrank yearly from large and palpable to an instantaneous speck.
- cautions: Galton treats visions as products of one part of the brain watched by another; giving them religious sanction is, in his word, an error. | He regards the visionary tendency as continuous with hallucination and with the 'familiar hallucinations of the insane', and says that when opinion favours the supernatural the faculty runs 'into extravagance owing to the removal of reasonable safeguards'. | Fasting, want of sleep and solitude produce visions; he links solitude with madness. These are reported as causes, not prescribed. | Very vivid imagery can be a burden: one informant 'craves for an absence of visual perceptions, they are so brilliant and persistent'.
- interpretation: Automatic brain activity that goes on 'wastefully' in the optical field and is normally overlooked; a slight functional change makes it vivid, and the mind then interprets the blur into figures, as with faces in the fire. Not supernatural; continuous with dreaming and hallucination.
- practice: From Galton's own trial, as a self-declared non-seer: go into perfect darkness and watch the field of view carefully. Do not look passively. Use the kind of strain with which you try to read a sign-post in the dark, as if something is there to be made out. Repeat until this way of looking is habitual. Keep the mind off every other subject, since the instant you begin to think about anything the phenomena leave sight and memory. Expect, at first, black with a faint purplish cloudiness, then a continual kaleidoscopic shifting of patterns and forms, fugitive and faint. From Henslow, a strong seer: shut the eyes and simply wait; within a minute or two an object appears unbidden and begins transforming. Watch as a spectator. Examine cautiously, because hard scrutiny makes the image oscillate or dissolve into haze. Attempts to impose a chosen object usually fail while the series is running; sustained effort sometimes wins a partial concession. The dark moments before sleep are the commonest occasion for others.
  - passage (onset; 'Visionaries', printed p. 158, source lines 6505-6523): Galton, who visualises with effort and is inapt to see after-images, phosphenes or light-dust, believed his dark field was uniform black with an occasional light-purple cloudiness. After habituating himself to examine it with the strain used to decipher a sign-post in the dark, he found a continual kaleidoscopic change of patterns and forms. He says many persons will find the same if they watch carefully in perfect darkness.
  - passage (caution; 'Visionaries', printed p. 159, source lines 6528-6538): The patterns are too fugitive and elaborate to draw; they vanish from sight and from memory the instant he thinks of anything; he finds it curious that they are so certainly present yet habitually overlooked, and supposes a very slight physiological change would make them vivid. The OCR has stray marks at the page head ('with • '' and '/K') which do not affect the sense.
  - passage (method; 'Visionaries', printed pp. 161-162, Henslow's letter (seen via Galton), source lines 6622-6663): Henslow: no need to think of any object first; with eyes shut something comes of itself within a minute or two, then changes rapidly. The images are numerous, often beautiful and highly brilliant, restless (oscillating, rotating), and he feels a mere spectator. Introducing a chosen object is next to impossible while the series runs. Galton's own summary just before (p. 159) says Henslow 'shuts his eyes and waits'.
  - passage (testimony; 'Visionaries', printed p. 163, Henslow's Fig. 73 (seen via Galton), source lines 6699-6715): A spontaneous series (poppy capsule, salvias, a 'rotating haze' when he concentrated on one spinning flower, a nest, eggs) ends with one egg whose bright point of light shines with great intensity like a star, then dims into the hazy obscurity into which all his visual objects finally vanish. This is the one place where the testimony describes plain light rather than a formed object.
  - passage (mode; 'Visionaries', printed pp. 163-165, Galton's statement and an unnamed friend's letter, source lines 6721-6780): Galton: some who visualise well and also see visions declare the vision is not a vivid visualisation but a different phenomenon, arriving by a different channel. The friend's criteria: mental image is stage-lit and vague, subservient to the will, slow to form and fade, far off in a recess of the mind; the waking vision is like broad daylight, independent of the will, instantaneous in coming and going, quite close, filling the whole head. The friend adds that no effort of imagination can call the visions up.
  - passage (development; 'Visionaries', closing paragraph, printed pp. 176-177, source lines 7225-7260): Galton's account of how the faculty is lost and regained: imaginative children see such things, are laughed at, learn to discredit them and stop attending, so the tendency is 'blunted by repression'. Where opinion favours them, the faint fantasies of ordinary people are attended to and encouraged and gain definition by being habitually dwelt on. He adds that no new faculty is evoked; a smothered one is released, and he warns that it then runs to extravagance.
- new leads: Goethe's closed-eye rosette (head bent, eyes closed, thinking of a rose), cited by Galton p. 159 without reference; the same passage Mueller discusses | St. James's Gazette, 10, 15 and 20 Feb. 1882: correspondence on phantasmagoria (Galton's footnote, p. 167) | Brierre de Boismont, Hallucinations (1862), chapter on the 'stars' of great men, including Napoleon's star reported by General Rapp | Lecoq de Boisbaudran, Education de la Memoire Pittoresque (repr. in Enseignement Artistique, Paris 1879): training of visual memory, three to four months to summon and hold images at will. Mind's-eye training, so probably out of scope

## [mod-goethe-farbenlehre-physiologische-farben] Zur Farbenlehre, Didaktischer Teil, Erste Abteilung: Physiologische Farben
Johann Wolfgang von Goethe; 1810; practitioner_testimony; rights public_domain; access fulltext_saved; screen pass; keep_marginal; relevance 3, practicality 4, confidence high (Fraktur OCR loses words at many line-ends; quotes in passages 4-5 are left as the OCR stands; the paraphrases restore the sense, with reconstructions flagged. Para 6 (eyes closed in total darkness) is too garbled to report. Not a spiritual source: kept marginal as the classic mechanism account for afterimage-based light practices (fire kasina, trataka); the light always decays and Goethe treats prolonged persistence as weakness. Quotes in passages 1-3 are joined across line breaks with hyphens removed and a few obvious OCR slips corrected (e.g. 'bu lebte' to 'belebte', 'bs druck “ag' to 'Eindruck nach'); anchors are exact.)
- mode: perceptual. Everything is explicitly retinal: the afterimage 'schwebt' before the eyes in the dark room or on a wall, is located on a fixed place of the retina, grows with projection distance (paras 21-22), and is called 'ein Bild, das nunmehr dem Auge angehoert' (para 49). Light from pressure or a blow is 'Licht ohne aeusseres Licht' (para 120).
- eyes: both; stimulus independence: unclear
- ONSET induction: Goethe gives a reproducible physical induction anyone can perform: stare five seconds at a sunlit white disc in a darkened room, close the shutter, look into the darkest corner (or close the eyes) and the coloured image floats there; it can be revived again and again by opening and closing the eyes, lasting about seven minutes in total. Conditions that strengthen it: a rested, receptive eye (on waking, at dusk), dark background. He also says gentle then increasing pressure on the eyeball, in certain bodily states (heated blood, heightened sensitivity), can excite 'ein blendend unertraegliches Licht'. Stimulus independence is partial: the light outlasts the stimulus by minutes, but always decays; Goethe treats any long persistence as weakness or pathology (paras 28, 121-125), and he does not frame any of it as spiritual. No grace or transmission claims.
- techniques: afterimage_retention, light_source_gazing, darkness_exposure, eye_pressure_or_sense_closure, gazing_fixed_point; phenomena: sun_disc, white, gold_yellow, red, blue, green, colour_fields, smoke_mist_cloud, sparks_or_points, lightning_flashes, geometric_patterns, sphere_pearl_bindu
- conditions: gaze_or_eye_position: Stare fixedly ('starr', 'unverwandt') at the bright disc; afterwards look at the darkest place in the room, or close the eyes; alternate opening and closing to revive.; setting: Room darkened as far as possible, a round shutter opening about three inches across letting the sun fall on white paper; for the green phase a moderately lit room and a light grey surface.; duration_and_schedule: Exposure five seconds; purple after 13 s, blue after a further 29 s, colourless after a further 48 s; revived by blinking for about seven minutes in all. Times vary with the strength of the eye.; prerequisites: A rested, receptive eye gives the strongest effects: morning on waking, dusk, after long darkness (paras 20, 54, 92).; other: Viewing the afterimage alternately on a white and a black board gives purple and green phases; with practice both opposite colours can be seen at once on the boundary (para 43).
- stages: farbloses Scheinbild (dark ground): Immediately after the shutter closes: a floating round image, centre bright, colourless, somewhat yellow; the rim at once purple. -> Purpur: Purple creeps in from the edge until it covers the whole disc and drives out the bright centre (about 13 s). -> Blau: As soon as the whole disc is purple the rim turns blue, which pushes inward until the image is wholly blue (about 29 s more). -> unfaerbig: The rim becomes dark and colourless and slowly displaces the blue until the whole is colourless (about 48 s more); then the image weakens and shrinks, losing its corners and becoming a small round image, until gone. Revivable by blinking for about seven minutes. -> on a light grey surface: Seen against a moderately lit light grey ground the image is dark and is overrun from the edge by a coloured rim (green is named in para 43; the OCR of para 42 is fragmentary) that fills the disc, then a further colour encircles it.
- with the light: Observe and measure it; revive the fading image by opening and closing the eyes; move it onto a white or black ground to switch its colour phase. Goethe also warns observers to keep afterimages from contaminating later observations.
- obstacles/fixes: Coming from bright daylight into the dark, the eye is blind to faint lights for a while; stay until the eye has recovered (paras 10-11). | A fading image can be revived by alternating opening and closing the eyes, 'Erregung und Schonung' (para 27). | Direct gaze can suppress subtle effects: the poppy 'flash' at dusk appeared only when looking sideways, from the corner of the eye (para 54).
- cautions: Very long persistence of an image (14-17 minutes or more) signals extreme weakness of the organ (para 28); excessive duration is pathological (paras 121-122). | Looking into the sun can leave the image for several days; one case of fifty years is reported (para 123, OCR partly lost). | Strong pressure on the eye in certain bodily states produces a dazzling, unbearable light (para 115); sparks, flashes and fiery figures are listed among pathological colours (paras 114-120).
- safety flags: sun_gazing, eye_pressure
- interpretation: Physiological colours are the living retina's own opposition to what is imposed on it: after strong light it produces, in a succession of 'Schwingungen', the demanded opposite states and colours as it restores itself, like the dying ring of a bell (Abklingen). Light from pressure or a blow is light without outward light, an 'abklingendes Licht' produced in the eye itself.
- practice: Darken a room completely except for a round hole about three inches wide in the shutter that lets sunlight fall on a sheet of white paper. From some distance stare fixedly at the lit disc for about five seconds, then cover the hole and look into the darkest part of the room (or close the eyes). A round image floats before you: centre bright, colourless to yellowish, rim purple. Watch the purple advance inward until the whole disc is purple, then a blue rim advance until it is all blue, then a dark colourless rim until it is all colourless; it then shrinks and fades. When it seems gone, open and close the eyes alternately and it returns; Goethe kept it alive this way for about seven minutes. Looked at instead on a light grey or white surface in a moderately lit room it appears dark with a green rim; on black it shows purple. Effects are strongest with a rested eye: on waking, at dusk, after time in darkness. Gentle, increasing pressure on the eyeball at night produces rings of light, or a dazzling light.
  - passage (stages; IV Blendendes farbloses Bild, para 40, source lines 2480-2506): In a room darkened as far as possible, let the sun through a round shutter opening about three inches across onto white paper, stare fixedly at the lit disc from some distance, close the opening and look at the darkest place in the room: a round apparition floats before you. Its centre is bright, colourless, somewhat yellow; the rim at once purple. Purple slowly covers the circle from outside inward and drives out the bright centre; as soon as it is all purple the rim turns blue and blue displaces the purple inward; when it is fully blue the rim turns dark and colourless, and only after a long time does the colourless rim drive out the blue. The image then shrinks and weakens. The retina restores itself against the violent outer impression through a succession of oscillations ('Succeſſion von Schwingungen'). Eyes here are open, directed at darkness; para 21 confirms the same image is seen with eyes closed after looking at the sun.
  - passage (testimony; IV, para 41, source lines 2525-2537): First-person timings, consistent over several trials: five seconds' gaze at the dazzling image; after closing the slide the coloured image floats; after 13 seconds wholly purple, after a further 29 seconds wholly blue, after a further 48 colourless. By closing and opening the eye he revived the image again and again, so that it was wholly lost only after seven minutes. Others will find shorter or longer times according to stronger or weaker eyes.
  - passage (method; II, paras 21 and 27, source lines 2187-2262; III-IV paras 42-43, lines 2553-2600): Closing the eye straight after looking at the sun, one is surprised how small the remaining image is; projected with open eyes onto a wall it appears larger the farther the surface (para 22). An image no longer noticeable can be revived on the retina by opening and closing the eyes, alternating excitation and rest (para 27). With the light impression still in the eye, looking at a light grey object in a moderately lit room shows the image dark with a coloured rim spreading inward (para 42, OCR fragmentary); alternating between white and black boards gives now a purple, now a green phenomenon, and with practice both opposite colours together at the boundary (para 43).
  - passage (onset; Subjective Hoefe, para 96, source lines 3647-3652): If one presses the eye only a little from the inner corner, darker or lighter circles arise. At night one can sometimes, with continued pressure, see a succession of such circles, each developing out of the other and swallowed by the next. (OCR loses line-ends; 'inner corner' and 'continued' are reconstructions.)
  - passage (caution; Pathologische Farben, paras 114-120 and 127, source lines 3918-4000, 4046-4049): Under morbid affections of the retina the eye is disposed to light-phenomena without outward stimulus. With a blow to the eye sparks seem to spray about; and in certain bodily states, especially heated blood and lively sensitivity, if one presses the eye first gently and then ever more strongly, one can excite a dazzling, unbearable light. Patients after cataract operations see fiery flashes for eight to fourteen days; others see light-sparks and balls, fiery sparks and luminous figures. 'As there light arises without outer light, so these images arise without outer images.' Para 127 (OCR fragmentary): as pressure on the eyeball produces a light-appearance on the retina, it is as it were a decaying ('abklingendes') light; a stray line reads that weak pressure produces a red [colour].
- new leads: Scherfer on afterimage colours (cited para 126) | Darwin, 'ocular spectra' (para 2) | Purkinje, Beobachtungen zur Physiologie der Sinne (later work on pressure phosphenes, not cited here)

## [mod-mueller-phantastische-gesichtserscheinungen] Ueber die phantastischen Gesichtserscheinungen: eine physiologische Untersuchung
Johannes Müller; 1826; practitioner_testimony; rights public_domain; access fulltext_saved; screen pass; keep; relevance 5, practicality 4, confidence high (Relevant sections read in full in Fraktur OCR; minor OCR noise does not affect sense. Chapter VIII (narcotics) not read.)
- mode: perceptual. Müller insists the images are 'wirklich leuchtend', as surely luminous as a pressure phosphene, and sharply distinguishes the moment a merely imagined outline (not luminous, not moving) gives way to figures that suddenly stand luminous 'ohne alle Anregung durch die Vorstellung'. He sees them with closed eyes in waking, by day, in sober reflection.
- eyes: closed; stimulus independence: yes
- ONSET both: Induction: sit, close the eyes, abstract from everything, in a dark place, calm, not just after eating or wine; fasting makes them 'wonderfully' vivid. He holds everyone has traces: whoever lacks them by day can notice them before sleep, and if not then, in dream. Limits: willing a specific image almost never works for him (half-days of practice in the dark; one whole evening trying for red yielded once a red drapery, unbidden); luminous images come only when the 'disposition' is present, sudden and against the will. Willed luminous imaging is extremely rare (Cardano, Goethe, an artist H.) and even then the image transforms on its own.
- techniques: darkness_exposure, eye_position, attention_to_spontaneous_phenomena, sleep_threshold, fasting_or_austerity, visualization_crossing; phenomena: sparks_or_points, smoke_mist_cloud, colour_fields, figures_or_forms, all_pervading_light, geometric_patterns
- conditions: posture: Seated or lying; Goethe: head bowed; gaze_or_eye_position: Closed, fully rested eyes, eye muscles slack; looking into the darkness of the visual field; any eye movement dispels images; setting: Dark place; any time of day, also before sleep; duration_and_schedule: Often followed for half an hour; hours of daytime rest; half-days in willed attempts; prerequisites: Good health, mental calm without passion; not just eaten; no wine; fasting helps; other: Ward off all thought and judgement; only observe
- stages: Lichtflecken, Nebel: Dark field rich in light-spots, mists, shifting colours. -> inneres mattes Tageslicht: Often the darkness gradually brightens into a dull inner daylight; images follow immediately. -> leuchtende Bilder: Bounded images, faint then clearer, luminous, sometimes coloured, moving and transforming, often at the field's edges; unknown figures, animals, lit rooms. -> Hellsehen des Traumes: Whole field lit as by daylight; one forgets one's bed and the images become dream.
- with the light: Observe passively without reflection (reflection and eye movement dispel); hold them as long as possible; do not try to impose content; treat as products of the sense's own life, not objective.
- obstacles/fixes: Eye movement or reflection dispels: keep eyes and mind still. | Wine and recent eating prevent; fasting intensifies. | Willed imagery stays non-luminous: wait for the disposition; images come unbidden.
- cautions: Ecstatic and magical visions falsely ascribe objectivity to these images; superstition worships or fears them.
- safety flags: fasting
- interpretation: Luminous phantasms are the visual sense's own life (light and colour immanent in the 'Sehsinnsubstanz') excited by plastic imagination; the same images become dreams.
- practice: In a dark place, when well, calm and neither fed nor having taken wine, sit or lie and close the eyes. Let them rest completely, with a feeling of slackness in the eye muscles, and sink into the stillness of the eye and the darkness of the field. Refuse all thoughts and judgements; only watch what appears. First come light-spots, mists and drifting colours; often the field slowly brightens to a dull inner daylight, then bounded luminous images appear, faint at first, then clear, moving and changing. Do not move the eyes or start reflecting, or they vanish. Follow them for up to half an hour; before sleep they pass into dream. Fasting strengthens them greatly. Do not expect to summon a chosen image: imagining it keeps it unlit; the luminous figure arrives suddenly, of its own. If nothing comes by day, watch at the edge of sleep.
  - passage (method; Ch. VI §34, source lines 1115-1125): Closed, fully rested eyes looking into the dark field; slack eye muscles; sink into the eye's stillness; ward off thought and judgement; only observe.
  - passage (onset; Ch. VI §36, source lines 1162-1175): By day he need only sit, close the eyes and abstract; in a dark place, calm, not after food or spirits, the images are certain.
  - passage (stages; Ch. VI §37, source lines 1178-1185): The dark field often brightens gradually into a dull inner daylight before the images appear.
  - passage (mode; Ch. VI §§39-40, source lines 1236-1265): What he imagines stays an unlit outline; suddenly luminous figures stand there unprompted, as surely luminous as a pressure phosphene; 'I do not see what I would like to see'.
  - passage (development; Ch. VI §41, source lines 1274-1280): Easiest when well and unexcited, especially after fasting, which makes them wonderfully vivid; never after wine.
  - passage (onset; Ch. XII §147-148, source lines 3741-3765): Despite half-days of willed practice in the dark he could almost never produce a chosen luminous image or fix a colour; one evening of trying for red yielded once, suddenly, a red drapery he had not imagined.
- new leads: Goethe, Zur Morphologie II.2 (1824) p. 444: closed eyes, head bowed, imagined flower unfolding luminous like a kaleidoscope | Cardano, De varietate rerum VIII and De subtilitate XVIII: willed luminous imaging | Gruithuisen, Anthropologie §449

## [mod-newton-letter-sun-phantasm] Letter to John Locke, 30 June 1691, on the 'phantasm' of the sun (in Lord King, The Life of John Locke)
Isaac Newton; 1691 (letter); printed 1830; practitioner_testimony; rights public_domain; access fulltext_saved; screen pass; keep; relevance 4, practicality 3, confidence high
- mode: perceptual. Newton sees a 'round bright spot of light like the sun' on clouds, books and bright objects, and with either eye; he calls it a spectrum or phantasm and attributes its return to the power of fancy acting on the sensorium. It reappears in a dark room and in bed at midnight with curtains drawn.
- eyes: both; stimulus independence: yes
- ONSET induction: Induction by a stimulus first (brief sun glance in a mirror with one eye, then dark corner, winking, repeated three times); on the third round the fading afterimage returned vivid when he intended his fancy on it. After that no sun was needed: attending 'as when a man looks earnestly to see any thing which is difficult to be seen' brought it back, easier each time; within a few hours it was uncontrollable. So the pathway is stimulus then volitional attention, and the independence of the stimulus is explicit. No grace claim. Dangerous: eye damage.
- techniques: afterimage_retention, light_source_gazing, darkness_exposure, attention_to_spontaneous_phenomena; phenomena: sun_disc, colour_fields, body_or_objects_luminous
- conditions: gaze_or_eye_position: right eye only at the sun's reflection, then eyes turned to a dark corner and winked; setting: dark corner of the chamber; duration_and_schedule: three repetitions the first time; within a few hours the effect pervaded all bright objects; recovery took three or four days dark, some months of recurrence on thinking of it; prerequisites: none stated
- stages: afterimage with circles of colours: decays by degrees and vanishes -> willed return: returns vivid when attention is intended upon it; vanishes when attention stops -> spread to bright objects and the other eye: round bright spot seen on clouds, book; left eye took the impression though never exposed
- with the light: intend the mind upon it to bring it back; for recovery, divert the imagination to other things and remain in the dark
- obstacles/fixes: loss of use of eyes: shut in a darkened room three days, divert the imagination from the sun, avoid bright objects for some days longer
- cautions: 'with the hazard of my eyes'; he durst neither read nor write; recurrence for months whenever he meditated on it
- safety flags: sun_gazing, prolonged_darkness
- interpretation: A disposition of the sensorium to move the imagination strongly and to be easily moved by imagination and light; he calls the cause too hard a knot to untie.
- practice: Newton looked very briefly at the sun's reflection in a mirror with one eye, then turned into a dark corner and winked, watching the impression and its colour rings fade. He did this three times. As the third image faded he directed his attention onto it as one strains to see something hard to see, and it came back as bright as at first, vanishing when he let go. Repeating the inward attention in the dark, with no further sun, made it return more easily each time, until a round bright spot of the sun appeared on any bright object, and the unexposed eye acquired it too. He lost use of his eyes; three or four days in a dark room, thinking of other things, restored them. He warns of the hazard to the eyes.
  - passage (method; Letter of 30 June 1691, source lines 14080-14219): He looked briefly at the sun in a looking-glass with the right eye, then turned to a dark corner and winked to watch the impression and colour circles decay.
  - passage (onset; Letter, source lines 14080-14219): He intended his mind on the faded image like one looking earnestly at something hard to see, and it returned without the sun, more easily with repetition.
  - passage (development; Letter, source lines 14080-14219): Repetition made return easier until the spot was seen on any bright object.
  - passage (caution; Letter, source lines 14080-14219): Within hours he could not look at any bright object without seeing the sun, and could not read or write.
  - passage (interpretation; Letter, source lines 14080-14219): He locates the effect in a disposition of the sensorium to be moved by imagination and by light.

## [mod-sagan-awakening-third-eye] Awakening the Third Eye
Samuel Sagan; 1992; 3rd ed. 2007; teacher_manual; rights in_copyright; access fulltext_saved; screen pass; keep; relevance 5, practicality 5, confidence medium (Read in digest; complete 3.5 and the Ch. 5/7/10 material were not visible.)
- mode: perceptual. Insists on 'no imagination, no visualisation – just awareness of what is in front of you' and calls the light 'spiritual light, perceived with your eyes closed', not physical light; it ranges from haze to colours, shining dots and patterns. Open-eye work: room appears darker, 'astral light' seen, paths glow at night. The source's own metaphysics (etheric/astral) frames it, but the instruction is perceptual.
- eyes: both; stimulus independence: not_applicable
- ONSET both: Staged ladder for those who see nothing: first find any vibration/tingling/pressure between the eyebrows (throat friction plus gentle awareness, 5 to 10 minutes per phase), then in the later phase look there for any fog, haze or glow and connect it to the throat friction. Says most who think they cannot see light see it but do not recognise it, faint whitish haze counts. Claims no student at his school failed to feel the vibration after a bit of practice, and urges persistence. Intensity varies day to day. No grace or transmission requirement stated in the digest; one should not grasp or concentrate. Suggested 5 to 10 minutes each morning plus constant background awareness.
- techniques: sound_or_mantra, breath_control, concentration_nonvisual, posture_or_body_lock, darkness_exposure, gazing_fixed_point, attention_to_spontaneous_phenomena; phenomena: smoke_mist_cloud, colour_fields, sparks_or_points, violet, blue, black_light, geometric_patterns, light_in_the_room, body_or_objects_luminous, white
- conditions: posture: first opening lying down; meditation sitting with spine vertical, still; gaze_or_eye_position: eyes closed; eyeballs not directed at the brow, which only creates tension; setting: darkness and candlelight favoured over electric light for open-eye seeing; duration_and_schedule: 30 minute meditation: 5 minutes per phase; at least 5 to 10 minutes every morning; first opening phases 5 to 10 minutes; prerequisites: none stated; avoid grasping; other: throat friction: soft laryngeal breathing sound kept up through the phases
- stages: vibration (etheric): tingling, pressure, density between the eyebrows; the first thread -> light (astral): haze, cloud, glow, colours, shining dots, patterns; to be refined into brighter light -> purple light / space: purple, dark blue or dark expanse behind the lights felt as space around you; relief in the heart -> open-eye seeing: the room appears darker, 'astral light' as glowing semi-darkness, auras and glowing objects
- with the light: connect throat friction to it to amplify it, drop the hazier parts and attend to the brightest; then rest awareness in the background space; do not label hues too readily
- obstacles/fixes: grasping or concentrating blocks the process: stay vacant | sensation elsewhere in the face: ignore and stay between the eyebrows | faint or blurry light: accept as first thread and keep practising | too intense: open the eyes | thoughts: do not fight, return to the eye or intensify throat friction
- cautions: no forcing, no visualisation | do not attach meaning to minor tingling and flashes | keep the practice relative; psychic protection treated in a separate chapter
- interpretation: Light between the eyebrows indicates activation of the astral layer of the third eye; the purple space is the astral space. A teacher-manual metaphysics.
- practice: Lie down or sit with a vertical spine, eyes closed, and breathe with a soft friction sound in the throat. Move awareness to the area between the eyebrows without grasping or turning the eyeballs there. Look for any vibration, tingling or pressure and join it to the throat friction for several minutes. Then drop the vibration and look in front of the brow for a fog, haze, glow or colour, however faint, and connect the friction to it so it grows brighter; attend to the brightest part, often tiny drifting particles. Finally rest in the dark or purple background as a space. Use no imagination or visualisation. Faint whitish haze is the right beginning. Practise 5 minutes per phase, and every morning, and keep a light awareness between the eyebrows through the day.
  - passage (method; 3.2 First opening, Phase 6, source lines 1335-1530): After stopping the throat friction, stay extremely still and watch for light or colour between the eyebrows.
  - passage (mode; 3.7 Meditation phase 3, source lines 1742-1803): Light is to be perceived, not imagined: it is spiritual light seen with the eyes closed.
  - passage (onset; Tips, phase 3, source lines 1872-1911): Most who think they cannot see the light do see it but do not recognise it; a faint whitish haze is the first thread, to be developed with the amplifying throat friction.
  - passage (onset; 3.5, source lines 1624-1699): For those who feel nothing, he says every student eventually felt the vibration and urges persistence.
  - passage (stages; 3.7 phase 3, source lines 1742-1803): Tiny shining particles drifting in the space in front are a common experience; the friction is connected to the most luminous part.
- new leads: Sagan chapters 5, 7 (seeing and auras), 10 (experiences on the way, pressure and headaches), 15 (night practice)

## [mod-wenger-image-streaming-backup] Image-Streaming, with 'Back-Up Procedures: 24 ways to get pictures started' (Project Renaissance web pages)
Win Wenger; 1993-2011 (page copyright lines); teacher_manual; rights in_copyright; access fulltext_saved; screen pass; keep_marginal; relevance 3, practicality 5, confidence medium (Read in digest; most of the technique is mind's-eye imagery rather than seen light, so marginal.)
- mode: ambiguous. Wenger's target is imagery in the 'mind's eye' described aloud; he even says to describe it 'as if you were still looking at it' and allows made-up detail to start the flow. But his starters (afterimage, phosphenes, strobe, light flicks, blobs of colour) are seen light, and he claims afterimages sustained by describing lasted long minutes, in one case 4 hours.
- eyes: closed; stimulus independence: unclear
- ONSET induction: Built for people who see nothing: 24 back-up procedures, each tried 10 to 20 minutes; claims no one has gone through them without getting pictures. Entry via helper partner who watches breath pauses and eye movements under lids and asks 'What was in your awareness just then?'; via afterimage; via reporting blobs of colour then looking beyond them as through a screen; via phosphenes from rubbing closed eyes. Time scale: minutes to hours per session. No grace claim; he says the ability is natural.
- techniques: afterimage_retention, light_source_gazing, eye_pressure_or_sense_closure, flicker_or_device, attention_to_spontaneous_phenomena, visualization_crossing, sleep_threshold, sound_or_mantra; phenomena: colour_fields, geometric_patterns, sparks_or_points, figures_or_forms, light_in_the_room
- conditions: gaze_or_eye_position: eyes closed throughout; look beyond colour as beyond a screen; setting: dark room for flicker starters; a listener or tape recorder; duration_and_schedule: 10 to 30 minutes per session; 10 to 20 minutes per starter before switching; prerequisites: a live listener or recorder to describe to; strobe only if not epileptic; other: describe rapidly in sensory detail, present tense, without judging
- stages: afterimage or blobs of colour: described continuously as it changes colour and shape -> other image: a face, eye, landscape or vase appears; switch to describing it
- with the light: describe it aloud continuously and richly; when something else appears switch to that; look beyond the colour
- obstacles/fixes: no pictures: move through the back-up procedures | afterimage fades: recharge on a 20 to 40 watt light and resume | drowsiness: use a live partner | feeling of forcing or making up: allowed at the start
- cautions: do not stare at anything near sun brightness | strobe contraindicated with epilepsy
- safety flags: flicker_seizure_risk
- interpretation: Images are messages from subtler faculties, often symbolic; the afterimage is mainly a bridge, not the goal.
- practice: Close the eyes and describe aloud, to a listener or tape recorder, whatever appears, rapidly and in sensory detail, in present tense. If nothing comes, stare half a minute at a modest light (20 to 40 watts) or a contrasty window, close the eyes and describe the afterimage as it changes, recharging when it fades. Or report any blobs, lines or patterns however trivial, and after some minutes look beyond them as through a screen. Or rub the closed eyes gently and describe the phosphenes. Flick lights on and off with eyes closed, or use a strobe at 4 to 12 beats per second if not epileptic. Sessions run 10 to 30 minutes. Keep the description flowing; new images arrive as you describe, and a partner can spot eye movements under the lids.
  - passage (method; Back-Up Procedures 1, no. 2 After-Image, source lines 159-219): Afterimages normally last seconds but, reinforced by attention and description, can last minutes; he claims one lasted 4 hours.
  - passage (onset; Back-Up Procedures 1, no. 3 Worth Describing, source lines 159-219): If reporting blobs of colour leads nowhere in 10 to 20 minutes, look beyond the colour as through a screen.
  - passage (method; Back-Up Procedures 1, no. 4, source lines 159-219): Gentle eye rubbing yields light-and-colour blips to describe.
  - passage (mode; Helper Technique, source lines 159-219): Primary mode is noticing and describing images; imagery is said to run all the time and the helper technique brings it to awareness.
- new leads: Wenger, A Method for Personal Growth and Development (more prompting procedures)


# STREAM: Online practitioner testimony (practitioner-forums)

## [frm-tulpa-luigi-tulpamancy-guide] Tulpamancy: Guide Into the Strange and Wonderful
Luigi.exe (also u/Eeveecraft), with contributions credited to 'Darkflame'; c. 2019-2020 (undated in text; archived April 2020); teacher_manual; rights unknown; access fulltext_saved; screen pass; keep; relevance 3, practicality 3, confidence medium (Community manual read in digest; claims are untested testimony and the light element is a simple imposed sphere rather than inner light as such.)
- mode: imaginal_crossing. The guide separates visualization (mind's eye, 'a prerequisite') from visual imposition, which it defines as seeing the figure in the physical world without the mind's eye, a controlled and intentional hallucination that overrides sight. The darkness method says the image should come to be seen even with closed eyes. Whether anyone actually obtains this is not documented; the guide calls it the hardest and rarest form.
- eyes: both; stimulus independence: unclear
- ONSET both: Induction ladder for someone who sees nothing: first visualization, then the wall method (plain white wall, dim light, impose the figure flat for several minutes without looking away, over multiple sessions, until a faint outline, shadow or distortion appears), then the darkness method (blacked-out room, eyes open, a glowing sphere of light first, blink sparingly, let it burn in until seen with closed eyes, then add light gradually). No time scale beyond 'multiple sessions'. An experimental third method relies on the tulpa manipulating the sensory centres and is flagged as untested. Nothing here says light cannot be forced.
- techniques: visualization_crossing, gazing_fixed_point, darkness_exposure, afterimage_retention, attention_to_spontaneous_phenomena, sensory_restriction; phenomena: figures_or_forms, sphere_pearl_bindu, light_in_the_room
- conditions: posture: sitting upright in a chair (wall method); comfortable sitting (darkness method); gaze_or_eye_position: eyes open, watching the wall without looking away; eyes wide open in the dark, blinking sparingly; setting: plain white wall in dim light; or a room in complete darkness, windows covered; duration_and_schedule: at least a few minutes per wall session, longer preferred; many sessions; impose occasionally afterwards or progress deteriorates; prerequisites: visualization ability; spatial awareness of the figure; other: experimental sensory-control method requires possession skill
- stages: wall method: A faint outline, shadow or distortion where the figure is imposed, becoming more distinct over sessions; the wall can then be dropped. -> darkness method: Image burned in until seen with closed eyes; light reintroduced gradually until it is seen in normal light.
- with the light: Stabilise and enlarge: affirm the form with further sessions if it fades, add detail, move from a simple glowing sphere to the full figure, and add room light step by step.
- obstacles/fixes: Nothing changes at first: repeat sessions. | Figure fades: continue sessions and impose occasionally. | Cannot be imposed with any light in the room: darken again and re-add light gradually. | Lack of detail or deformities are normal at first.
- cautions: Visual imposition is the most challenging, rare and tedious type. | Guide advises waiting until the tulpa is developed before switching or fusing, and breaks if overwhelmed.
- safety flags: prolonged_darkness
- interpretation: The guide treats the seen figure as a controlled hallucination produced by the brain; it also presents the tulpa as a sentient being, a claim outside what this research needs.
- practice: Start with plain visualization until the figure is clear in the mind. For the wall method, sit upright in dim light before a plain white wall and impose the figure flat on it, watching without looking away for several minutes, over many sessions, until a faint outline, shadow or distortion appears. For the darkness method, sit with eyes wide open in a fully darkened room and impose a glowing sphere of light, blinking sparingly, letting it burn in; the aim is for it to be seen even with the eyes closed. Then reintroduce room light step by step, re-darkening if it vanishes. Keep sessions regular and impose occasionally afterwards.
  - passage (mode; Section 15, Imposition, source lines 1199-1351): Visual imposition is a controlled, intentional hallucination that overrides sight, done without the mind's eye.
  - passage (onset; Section 15, Method One: Wall Method, source lines 1199-1351): After multiple wall sessions the host should begin seeing a faint outline, shadow or distortion where the figure is imposed.
  - passage (method; Section 15, Method Two: Darkness Method, source lines 1199-1351): In a dark room with eyes open, concentrate fully on the imposed object, letting it burn in, blinking sparingly.
  - passage (development; Section 15, Method Two: Darkness Method, source lines 1199-1351): With enough time the image is meant to be seen with closed eyes, like staring into a bright light, and light is then added back gradually.
